Diffi   culty: 17 | Thresholds: 18/35 | HP: 6 | Stress: 5
ATK: +3 | Curved Dagger: Melee | 3d8+4 phy
Experience: Snaky +3

until the Lamia moves or takes Major or greater damage. While 
Constricted, the target is Restrained and must mark a Stress 
when the Lamia takes the spotlight.

Sleep Toxin - Reaction: When the Lamia makes a successful 
standard attack against a PC, you can spend a Fear to make 
them Vulnerable until they succeed on a Instinct Roll (19).

the target Restrained and Vulnerable until they escape with a 
successful Strength Roll (19).

SHAPESHIFTING FIEND
Tier  Social
An evil entity who impersonates a trusted fi  gure.

ATK: +3 | Hidden Blade: Melee | 3d8+3 phy

FEATURES
Fiendish Nature - Passive: The Fiend has resistance to magic 
damage and can’t be fooled by magic illusions.

transform into a humanoid they have previously interacted with. 
A PC who targets the Fiend while they’re in the form of a person 
the PC cares for must succeed on an Instinct Reaction Roll or the 
attack fails and the PC must mark a Stress.
Turn Invisible - Action: Mark a Stress to make the Fiend Hidden 
until they make an action roll or take damage.
Backhanded Guidance - Reaction: When a PC makes an action roll 
while the Fiend is in the scene, you can mark a Stress to add 1d6 
to the result of their Fear Die.
Exposed! (Phase Change) - Reaction: When the Fiend is defeated 
or the PCs discover its true identity, replace the Fiend with 
Shapeshifting Fiend Revealed and immediately spotlight them.

marked.

you gain a Fear.

TIER 1 ENVIRONMENTS (LEVEL 1)
ABANDONED MINE
Tier  Traversal
A twisting warren of disused tunnels carved out of the bedrock by a 
doomed mining outfi  t.
Impulses: Poison the party with deadly gasses, rumble and 
collapse, split the party, turn them around
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Darkweave Spiders (Darkweave 
Crawler, Darkweave Spinner, Darkweave Swarmlings), Miner 
Ghosts (Poltergeist, Will-o’-the-Wisps)
FEATURES
Pitch Black - Passive: There is no light, natural or otherwise, in 
these underground mining tunnels. Ask the PCs what they use 
for light or how they otherwise fi  nd their way in the dark. PCs 
without a reliable source of light make action and reaction rolls 
with disadvantage.
What fuel does the party’s light source use, and how long will it last? 
What unwanted attention does the light attract?
Labyrinth - Passive: The party must complete a Progress 
Countdown (4) to fi  nd their way through the mines. Each time a 
PC rolls with Hope while fi  nding their way through, they discover 
one of the curiosities below.
• An unmined vein of precious metals or gemstones
• Various mining tools, including a broken (but mendable) mine 
cart made of adamantine
• Psychic echoes of the miners’ tragic end
• The desiccated corpses of miners who lost their way in the 
tunnels
• T attered remnants of darkweave spider silk
What did miners previously extract here? Who in the party knows 
how to navigate below ground? What are the PCs most afraid might 
happen to them down here?
Into the Spider’s Web - Action: Spend a Fear to summon a 
Darkweave Queen inside her gossamer-strewn lair. She drops 
from the ceiling within Close range of a PC and immediately 
takes the spotlight.
How does the Darkweave Queen use her lair to her advantage? What 
remnants of her past meals can the party ﬁ  nd here?
Impending Collapse - Reaction: Countdown (2). The fi  rst time 
the PCs roll with Fear, describe how the ground subtly vibrates 
as rocks shift overhead and activate the countdown. It ticks 
down when a PC rolls with Fear. When it triggers, each PC 
must succeed on an Agility Reaction Roll or take 1d8+3 physical 
damage and become temporarily Restrained by the rubble.
What other signs of instability does the mine oﬀ  er the party? What 
new dangers does the tunnel system’s new conﬁ  guration expose the 
party to?
      
ALCHEMIST’S ABANDONED 
WORKSHOP
Tier  Exploration
A lightning-powered laboratory built by a scientist searching for the 
secret to immortality.
Impulses: Break down the barrier between life and death, draw 
down lightning from the heavens, overclock the machinery
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Lab Leftovers (Construct, Minor Fire 
Elemental, Patchwork Zombie Hulk, Swarm of Rats)
FEATURES
Cabinet of Curiosities - Passive: The workshop’s shelves, 
cupboards, and tables are cluttered with macabre bric-a-brac, 
including body parts in jars of formaldehyde and bloody surgical 
implements. Once per scene, a PC can make an Instinct Roll to 
look for something useful in the chaos.
What was the alchemist studying? What smell overwhelms you as 
you shuﬄ   e through their things?
Strange Machinery - Passive: The workshop contains numerous 
pieces of bizarre technology and scientifi  c instrumentation. A 
PC can make a Knowledge Roll to try to fi  gure out how it works. 
On a success, they can get it running. On a failure, they break 
something and take 1d8+4 direct magic damage as they’re 
shocked by electricity and doused in caustic chemicals.
What do these machines do? What do these instruments measure?
It’s Alive! - Action: Consequence Countdown (6). A thunderstorm 
gathers overhead, threatening to jolt the lightning rod assembly 
installed atop the workshop. Activate the countdown and narrate 
how the storm’s power increases each time it ticks down when 
a PC makes an action roll. When it triggers, lightning strikes the 
rod assembly and overloads the workshop machinery, which 
explodes after resurrecting one of the alchemist’s abandoned 
projects. Each target within Close range of the machinery must 
succeed on an Agility Reaction Roll or take 1d10 magic damage. 
Summon a Failed Experiment at a location within the workshop 
and gain a Fear for each PC within Very Close range of it.
What was the alchemist trying to achieve with this experiment? 
Where did they get the materials for it?

CORRUPTED SWAMP
Tier  Traversal
A wretched stretch of slimy water, toxic creatures, and decay.
Impulses: Corrupt, emphasize putrescence, reveal the twisted and 
unnatural, sicken
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Any Undead (Skeleton, Zombie, 
Spectral Guardian), Giant Rat, Green Ooze, T angle Bramble
FEATURES
Swamp Sickness - Passive: At the end of a long rest, each PC 
must succeed on a Strength Reaction Roll or gain a d4 Sickened 
Die, starting with the 1 value facing up. If a PC fails this roll and 
already has a Sickened Die, they increase the value by 1, to a 
maximum value of 4. A PC with a Sickened Die gains a penalty to 
their action rolls equal to its value. When a PC with a Sickened 
Die would clear a HP, they can decrease the value of their 
Sickened Die by 1 instead. When the die’s value would fall to 0, 
remove it.
Is the swamp teeming with life, ﬁ  lled with decay, or both? What force 
blighted this swamp, and what signs of its origins remain?
Soggy and Slogging It - Passive: When a PC moves as part of an 
action, they can only move up to Very Close range. Action rolls 
made to move farther than Very Close range have disadvantage. 
Before rolling, a PC can mark a Stress to remove this 
disadvantage.
What creatures, living or otherwise, lurk beneath the water’s murky 
surface? Where or how do the PCs ﬁ  nd relief from the stinking water?
Rotbloom - Action: Spend a Fear to dust an area within Far range 
in noxious pollen. Each target in that area must succeed on a 
Strength or Instinct Reaction Roll or take 1d6+3 magic damage 
and become Vulnerable until the end of the scene.
How can you tell the plants that emit this pollen are carnivorous? 
What nightmares or dreamscapes does the pollen’s scent conjure?
Dancing Lights - Action: At the start of a rest, you can spend 
a Fear to have fl  oating orbs enthrall the weary and lure a PC 
deeper into the swamp. A diff  erent PC can use a downtime move 
to make an action roll to search for them. On a success, both 
PCs return to camp and can choose downtime moves as normal. 
On a failure—or if no one searches for them—the lost PC 
returns hours later, covered in mud and worse, and can’t choose 
downtime moves until their next rest.
What does the lost PC see in the lights that draws them into the 
darkness? What other glimpses of surprising beauty do the PCs 
encounter here?
CURSED GRA VEY ARD
Tier  Exploration
A sprawling cemetery fi  lled with crumbling headstones, twisted trees 
possessed by the dead, and crypts overgrown with moss and ivy.
Impulses: Bury them six feet deep, choke with fog, lure with 
promises of entombed treasure, unleash the restless dead
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Corrupted Spirits (Deeproot Defender, 
Phantom), Living Dead (Skeleton Warrior, Zombie Pack, 
Patchwork Zombie Hulk) 
FEATURES
Room for One More - Passive: If a PC marks their last HP while in 
the graveyard, they become a Specter instead of making a death 
move. While a Specter, a PC can communicate with the living but 
can’t interact with the physical world, and they must stay within 
Close range of their corpse or cross through the veil of death. 
This condition is cleared when the PC clears a HP.
What new information or experience does a PC gain from becoming 
a specter? How can the party reunite the PC’s spirit with their fl  esh? 
What happens if their ritual goes wrong?
Hallowed Ground - Passive: At the center of the graveyard is 
a seemingly undisturbed chapel. On a successful Instinct 
Roll, a PC can discover this structure is sanctifi  ed against the 
cemetery’s denizens. While inside the chapel, the PCs have 
advantage on action rolls to fi  ght or drive off   spirits and undead.
Who sanctiﬁ  ed this place, and what happened to them? What holy 
relics can be found here?
Misty Miasma - Action: Spend a Fear to fi  ll an area within Far 
range with a low-lying ectoplasmic fog that seeps up through the 
soggy earth. When a PC rolls with Fear while in the miasma, they 
must mark a Stress.
What horrors do the PCs see in the fog? What disorienting smell does 
the miasma emit?
The Dead Arise - Action: Spend a Fear to have undead hands burst 
forth from the ground beneath a PC. The target must succeed 
on an Agility or Presence Reaction Roll or mark a Stress and 
become temporarily Restrained as hands grasp them.
Who were these people in life? Why were they buried here? Why won’t 
they stay dead?
The Dead Don’t Die Easy - Reaction: When an adversary would 
mark their last HP, roll a d6. On a result of 5 or 6, the adversary 
doesn’t mark it.
Who commanded this land to reject death? How do the dead come 
back diﬀ  erent here?

GRAND FEAST
Tier  Social
A community-wide celebration featuring way too much drink, food, 
and fun.
Impulses: Bring everyone together, celebrate a momentous 
occasion, encourage revelrous abandon
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Apprentice Assassin, Guard, Merchant, 
Petty Noble, Sellsword, Spy
FEATURES
Loose Lips & Fast Friends - Passive: Everyone is here to have a 
good time. PCs have advantage on Presence Rolls to socialize 
with other revelers.
What is everyone celebrating? Where’s the party? Who’s footing the 
bill?
The Evening’s Entertainment - Action: The host of the feast kicks 
off   the entertainment portion of the evening. Each PC can make 
an action roll to initiate a performance, competition, or other 
lighthearted diversion. A PC who succeeds becomes an Honored 
Guest. Until the feast ends, an Honored Guest can claim a gift 
from the host, then clear the condition.
What does the host want to see or hear more than anything else? 
What can they oﬀ  er the PCs in return?
“There’s Always That One…” - Action: Spend a Fear to Trap a PC in 
conversation with the most unpleasant person at the party until 
the PC succeeds on an action roll to extricate themself from this 
social black hole. While Trapped, a PC can’t make action rolls 
except to try to clear this condition. When a Trapped PC fails an 
action roll, they must mark a Stress.
What makes this dud so unpleasant? What’s something the PC cares 
about that the dud constantly diminishes or dismisses? What boring 
or awful thing can the dud not shut up about?
Last Call - Reaction: After a long night, the partygoers start 
fi  ltering out. When the feast concludes, all PCs who participated 
in the festivities clear all Stress and gain a Hope. Additionally, 
each Honored Guest clears all HP and gains an additional Hope, 
then clears the condition. Each PC who overindulged must 
succeed on a Strength Reaction Roll or become Hungover until 
their next rest. While Hungover, a PC gains a −1 penalty to action 
rolls.
What crazy stories do the PCs share with one another as they leave 
the party? Who do the PCs see unexpectedly canoodling afterward?
HOLD THE LINE
Tier  Event
The party must fend off   multiple waves of enemies long enough for 
an ally to complete an important objective.
Impulses: Crash waves of enemies upon the party’s shores, never 
let them breathe, target their charge
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Guards, Jagged Knives, Pirates, 
Skeletons, Zombies
FEATURES
Bodyguards - Passive: The PCs must protect an NPC (their 
“Charge”) long enough for the Charge to complete a task, such 
as fi  nishing a magical ritual or repairing a mechanical device. At 
the start of the scene, activate a T ask Countdown with a value 
equal to the number of PCs in the scene. It ticks down when the 
PCs defeat a wave of adversaries. When it triggers, the Charge 
completes their task.
Who are the PCs protecting, and what is the Charge trying to do? 
Who wants to stop them? What will happen if the Charge isn’t able to 
complete their task?
“I Must Not Fail!” - Passive: When the fi  rst wave of adversaries 
appears, place a Resolve Countdown next to the Charge with 
a value equal to the number of PCs in the scene. When an 
adversary makes an attack against the Charge, the attack 
automatically succeeds and ticks down the Resolve Countdown. 
When it triggers, the Charge either fl  ees, dies, or becomes 
incapacitated.
Why is this task so important to the Charge? What has the power to 
break their resolve?
Swarming Multitudes - Passive: All adversaries gain the 
“Relentless (2)” feature: They can be spotlighted up to two times 
per GM turn. Spend Fear as usual to spotlight them.
Where does these foes’ newfound power come from? Is there any way 
to cut them oﬀ   from it?
Waves upon Waves - Reaction: When there are no adversaries in 
the scene, summon a wave of adversaries at Far range from the 
Charge. Each wave comprises two Tier 1 Minions for each PC in 
the scene.
Where do these adversaries come from? Who leads them from the 
shadows?

LOCAL FESTIVAL
LOCAL FESTIV AL
Tier  Social
Commoners and nobles alike gather from the surrounding area for a 
weeklong celebration.
Impulses: Express local culture and history, forge new 
relationships, reinforce community
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Festival Security (Bladed Guard, Head 
Guard), Local Luminaries (Commoner, Merchant, Petty Noble), 
Sundry Ne’er-Do-Wells (Jagged Knife Bandit, Lackey)
FEATURES
Remember, Remember - Passive: This time of year marks an 
important anniversary or astronomical event, such as the 
founding of the town or the summer solstice. On a successful 
Knowledge Roll, a PC can answer one of the questions below 
(or two, if they critically succeed). On a failure, they answer one 
question, but you decide which part of their answer is inaccurate 
and gain a Fear.
• The festival celebrates a crop or export that is critical to the 
economy here. What is it?
• Why didn’t the festival happen last year?
• This festival is similar to another one held in a neighboring 
region. What makes them diff  erent?
What are the locals celebrating? What is the festival famous for? 
How did the PCs ﬁ  rst hear about it?
Political Machinations - Passive: While everyone is gathered in 
one place, the locals take the opportunity to quash feuds, drum 
up new business, and navigate local politics. A PC can stick their 
nose in the goings-on by making a Presence Roll. On a success, 
they make a connection to a local luminary who can give them a 
job, share important information, or make useful introductions. 
On a failure, they must mark a Stress.
What new crisis is on everyone’s lips? Why can’t this community 
solve the problem on its own? What reward can they oﬀ  er the PCs in 
exchange for their aid?
“Let the Games Begin!” - Action: One of the festival’s signature 
events is a sporting competition or series of games. If one 
or more PCs throw their hat into the ring, introduce rival 
competitors for them to face off   against. T o claim victory, the 
PCs must complete a Progress Countdown (5) to win events 
or score points before their rivals complete a Consequence 
Countdown (5).
What skills or abilities does this competition test? What does the 
victor win? Why is failure not an option for the PCs or their rivals?
Legendary Local - Action: The festival culminates in the arrival of 
a fi  gure from local folklore who performs special duties for the 
local community, such as giving treats to children, heckling the 
gentry, or blessing next year’s crops. If the PCs show this fi  gure 
the proper deference and respect, they gain an appropriate 
blessing, such as a cryptic prophecy or a magical trinket, and 
gain 1d4 Hope to distribute among the party. If they insult the 
fi  gure, they take 1d6+2 magic damage as the fi  gure curses them, 
and you gain a Fear.
Is this a singular entity or a mantle passed from person to person? 
What distinctive form do they possess? What is the source of their 
power to grant boons and banes?
RAIDING PARTY
Tier  Event
A pirate ship disgorges its crew into a port town or shoreline village 
to loot and plunder.
Impulses: Light it on fi  re, overwhelm with speed and numbers, 
take everything that isn’t nailed down
Diffi   culty: 11
Potential Adversaries: Ship’s Crew (Pirate Captain, Pirate 
Raiders, Pirate T ough)
FEATURES
Rally the Locals - Passive: Progress Countdown (5). When the 
pirates fi  rst appear, activate the countdown. It ticks down 
when a PC succeeds on an action roll to stem the chaos and 
destruction of the pirate raid. When it triggers, the locals 
become inspired by the party’s actions and join the fi  ght against 
the pirates. Each PC gains a d6 Morale Die, which they can 
spend to add to an action, reaction, or damage roll.
How do the pirates threaten or endanger the town besides attacking 
its inhabitants? What defenses can the PCs take advantage of?
Parley - Passive: On a successful Knowledge Roll, a PC knows 
enough pirate law to demand a parley with the Pirate Captain. 
Once the parley is issued, the captain pauses the combat to 
allow a PC to sue for peace or negotiate the terms of surrender 
with a Presence Roll.
Why do these pirates respect this captain enough to follow them? 
What do they care about more than ill-gotten booty?
“Fire the Cannons!” - Action: Spend up to 3 Fear to have the 
pirate ship fi  re its cannons into the combat. For each Fear spent, 
choose a point anywhere on the battlefi  eld. Each PC within 
Close range of one or more of those points must make an Agility 
Reaction Roll. T argets who fail take 1d8+3 physical damage. 
T argets who succeed must mark a Stress or take half damage.
What collateral damage do these cannonballs cause? Whose lives do 
they wantonly endanger?
Take Captives - Reaction: When a number of pirates equal to the 
number of PCs in the scene are defeated, roll a d6. On a result of 
5 or 6, the pirates change tactics, capturing locals and retreating 
to the ship so they can ransom the captives later. Activate a 
Consequence Countdown (4). It ticks down when a PC makes 
an action roll. When it triggers, the pirates sail away with their 
victims.
Who do the pirates deem the best captives, and why? What do they 
want in exchange for the captives’ return?

HEIST
DUEL
Tier  Event
An adversary challenges one of the PCs to single combat as the rest 
of the party aids them from the sidelines.
Impulses: Focus on one PC, goad them into overcommitting, 
infl  ict an embarrassing defeat, sideline the rest of the party
Diffi   culty: Special (see “Relative Strength”)
Potential Adversaries: Any
FEATURES
“I Demand Satisfaction.” - Passive: When an adversary challenges 
a PC to one-on-one combat, the PC chooses whether to accept. 
If they reject the challenge, they must mark a Stress, and you 
gain a Fear. If a PC accepts an adversary’s challenge, activate 
a Progress Countdown (6) and a Consequence Countdown (4). 
If the Progress Countdown triggers fi  rst, the PC wins the Duel 
and gains a favor or concession from their adversary. If the 
Consequence Countdown triggers fi  rst, the adversary wins the 
Duel and claims a favor or concession from the PC.
Why was this PC chosen? What do they expect to win if they prove 
victorious?
Relative Strength - Passive: The Diffi   culty of this environment 
equals that of the adversary who issued the challenge.
Who issues the challenge? What are they ﬁ  ghting for?
Rules of Engagement - Passive: During the Duel, the challenged 
PC is the only one who can make action rolls. Other PCs can 
only aid the challenged PC indirectly: They can Help an Ally, 
initiate group action rolls or T ag T eam Rolls, and use any of their 
features that don’t directly aff  ect the challenging adversary.
Where does this confl  ict take place? How does the environment 
oﬀ  er the rest of the party an opportunity to aid their ally without 
interfering?
Dirty Tricks - Action: Spend a Fear to have the challenging 
adversary or one of their allies cheat. A PC can notice their 
underhanded action with a successful Instinct Reaction Roll and 
respond in kind.
What’s oﬀ  -limits in this confl  ict? How does the adversary’s ally bend 
the rules without breaking them, or break the rules without getting 
caught? How can the PCs rebalance the scales?
      
HEIST
Tier  Event
The party must gain access to a hidden or well-protected location to 
acquire the information or items secured within.
Impulses: Make the party prepare, split them up, throw up 
obstacles, trap them inside, use up their resources
Diffi   culty: 14
Potential Adversaries: Head Guard, Minotaur Wrecker, Secret-
Keeper, Spectral Guardian
FEATURES
Prep Work - Passive: Prior to the Heist, each PC has the 
opportunity to gather a key piece of information, surveil 
the location, or acquire a helpful tool or resource. Give each 
player the opportunity to describe one obstacle they expect 
to encounter and how they set themselves up to overcome it, 
then have them make an action roll. On a success, they get the 
information or asset they sought. On a success with Fear, it’s 
less useful than they expected (for example, they discover the 
location of a hidden access point but learn that breaching it will 
likely be diffi   cult and noisy).
How does the PC gain this advantage? Do they go it alone or ask for 
help? What unforeseen complications arise during the heist itself?
Infi  ltration - Passive: Establish the goal of the Heist and start 
two dynamic countdowns: a Progress Countdown (12) to 
represent the party’s work toward their goal and a Consequence 
Countdown (10) to represent the rising risk of discovery and 
capture. Advance both according to the Dynamic Countdown 
Advancement table as the PCs make action rolls to complete 
the Heist. If the Progress Countdown triggers fi  rst, spotlight 
this environment to use the “Exfi  ltration” feature. If the 
Consequence Countdown triggers fi  rst, gain 1d4 Fear, spotlight 
this environment, and use the “Sound the Alarm” feature.
What is the party going after, and where is it located? What 
unexpected obstacles will they encounter? What happens if the party 
gets caught?
Saw This Coming - Passive: Once per session, a PC can spend 
a Hope to explain how they prepared for the current situation 
and reveal a key piece of information (such as the location of a 
hidden passage or the password to a restricted area) or produce 
a helpful item (such as a cultist’s uniform left unattended or a 
common consumable hidden in their boot).
What did the PC prepare in advance? How did they know they’d need 
it? What did it cost them to set up, or whom do they owe a favor to?
Exfi  ltration - Reaction: When the Progress Countdown triggers, 
the party acquires the object of the Heist and must succeed on 
a group action roll to escape without capture. On a failure, each 
PC takes 2d12 direct physical damage and must mark a Stress.
What makes getting out even more dangerous than getting in? What 
unforeseen developments stymie the party’s original escape plan?
Sound the Alarm - Reaction: When the Consequence Countdown 
triggers, the party is discovered and the location enters High 
Alert. Summon a number of Tier 2 adversaries equal to the 
number of PCs in the scene and immediately spotlight one. 
While this environment is on High Alert, all adversaries gain a +1 
bonus to their Diffi   culty.
What makes these adversaries uniquely suited to guard this location? 
What do they know about this place that gives them an edge?

MASQUERADE BALL
MASQUERADE BALL
Tier  Social
A high society gala where masked revelers trade secrets and favors.
Impulses: Dance the night away, eat and be merry, make secret 
deals, trade valuable information
Diffi   culty: 14
Potential Adversaries: Operatives (Masked Thief, Master 
Assassin, Spy), Revelers (Courtesan, Merchant Baron, Royal 
Advisor)
FEATURES
Costumes Required - Passive: Everyone at the ball is expected 
to arrive in costume. PCs have advantage on Presence Rolls to 
convince others they are someone—or something—they’re not.
What do the PCs come dressed as? What do their costumes reveal 
about their true selves?
“Care to Dance?” - Passive: While dancing, a PC can make an 
Agility Roll instead of a Presence Roll to infl  uence others.
What’s notable or unique about the dances that are in fashion here? 
Where did the PCs learn how to perform them?
Mistaken Identity - Action: A very important person confuses a 
PC with an even more important person. The target can play 
into the misunderstanding with a Presence Reaction Roll. On a 
success, the PC can ask the VIP to perform a favor, relay a key 
piece of information, or make an introduction. On a failure, the 
PC marks a Stress as their true identity is discovered, and they 
are threatened with ejection from the party.
Who does the VIP mistake them for? What doors does their mistake 
open? What dangers does it bring?
Cloak and Dagger - Action: Spend a Fear to have an operative try 
to steal something valuable or assassinate someone important. 
A PC can make an Instinct Roll to try to notice them before 
they achieve their objective. On a success, the PC gains the 
opportunity to intervene. On a failure with Fear, the PC is 
erroneously implicated in the crime.
Who does the operative work for? Why did they send them to the 
masquerade?
      
OCEAN VOY AGE
Tier  Traversal
The PCs gain passage to a far-off   destination by off  ering their labor 
in exchange.
Impulses: Reveal what lurks beneath the waves, send them in the 
wrong direction, sink the ship, use up their resources
Diffi   culty: 14
Potential Adversaries: Deep Sea Denizens (Electric Eels, 
Kraken, Shark, Siren), Pirates (Pirate Captain, Pirate Raiders)
FEATURES
Open Water - Passive: Countdown (8). Reaching their destination 
requires the party to complete the Open Water Countdown. It 
ticks down when a PC succeeds on an action roll to aid the ship’s 
journey.
Where is the ship headed? What dangers along the route do the crew 
speak about in hushed tones?
Rations Run Low - Passive: The fi  rst time a PC rolls a failure with 
Fear on this journey, the crew discovers that the food and fresh 
water in the ship’s hold have been befouled by pests and mold. 
Until the party reaches its destination, a PC who makes an 
action roll to advance the Open Water Countdown must succeed 
on a Strength Reaction Roll or mark a Stress fi  rst.
What happened to the rations? What drastic measures does the crew 
take to ensure they don’t all die of hunger and thirst?
“Pirates!” - Action: Spend a Fear to have a crewmember in the 
crow’s nest spy black sails on the horizon! Start a Consequence 
Countdown (4). If it triggers before the party reaches their 
destination, the pirates catch up to the ship and board it. Gain 
a Fear, then summon one Pirate Captain and three Hordes of 
Pirate Raiders within Close range of the party.
Does the captain recognize the ship that’s giving chase? What 
ungentlemanly practices are the pirates in this area famous for?
Foul Weather - Reaction: When the Open Water Countdown ticks 
down to an odd number, roll a d6. On a result of 4 or higher, the 
ship runs into a patch of foul weather, such as a fog bank that 
makes navigation diffi   cult or a squall that threatens to capsize 
the ship. Each PC must succeed on a reaction roll using a 
relevant trait chosen by the GM or mark 1d4 Stress.
What makes this weather diﬀ  erent from anything the PCs have 
experienced before? How do they coordinate with the crew to ﬁ  nd 
their way through this hazard?
Here There Be Monsters - Reaction: When the Open Water 
Countdown ticks down to an even number, roll a d6. On a result 
of 4 or higher, summon 1d4 Deep Sea Denizens.
What attracts these creatures to the ship? Is there any way to drive 
them oﬀ   without combat?

CRYSTAL WASTELAND
CRYSTAL WASTELAND
Tier  Traversal
A harsh and alien landscape that shines with towers of glittering 
crystal.
Impulses: Cut them to their core, fracture their psyches, show 
their true refl  ection
Diffi   culty: 16
Potential Adversaries: Adult Flickerfl  y, Constructs, Elemental 
Spark, Vault Guardians, Whisper Wraiths
FEATURES
Harmonic Resonance - Passive: The crystals here reverberate 
with magical energy. A PC can use a downtime move to try to 
Attune to the environment with a successful Spellcast Roll. While 
Attuned, the PC has advantage on Spellcast Rolls inside the 
Crystal Wasteland. The condition is cleared when the PC leaves 
the Crystal Wasteland.
How does attunement to this place alter your sensory experience of 
it? What sound or song from your childhood does this place conjure?
Gravitational Anomalies - Action: Strange, localized phenomena 
dot the landscape, warping distance and direction. Spend a 
Fear to alter the gravity at a chosen point. Each target within 
Close range of that point must make a Knowledge Reaction Roll 
to calculate their new trajectories. T argets who fail take 3d8+4 
physical damage as they are thrown up and away. T argets who 
succeed must mark a Stress or take half damage.
What does it feel like as your body and mind ﬁ  ght to reestablish 
equilibrium? Is there any way to use this to your advantage?
Arcane Refl  ection - Action: Spend a Fear to have any number of 
PCs glimpse a refl  ection in the crystal of who they once were 
or might become. Each target must make an Instinct Reaction 
Roll. T argets who succeed see a vision of their better self and can 
replace one Experience. T argets who fail see a vision of their dark 
past—or an even darker future—and must mark a Stress. You 
gain a Fear for each target who failed.
Who do the PCs see on the other side of the crystal? How does it make 
them feel?
Razor-Sharp Terrain - Reaction: When a PC fails a roll to navigate 
the wasteland, they must make a Finesse Reaction Roll. On a 
failure, they cut themselves on the sharp edges of the terrain. 
Shards of magic crystal become embedded in their wound and 
slowly grow, Crystallizing them. Activate a Crystal Countdown 
(1) for that PC. Until they remove the condition with a successful 
Knowledge Roll, a Crystallizing PC ticks up their Crystal 
Countdown at the start of each rest and gains a penalty to 
their Evasion equal to its value. The countdown ends when the 
condition is cleared.
What does the crystal look like as it grows? What does it feel like?
Psychic Shuﬄ   e - Reaction: When the PCs take a long rest, you 
can spend a Fear to swap their psyches. Collect all the players’ 
character sheets, then shuffl   e and randomly redistribute them. 
Each character sheet comes with that PC’s Domain cards and 
player aids. A PC can regain their psyche with a successful 
Knowledge Roll, at which point they trade sheets with the player 
who has their original character sheet.
How does it feel being in someone else’s skin? If you could choose to 
stay like them in one way, what would it be?

Underwater Environment - Passive: This environment is

their countdown an additional time. If the roll is a failure with 
Fear, you can tick it down twice instead. Once their countdown 
triggers, a PC must mark a Stress whenever they take an action 
underwater until they resurface.
Have you ever known anyone who drowned? What do you think about 
to keep calm and stretch your air supply?
Air Pockets - Passive: A PC can reset their their Air Supply 
Countdown to its starting value by locating a pocket of air with a 
successful Knowledge or Instinct Roll.
What signs or structures do you look for? What does it feel like to 
know this might be the last breath you take?
Lost Technology - Passive: The ruins are carved with strange 
magical runes and littered with lost technology. A PC can make 
a Knowledge Roll to learn useful information about this lost 
civilization from any runes or devices they come across.
Who once called this place home? How did their technology work? 
Why were they ultimately destroyed and cast down into the depths?
Deepwater Denizens - Action: Spend a Fear to summon up to two 
Deep Dwellers and 1d6 Cursed Merfolk who try to bind the PCs 
to their will. These adversaries appear at Close range of a PC, 
then one immediately takes the spotlight.
Why do these creatures stay here among the ruins? Are they unwilling 
or unable to resurrect this once-prosperous civilization?

VOLCANIC ERUPTION
UPSCALE CASINO
Tier  Social
A glittering gambling hall where well-heeled merchants and nobles 
come to fritter away their fortunes.
Impulses: Dangle the jackpot just out of reach, dazzle them with 
spectacle, prey on their vices
Diffi   culty: 16
Potential Adversaries: Automated Security (Vault Guardians), 
Gamblers (Courtesan, Merchant Baron, Petty Noble, Spy)
FEATURES
Glitz & Glamor - Passive: The overwhelming spectacle of this place 
can entrance even the most jaded patron. While here, all PCs 
have disadvantage on reaction rolls caused by a staff   member or 
regular patron of the casino.
What’s so wondrous about the casino’s trappings? Are they truly 
magical in nature or merely mundane wonders taken to an impossible 
extreme?
Fortune Favors the Bold - Passive: Once per long rest, a PC can 
gamble any amount of gold by stating how much they want to 
wager and rolling their Hope Die. On a result of 7 or higher, the 
PC wins an equal amount, doubling their money. On a result of 6 
or lower, they lose it all.
What game does the PC choose to play? What house rules does this 
casino have? Is there anything besides gold the PCs are willing to risk?
The House Always Wins - Reaction: When an adversary fails a roll, 
you can spend a Fear to make it a success instead.
Is this place blessed with impossible fortune, or does something curse 
anyone who steps through its doors?
Automated Security - Reaction: When a PC attempts to cheat at 
a game in the casino and fails, they must mark a Stress as they 
activate the casino’s mechanical security detail. Gain a Fear 
and summon one Vault Guardian Gaoler and Turret for each PC, 
plus a Vault Guardian Sentinel. All adversaries appear within Far 
range of the PCs, and the Vault Guardian Sentinel immediately 
takes the spotlight.
Who built these machines? Why are they preferable to living beings?
The Devil’s Playground - Reaction: If the PCs defeat the casino’s 
automated security or try to leave with stolen winnings, you 
can spend an amount of Fear equal to the number of PCs to 
summon the casino’s true owner, a Demon of Avarice, within 
Close range of a PC. The Demon is immediately spotlighted and 
bars the party’s exit at all costs.
How did the demon mask their infl  uence until now? How does the 
casino itself change to reveal its true nature?
      
VOLCANIC ERUPTION
Tier  Event
An explosion of heat and molten rock that leaves behind only charred 
desolation.
Impulses: Create panic all around, cut off   the exits, isolate, make 
them scramble for safety, split the party
Diffi   culty: 16
Potential Adversaries: Elemental Spark, Lava Elemental 
(Greater Earth Elemental), Volcanic Dragon
FEATURES
Unstable Ground - Passive: The ground here is uneven, prone to 
collapse, and constantly shifting. PCs who fail an Agility Roll to 
move must mark a HP and are temporarily Restrained.
What strange shapes and colors do the PCs see in the cooled lava? 
How do the people who live nearby keep their footing on the slick, 
shiny surface?
Violent Eruption - Action: Spend a Fear to have the ground erupt 
at a point of your choice, spewing lava into the air and spreading 
it along the ground. All targets within Very Close range of that 
point lose a Hope and take 3d8+3 physical damage. The area 
within Very Close range of the eruption point becomes lava until 
the end of the scene. A creature who moves into or through the 
lava takes 3d8+3 physical damage. Until the end of the scene, 
you can spend a Fear to expand the lava area by one step (such 
as Very Close to Close or Close to Far).
What makes the lava seem alive? How can the PCs predict its 
behavior?
Vent Toxic Gas - Action: Spend a Fear to open a fi  ssure that 
belches out toxic gas at a point of your choice, creating a cloud 
that blankets everything within Far range. While inside the cloud, 
a PC must track their air supply with an Air Supply Countdown. 
Its starting value is equal to 3 + their tier, and it ticks down 
when they take an action. If a PC fails an action roll or rolls with 
Fear, you can use a GM move to tick down their countdown an 
additional time. If the roll is a failure with Fear, you can tick it 
down twice instead. Once the countdown ends, a PC must mark 
a Stress whenever they take an action within the gas until they 
escape the cloud.
What signs do you see to indicate the presence of toxic gas? What 
taste lingers in the mouths of the PCs who inhale the gas?

TIER 4 ENVIRONMENTS (LEVELS 8–10)
CONVERGENCE, THE 
CITY OF PORTALS
Tier  Social
A sprawling metropolis at the nexus of creation, where infl  uential 
fi  gures from across the realms gather.
Impulses: Confront them with alien customs, dazzle them with 
infi  nite variety, tempt them with the impossible
Diffi   culty: 20
Potential Adversaries: Power Players (Arch-Necromancer, 
Demon Lord Berzug, Fire Titan Warlord, High Seraph, Monarch, 
Oracle of Doom, Vampire Lord), Travelers and Residents (Any)
FEATURES
Impossibilities Abound - Passive: Convergence is a nexus of gods, 
monsters, aliens, and all manner of dimension-hoppers. Because 
mortal life is generally beneath these beings’ concern, PCs have 
disadvantage on action rolls to infl  uence them unless they have 
leverage against them or know what the NPC cares about.
What manner of being currently holds sway in the city? What strange 
customs do they practice? Which PC is most confused or oﬀ  ended by 
them, and which PC adopts these customs as their own?
Into the Multiverse - Passive: The city of Convergence has a portal 
or door to every realm of existence.
Where (or when) does the party want to go? Where is that portal 
located, and who controls that area?
Infi  nite Inventory - Passive: This city has the solution to any 
problem, if you can fi  nd it. A PC seeking a particular item with 
wondrous properties can make an Instinct or Presence Roll to 
fi  nd it in the city’s pandimensional marketplace.
• Critical Success: The PC fi  nds the perfect item, and the 
merchant who has it only wants something the PC considers 
inconsequential in exchange.
• Success with Hope: The PC fi  nds the perfect item and can 
exchange something of equal value for it.
• Success with Fear: The PC fi  nds the perfect item, but the 
merchant who has it will only exchange it for something the 
party can’t give them... yet.
• Any Failure: The PC must mark a Stress as they go on a wild 
goose chase but come up empty-handed. The PC can mark 
an additional Stress to change this result into a success with 
Fear.
Who has the item the party needs? What do they want in return?
Don’t Make Waves - Passive: Countdown (6). Because anything 
is possible in Convergence, the city maintains a labyrinthine 
bureaucracy that issues and enforces ordinances regarding 
all exchanges and interactions between the realms. When the 
party arrives in Convergence, activate the countdown. It ticks 
down when a PC does something that upsets the city’s delicate 
political equilibrium. When it triggers, the PCs become Wanted 
until they clear their names with the authorities. When a Wanted 
PC rolls with Fear, you can summon 1d4 T emporal Enforcers 
who appear within Very Close range, immediately take the 
spotlight, and try to apprehend the party.
What has the party done (or what do they plan to do) that draws the 
attention and ire of the city oﬃ   cials? How can they ﬁ  x the problem 
they caused?
An Oﬀ  er You Can’t Refuse - Action: Convergence is home to a 
tenuous network of rival factions. Spend a Fear to have one 
of them present the party with an easy solution to a diffi   cult 
problem in exchange for their allegiance. If the party refuses, 
each PC must mark a Stress, and they can’t turn to that faction 
for help in the future.
Which faction oﬀ  ers the party their support? Who do they want the 
party to act against?
      
MOON KINGDOM
Tier  Exploration
The mysterious, luminous, and isolated ruins of a long-lost lunar 
kingdom that echoes with forgotten magic and ancient technology.
Impulses: Blur the line between dream and reality, let 
consciousness ebb and fl  ow like the tide, whisper echoes of 
ancient opulence
Diffi   culty: 20
Potential Adversaries: Local Fauna (Cryptimoths, Yufos), 
Moon Queen’s Spirit (Arch-Necromancer), Reawakened Relics 
(Constructs, Vault Guardians)
FEATURES
Dreamlike Atmosphere - Passive: Everything in the Moon Kingdom 
feels dreamlike and fl  oaty. A leap carries you far, and the sound 
is muted. The air is full of an iridescent crystalline dust that 
shifts in response to thoughts or emotions and might record 
memories of the past or visions of the future. Once per scene, a 
PC can make an Instinct Roll to read the psychic impressions left 
by past events.
What ethereal scents or sensations do the PCs experience? How does 
the atmosphere aﬀ  ect the interactions between them?
Silent Ruins - Passive: The towering silver and blue architecture 
of the capital is undamaged but overgrown with bioluminescent 
fl  ora that pulses in time with the lunar tides. A PC who 
succceeds on a Finesse Roll can harvest a moon plant without 
killing it. A PC who eats one or more living moon plants gains a 
+1 bonus to Spellcast Rolls until their next long rest.
What earth fl  ora do these moon plants look like? What do they taste 
like, and how do they aﬀ  ect the eater physically?
Lunar Astrologium - Passive: Atop the highest tower in the capital 
city sits a crystal dream machine that can replay history. A PC 
who succeeds on a Knowledge Roll can repair the machine. Once 
the machine is repaired, a PC who succeeds on a Presence Roll 
can operate it to learn the history of the Moon Kingdom’s rise 
and fall.
• Critical Success: The PC receives clear information about key 
events and fi  gures in the Moon Kingdom’s history and gains 
1d4 Hope, which they can distribute among PCs they share 
their visions with.
• Success with Hope: The PC receives clear information about 
key events and fi  gures in the Moon Kingdom’s history.
• Any roll with Fear: The PC receives brief fl  ashes and 
sensations related to the kingdom’s cataclysmic end. If the 
roll was a failure, the PC takes 4d10+5 magic damage as the 
visions assault their psyche.
What does this machinery look like, and how does it work? What do 
the PCs learn about the Moon Kingdom’s history?
The Moon Queen’s Tomb - Reaction: The Moon Kingdom’s fi  nal 
ruler has been preserved in a selenite coffi   n deep within the 
citadel. On a roll with Fear to investigate her chamber, her 
baleful shade awakens and attacks the party. Summon the 
Moon Queen’s Spirit (Arch-Necromancer) next to her coffi   n and 
immediately spotlight her.
Who buried her here, and why? Can she be resurrected, or is 
destroying her spirit the only path forward?

CHAPTER FOUR: 
CAMPAIGN FRAMES
When starting a Daggerheart campaign, you might wish to begin with a campaign frame.  
Four campaign frames are presented here in order of complexity.
CASTLE HIGH
In a modern fantasy world, teenagers attend a boarding school 
in the clouds—sneaking out of class to fly brooms, explore 
floating islands, and save their friends.
Designed by Rowan Hall, Mikey Hamm, Cass Rea, and Spenser Starke
Complexity Rating: •
The Pitch:
In a modern world of knights and cars, wands and flip phones, 
there’s no better place to study magic than the floating school 
known as Castle High. Drifting among the mysterious ruins of 
the ancient world and modern settlements that populate the 
clouds, this school and its surroundings hold secrets, danger, 
and something more sinister. A string of disappearances has 
recently plagued the school, and no one knows who or what is 
behind it—or who will disappear next.
In a Castle High campaign, you’ll play adventurous high 
schoolers who take magic classes, explore sky islands, and 
search for their missing classmates.
This campaign frame starts on page 116.
REIGN OF THE WEREDRAGON
In a land ruled by a violent weredragon tyrant, heroes ordained 
by the stars to defeat her must rise to the challenge and defend 
their people.
Designed by Jeremy Crawford, Rowan Hall, Christopher Perkins, and 
Spenser Starke
Complexity Rating: ••
The Pitch:
Once upon a time, in the peaceful land of Whelvia, a powerful 
sorceress named Forlorne Lykona deceived the country’s great 
dragon warden to absorb his power and rule the kingdom 
herself. Transformed into a weredragon by the powerful ritual 
that fused the warden’s power with her cold heart, Forlorne 
now takes to the skies, feasting on all who challenge her and 
cursing innocent villagers to become ravenous weredrakes. 
The people called on the brave defenders of the realm, the 
Knights of Whelvia, to protect them—but the knights instead 
bent the knee to the draconic tyrant, allowing her to command 
their forces and leaving the land without protectors.
In a Reign of the Weredragon campaign, you play destined 
heroes of Whelvia, rising up against Forlorne Lykona and her 
knights to save this once-idyllic land from the jaws of tyranny.
This campaign frame starts on page 128.
DARK HEART OF ANDALURIA
As the nights grow long in a land of rich wine and ancient 
superstitions, a band of monster hunters battles the creatures 
that stalk the shadows.
Designed by Carlos Cisco, Rue Dickey, Rowan Hall, and Rob Hebert
Complexity Rating: •••
The Pitch:
Andaluria is a land of sweeping vineyards where beauty and 
dread intertwine. By day, the streets and valleys are alive 
with festivals, music, and the scent of wine—but when the 
sun sets, monsters come out to feast. For generations, the 
old superstitions and folk rites kept the beasts at bay, but 
the nights have grown unnaturally long, and the citizens of 
Andaluria live in fear of increasingly terrifying monsters.
In a Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign, you’ll play monster 
hunters who must investigate horrific crimes, navigate 
political strife, and slay supernatural creatures to protect the 
people from eternal darkness.
This campaign frame starts on page 144.
JOURNEY TO HORIZON
After a thousand-year cataclysm, a fellowship of heroes 
embarks from their magically protected sanctuary to journey 
across a broken continent and resurrect the fallen goddess who 
can heal their world.
Designed by James Haeck, Rob Hebert, James Introcaso, and  
Spenser Starke
Complexity Rating: ••••
The Pitch:
Long ago, the vast continent of Valloren was home to 
wondrous landscapes shaped by the goddess Aurelia, wielder 
of the elemental essence called terrana. But when her 
disciples’ betrayal threatened to extinguish magic forever, 
Aurelia sacrificed her own life to call down the stars and purge 
the land of corruption. T o protect her people from annihilation, 
Aurelia raised magical barriers that would last a thousand 
years.
In a Journey to Horizon campaign, you play heroes chosen 
by fate to leave their recently unsealed sanctuary and travel 
across a turbulent and hostile world on a quest to resurrect 
the goddess.
This campaign frame starts on page 158.

CAsT l E  Hig H
CAsT l E H ig H
In a modern fantasy world, teenagers attend a boarding school in the clouds—sneaking out of 
class to fly brooms, explore floating islands, and save their friends.
Designed by Rowan Hall, Mikey Hamm, Cass Rea, and Spenser Starke
Complexity Rating: •
THE PITCH
Read this section to your players to introduce them to the 
campaign.
In a modern world of knights and cars, wands and flip phones, 
there’s no better place to study magic than the floating school 
known as Castle High. Drifting among the mysterious ruins of 
the ancient world and modern settlements that populate the 
clouds, this school and its surroundings hold secrets, danger, 
and something more sinister. A string of disappearances has 
recently plagued the school, and no one knows who or what is 
behind it—or who will disappear next.
In a Castle High campaign, you’ll play adventurous high 
schoolers who take magic classes, explore sky islands, and 
search for their missing classmates.
TONE & FEEL
Adventurous, Dreamy, Full-Hearted, Melodramatic, 
Modern, Playful, Thrilling
THEMES
Coming of Age, Curiosity & Rebellion, Friendship & Loyalty, 
Magical vs. Modern
TOUCHSTONES
Kids on Brooms, Kiki’s Delivery Service, The Legend of Zelda: 
Skyward Sword, Little Witch Academia, Onward, The Owl 
House, She-Ra and the Princesses of Power, Slugblaster
116 Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 118 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
OVERVIEW
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players the following information before character creation.
Modern civilization has come a long way. Industry. Innovation. 
Plastic. The elders have seen horses give way to cars, science 
catch up with magic, sending spells all but abandoned for 
cell phones, and the gods replaced by pop stars. Much has 
been lost, but more has been gained, and most people live 
long, comfortable lives that involve little in the way of slaying 
monsters or protecting kingdoms. The future is bright, and the 
skies are mostly clear. Mostly.
But it wasn’t always this way. Long ago, in a time of great 
danger, large portions of the earth were torn from the surface 
and raised into the air by powerful crystals buried deep within 
the land—crystals that became known as skyshards. No one 
knows what caused the shards’ power to activate, but when 
they did, they took castles, forests, and entire towns into 
the clouds with them. Smaller skyshards could be attached 
to mundane objects and vehicles, and these skybound 
innovations allowed people to navigate the new world above.
The lands held aloft by skyshards became known as the 
sky islands (usually shortened to skylands). Each island is 
said to hold unique magical energy and host treacherous 
secrets. Some contain ancient ruins, remnants of long-dead 
civilizations visited by only the bravest adventurers and most 
dedicated scholars. Those who study these ancient citadels 
believe they were purposefully constructed around skyshards 
to harness their power.
T oday, people live in many of the villages, fortresses, and 
homes that were raised into the sky so many centuries ago, 
their buildings and technologies evolving with their needs. 
Other skylands are only recently inhabited, with daring 
entrepreneurs building malls for magical goods, hotels for 
weary adventurers, and diners that drift through the clouds. 
Smaller skylands are claimed by recluses who construct small 
homesteads on portions of rock they can pilot through the 
skies. Still more are entirely uninhabited—inhospitable, filled 
with wild magic, and teeming with beasts too powerful to 
tame.
One sky island holds a massive castle. This ancient edifice is 
a prestigious high school where young people from all over 
enroll to study magic, train with swords, soar through the 
skies, and sit through algebra. Everyone calls it Castle High.
Sought after for its premier education and location, Castle 
High invites students from across the realm to learn from 
expert teachers. Here, the next generation of adventurers, 
magicians, fighters, and sky explorers train among the clouds.
Castle High is a boarding school—new students are grouped 
into small teams and assigned a shared dormitory within one 
of four unique towers. Though each student takes their own 
classes, Castle High also doles out “tower grades” based on 
a team’s ability to collaborate on extracurricular projects. 
Defined by the teaching style and preferences of their tower 
dean, each group at Castle High receives a unique education 
tailored to their “individual goals and styles of learning.” Or, at 
least, that’s what it says in the brochure.
In their tower dorms, students study together, live together, 
and fight over sink space. In their classes, they pass notes in 
homeroom, doodle hexes on their binders, and get sent to 
detention. And between it all, they sneak out to explore and 
to find answers to the sky’s biggest questions—maybe now 
more desperately than ever.
Over the past school year, eight kids have gone missing 
exploring the skylands—far more than have ever been lost 
before. It’s all the older students could talk about during 
orientation. The school has stayed silent on the matter, and no 
teacher will say anything about the disappearances, much less 
mention the kids by name.
Even so, the students have heard (and spread) rumors 
of something sinister happening out among the clouds, 
something they think the grown-ups might be trying to hide. 
Some kids are whispering that all the missing students 
were members of one tower, and their tower dean caused 
their disappearance. Others claim they were cult initiates or 
were snapped up by a ravenous dragon. Some—especially 
the younger kids—are saying that the mysterious “Night 
Snatcher” who roams the towers got them. One particularly 
prevalent rumor claims that the missing kids discovered 
something in the school, and Headmaster Fellowes (she/her) 
got rid of them to keep it a secret.
Adding fuel to this rumor, Headmaster Fellowes (who led 
the school for four decades) didn’t come back this semester. 
Instead, newly installed Headmaster Dove (they/them) took 
over. Formerly the head of the Arcana Department, they were 
known for their boisterous energy and creative approach to 
teaching. But now, Dove’s demeanor is different—they’re 
more serious and much stricter about the rules. Dove won’t 
talk about Fellowes’s apparent departure, and they sent a kid 
to detention during orientation for using an illusion spell to 
hang a banner commemorating their missing friend.
With a new headmaster and all the celebration that comes 
with orientation, most people were hoping to put the 
tragedies in the past. But on the fifth night of orientation 
week, right after the big dance, a student named Riley Averill 
disappeared. Some kids have said Riley was acting strangely 
the day before their disappearance, but nobody has come 
forward with any more information. Their broom is missing, 
but they left their backpack and private journal behind in their 
tower room.
No one knows what to do. Students are panicking, and the 
adults are pretending nothing is wrong. The school year is off 
to an unnerving start, and one thing is for sure: something 
strange is going on at Castle High.
117Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 119 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
COMMUNITIES
All communities are available in a Castle High campaign. As needed, provide the following information to your players and 
choose one or more of the questions to ask them during your session zero.
All Communities
Castle High is a campus where students of all backgrounds 
study and live together miles away from their homes. And 
while the towers, majors, cliques, and hierarchies of Castle 
High might quickly feel more important than their upbringing, 
each student arrives with a history and culture that help shape 
their life in the castle.
• How does your community feel about Castle High? Did 
they help you go, make you go, or try to stop you?
• Who from your community do you miss most? What 
unique way have they promised to communicate with you 
while you’re away?
• What about living here feels very different from home? 
What feels familiar?
ANCESTRIES
All ancestries are available, but some have unique aspects in a Castle High campaign. As needed, provide the following 
information to your players.
All Ancestries
This modern fantasy world includes all ancestries. You’ll find 
elves wearing jeans, fungril watching TV, and ribbets shopping 
in grocery stores.
Regardless of their lifespan, all ancestries in a Castle High 
campaign go through a period of adolescence.
Faeries & Aetheris
Naturally winged, faerie and aetheris students zip around the 
campus with ease. A player choosing an ancestry with flight 
should consider how this informs their choice of skyshard 
artifact (see “The Skyshards” on page 125). They can still 
choose a skyshard if they’d like to have one for aesthetic 
reasons, but their flight isn’t dependent on it.
CLASSES
All classes are available, but some have unique aspects in a Castle High campaign. As needed, provide the following information 
to your players.
Beastbound Rangers
At Castle High, many Beastbound ranger students bond with 
an animal companion that can fly. If a player wants to use their 
animal companion as a flying mount, consider what happens 
if their companion is forced out of the scene and that PC no 
longer has a means to fly.
A ranger can incorporate their skyshard into their companion’s 
saddle or use it as a trinket that allows them to communicate 
with and call their mount. During character creation, discuss 
how the ranger and their companion navigate the skies 
together.
Druids
The Beastform options available to a Tier 1 druid don’t include 
one with flight, so players who want to rely on their Beastform 
for flight won’t have access right away. A druid character can 
rely on their skyshard artifact until Tier 2, or the player can 
work with the GM to modify a Tier 1 Beastform option to have 
wings.
Syndicate Rogue
Though the Syndicate rogue’s “Contacts Everywhere” feature 
can certainly be used when visiting other skylands outside 
Castle High, players might also consider using this feature 
around campus, such as when visiting a rival tower or when 
another school comes to visit for a sports or academic 
competition.
Winged Sentinel Seraphs
The “Wings of Light” class feature grants flight. As Winged 
Sentinel seraphs already have a means to fly, players should 
consider how this informs their choice of skyshard artifact 
(see “The Skyshards” on page 125). They can still choose a 
skyshard if they’d like to have one for aesthetic reasons, but 
their flight isn’t dependent on it.
118 Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 120 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
PLA YER PRINCIPLES
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players the following information before character creation.
Cast Spells, Wear Sneakers
Play a modern teenager in a fantastical world, reinterpreting 
character details in a contemporary context. Does your 
character keep their spells in a three-ring binder? Do they 
decorate their shield with stickers? What’s the name of that 
katari rock band they’re obsessed with?
Do Your Homework
One day, your character will be a hero, but today they’re a 
student. Whether they’re a teacher’s pet, a rebel, or a would-
be legend who just wants to save the world already, they have 
to deal with school. So take time to explore that side of the 
game. Who’s their favorite teacher? How are their grades? 
Yes, the world is in peril, and yes, their boyfriend just dumped 
them, but right now, the real problem is that their chemistry 
paper is due in four hours, and they haven’t even started.
Play in the Clouds
Students explore the skylands for many reasons, such as 
treasure, knowledge, or investigating their fellow students’ 
disappearances. But it’s also fun! Young people have a strong 
appetite for adventure, and Castle High campaigns are great 
for excited, proactive PCs who do things for the joy of it. 
Describe them flying, doing tricks, feeling the sunshine on 
their skin. Horse around. Hang out a bit. Poke things with 
sticks. Have them laugh after a dragon nearly eats them, then 
show off their gnarly bruises. Go make some memories.
Fly for Your Life
Castle High can have its fair share of battles, but teenage 
adventure stories often feature other kinds of action—
especially chases. Your character won’t be able to fight every 
monster head-on. They’ll need to keep out of biting range, lead 
adversaries into traps, and make them eat dust. The sky is 
their battlefield, and flight is their most powerful weapon. So 
when they see that big monster, take off!
Feel like a Kid
No matter how fantastical your character is, they’re also just 
a normal kid going through the same things kids have gone 
through forever: growth, change, heartbreak, caring too much 
about a pair of jeans, and pretending to be adults until the 
moment they suddenly are. Your character might have grown 
from a spore or be able to breathe fire, but when you describe 
their feelings, they become grounded and relatable.
Search out Answers
With the adults working hard to keep the missing students 
hush-hush, ensure your character has a strong motivation to 
search for answers. Maybe one of the missing kids was their 
neighbor growing up, they developed a connection to Riley 
during orientation, or their best friend is obsessed with finding 
the missing students and your character’s doing their best to 
support them. Whatever works best for your character, tie 
them into the mystery in a meaningful way.
119Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 121 =====
Castle HigH
GM PRINCIPLES
Keep the following guidance in mind while you GM this campaign.
Synthesize the Modern and the Fantastical
Everyone at the table will modernize elements of Daggerheart 
throughout play, but they’ll look to you to set the tone and 
guardrails. Are you going for something general (“early 21st 
century, phones, jeans, and sneakers”) or specific (“2003, 
social media, Y2K fashion”)? Modernize certain things and 
leave others alone—maybe flashlights have replaced torches, 
but the students use swords because modern weapons don’t 
work on magical beasts.
Cut to the Chase
The setting and tools in Castle High are designed to support 
thrilling aerial escapes, but it’s up to you to tee things up. 
Create enemies who can fly, and challenge the PCs with foes 
too powerful to face head-on. And when it’s time for a chase, 
don’t wait for them to run. Just say this magic phrase: “It 
starts chasing you! Where are you heading?”
Keep It Emotionally Grounded
No matter how strange the world or how high the fantasy, if 
the people feel real, the story will, too. Let your NPCs behave 
realistically, feel normal emotions, and care about the things 
teenagers (or teachers) actually would. Emotional truth can 
ground even the most fantastical scenes.
Run a School in the Sky
Castle High is a living place—it exists to push and pull on 
the characters with rigid structure, dynamic characters, and 
high expectations. The teachers have relationships and goals. 
The students form factions and follow trends. The school has 
rules, and those rules are enforced. Every time the students 
meet a new NPC, write down their name, motive, and one 
small detail about how they look or talk. Within a few sessions, 
you’ll have a bustling campus.
Build an Aerial Playground
Design skylands that are fun for teenagers to explore. 
Describe architecture that’s fun to fly through, such as 
narrow gaps, ramps, or chunks of winding staircase. Place 
strange objects behind mysterious doors. Create levers for 
them to pull, figuratively and literally. Entice them with areas 
that are safe to mess around in and areas so dangerous 
they’re a puzzle just to navigate. Give them problems they 
can’t immediately solve and toys they can’t immediately see 
the purpose of, then look to the players to concoct clever 
solutions.
120 Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 122 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
DISTINCTIONS
Use this information to prepare your campaign. You can also share it with your players as needed.
The School
Castle High has been around as long as anyone remembers. 
Students constantly debate its age, its history, the original 
purpose of each chamber, and whether Dean Lesher (he/him) 
was hired by the principal or simply freed from his coffin when 
the east wing of the castle was being renovated.
The castle is built of hewn granite on a mass of jagged 
bedrock, with a particularly large skyshard jutting out of 
its base. The island can be piloted to specific destinations, 
but it typically follows the trade winds in a yearlong circuit, 
anchoring at occasional port towns along the way to resupply.
Experts in their field aim to teach at Castle High because it 
funds and facilitates its faculty’s research. Many teachers 
have their own goals beyond classroom instruction. Some 
view teaching as a chore or an opportunity to push their 
projects forward, while others consider it a duty. A few even 
enjoy teaching for its own sake.
DOMAINS AS FIELDS OF STUDY
In Castle High, domains represent areas of study. Students 
are free to take any courses they want, but they must focus 
most of their studies on the two domains that make up their 
class. Around campus, a student studying to become a wizard 
might share their Codex course notes with a classmate looking 
to become a bard while a druid student scurries by them, late 
to a botany class for their studies in Sage. Meanwhile, a Blade 
domain fencing course might be intended for students hoping 
to become warriors and guardians, but that doesn’t stop 
students with a variety of academic interests from enrolling.
THE CAMPUS
The campus is full of locations where students study, 
socialize, and relax between it all.
Armory: This bustling place holds crowded workbenches, 
weapon racks, and bins of miscellaneous survival gear. It’s a 
common meeting place for Blade courses. They keep the good 
stuff locked in a sentient chest. Only a few teachers have a key.
Atrium: This campus garden has seen better days, but it still 
hosts an impressive variety of flora. It’s a common meeting 
place for Sage courses. The atrium is always accepting 
donations of wild flora, and the teachers won’t ask where 
students got them.
Catacombs: The catacombs are the deepest part of the 
school, accessible only by winding spiral staircases. Many 
Dread courses are located in these caverns. Strange, 
otherworldly sounds echo through the halls of the catacombs, 
but nobody seems to be able to locate their source.
Clerestory: In this sunlit chamber, students pray, carve 
their initials into pews, and take Splendor courses. 
The intricate stained glass contains three words in an 
unknown language.
Courtyard: The Castle High quad is the school’s social hub, 
where students eat lunch, gossip in study groups, and practice 
broom tricks. A statue of the school’s mysterious founder was 
vandalized last week with the word “liar.”
Forgotten Corridors: A place of shadows, the corridors 
are where kids go to play truth or dare, hold clandestine 
meetings, and take secret Midnight courses not on the official 
curriculum. Older students have a map they pass around, but it 
has never been finished.
Gymnasium: With weights, sparring rings, and storied locker 
rooms, the gym is the common meeting place for Bone 
courses, assemblies, and the occasional school dance. Every 
morning, the floor is covered in ashes, and no one knows why.
Landing: This large open-air platform is used for unloading 
skyships, stabling mounts, learning to fly, and playing broom 
sports. A pair of cranky old manticores sleep behind the stables.
Library: This archive houses books, maps, and a computer 
room possibly older than the castle itself—and at least 
one ghost. Codex courses are commonly held within its 
collections. They say the ghost will tell you a secret if you tell 
her one first.
Ramparts: Castle High’s main observation platform is also the 
common meeting place for Valor courses. A mounted telescope 
is used to monitor the nearby skylands, but it can also be used to 
spy on campus.
Theater: Here you’ll find musical instruments, costumes, and 
a couple of students making out in the tech booth. The theater 
is a common meeting place for Grace courses and school 
assemblies. Sometimes the stage lights flash in a mysterious 
pattern no one can decipher or control.
Vents: The system that steers Castle High uses massive 
exhaust vents on the bottom of the skyland. The mists here 
are heavy with magic and make it a common meeting place 
for Arcana courses. I bet you can’t make it through the tunnels 
before they turn the air vents on!
