Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 123 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
CASTLE HIGH TOWERS
Students at Castle High are first sorted into four towers. 
Within each tower, they’re split into teams of 2–5 students 
known as tower groups. Students in a tower group share 
a dorm room, work on group projects together, and are 
expected to help each other. Each group is given a tower 
grade based on their class scores, participation in projects and 
extracurriculars, and general behavior.
While the characters typically have no choice where they go, 
the players should select their tower together, because their 
choice will influence the style of the campaign they’re playing. 
The remaining towers can be brought into the story as the 
homes of friendly classmates, bitter rivals, or crushes living 
across campus.
Zenith
The tallest tower in the castle is overseen by Dean Parsons 
(she/her), a peppy eccentric with pencils in her hair and 
no time for paperwork. She loves teaching but dislikes the 
classroom, preferring to give students assignments that get 
them out of the school and in over their heads.
Choose Zenith if you want a mission-based campaign where 
school assignments often require you to explore other 
skylands.
Are these strange missions part of some greater purpose? 
What does Parsons mean when she says you’ve “been chosen 
for a reason”?
If players choose Zenith tower, consider how someone or 
something on the surrounding skylands plays a prominent 
part in the mystery of the missing kids.
Star
Castle High’s starboard tower (or simply Star) is overseen 
by Dean “Coach” Hallister (he/him), a gruff gym teacher 
who’s nearly as intense as his aftershave. Coach structures 
each school year as a multi-event tournament complete with 
jerseys, prizes, and every team’s grades on a giant scoreboard.
Choose Star if you want a structured campaign about 
competitive underdogs, tower rivalries, and learning to work 
as a team.
Why is Coach so hard on you? Does he see your potential or 
think you’re losers—or is this tournament rigged?
If players choose Star tower, consider how a competition 
or tournament at the school plays a prominent part in the 
mystery of the missing kids.
Bower
Bow T ower (called Bower by students) is nominally overseen 
by Dean Lin (they/them), a famously easygoing, chronically 
absent teacher usually sleeping one off. Most students use 
this freedom to slack off and party, but some use it as an 
opportunity to pursue their own adventuring goals unimpeded.
Choose Bower if you want a more player-driven campaign 
about independent young adventurers and opportunists.
Is Lin really as uninterested as they seem? Why are they tired 
all the time? Are their headaches a cover for something else?
If players choose Bow tower, consider how the failings 
of the adults in Castle High play a prominent part in the 
mystery of the missing kids.
Stern
Stern T ower is overseen by Dean Lesher (he/him), a strict 
teacher with an old-fashioned teaching style and zero 
tolerance for nonsense. The windows are barred, and curfew 
is early. Delinquents go in and honors students come out, 
rebuilt by drills, discipline, and rote memorization. Of course, 
some students just refuse to be taught.
Choose Stern if you want a more player-driven campaign 
about rebellious students sneaking out, getting into trouble, 
and learning things they shouldn’t.
What’s Lesher hiding? A scheme to take over the school? 
A shrine to the Demon Lord? A sensitive side and good 
intentions?
If players choose Stern tower, consider how the themes 
of freedom and control play a prominent part in the 
mystery of the missing kids.
Lock
The portside tower is sealed off. Everyone calls it Lock T ower, 
and students whisper about what’s inside, how to get in, and 
why a single window lights up every midnight.
GMs can use Lock T ower as an adventure hook or a space for 
the table to create their own tower.
Transferring Towers
Your table might want to try a few towers during the 
campaign. In this case, you could say that groups transfer 
to new towers each time Castle High completes its circuit, 
representing a single grade of high school.
Additionally, a midyear transfer might be required in the event 
of expulsion, faculty restructuring, or some other school 
scandal.
122 Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 124 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
The Missing Kids
Something has been causing students to go missing. During 
your session zero, ask the players for rumors about what 
caused their disappearance and gather ideas from their 
character building and map creation to come up with the 
answer. Alternatively, you can choose an option from the list 
below. Then select a few additional rumors to create multiple 
potential threats, ensuring there are many avenues for players 
to explore before uncovering the answer.
• An ancient creature of dark magic has been unleashed 
on a nearby skyland.
• A teacher is trying to summon a powerful deity or 
monster from another realm of existence to harness 
their power, and they’ve been using students as bait.
• A band of sky pirates has been recruiting young 
members as crew.
• A huge dragonshark has been hunting in the clouds 
surrounding the school.
• A magical storm is teleporting kids to skylands on the 
other side of the world.
• A powerful and corrupt sorcerer is using the hidden 
basement within the deepest chambers of the school 
as a workshop for their nefarious experimentation on 
students.
RILEY AVERILL
There are nine missing students—including one who’s 
particularly close to the PCs named Riley Averill. Riley was 
a member of the PC’s tower group, and they bonded with 
everyone over the first five days of orientation. They were 
ready to spend the next year hanging out, studying, and living 
with the team. Now they’re gone, and the PCs should feel that 
loss.
The PCs should all have a connection to and care about Riley, 
so work with the players to build Riley together. Once you’ve 
agreed on Riley’s pronouns, have each player answer one 
or more of the questions below. Roll a d20 to select them 
randomly or have the players choose. You can also come up 
with your own questions.
Roll Description
1 What did Riley look like? How did they like to 
dress?
2 What unique interest or hobby did you and Riley 
bond over?
3 Where did Riley say they came from? How did their 
family feel about Castle High?
4 Did Riley want to come to Castle High? Why or 
why not?
5 What nickname did you give Riley?
6 How did Riley decorate their portion of the dorm?
7 Which domains did Riley plan to study?
8 Which extracurriculars was Riley excited about?
Roll Description
9 What did Riley do during orientation that made you 
laugh?
10 What secret did Riley tell you?
11 What could you tell Riley was insecure about?
12 What surprising skill did Riley have?
13 What did you notice Riley always doodling on their 
papers?
14 What secret did Riley find in your tower?
15 What did Riley say they wanted to be when they 
grew up?
16 Which room in the school was Riley’s favorite? 
Why?
17
Riley got in a fight with another student during 
orientation. Who was it, what caused the fight, and 
how did it end?
18
Riley got along well with one of the older kids who 
helped new students during orientation. What was 
their name, and why did they bond with Riley?
19
One of the teachers threatened Riley with 
detention during orientation. Who was the 
teacher? What did the teacher accuse them of? 
Did Riley do it?
20
On the second night of orientation, you found Riley 
crying in the middle of the night. What did Riley 
confide in you?
Riley’s Journal
Riley left their journal behind when they disappeared, and you 
can use it to drop clues to your players about the mystery. 
Before your first session, handwrite a journal entry on a piece 
of lined paper from Riley’s point of view about orientation 
week. Make sure to include references to specific moments of 
orientation the players created during your session zero. Then, 
near the end, include some ominous message like:
“I still don’t know who to trust with what I know about the 
disappearances. For now, it all has to stay a secret until I can 
find out more.”
Lastly, decide where you want to send the players to gather 
clues about the students’ disappearances and add a final 
sentence to the journal entry that hints at that location. For 
example, if you want to send them to the stables, the journal 
might say, “I’m headed to go feed Willis. That calms me 
down.” Record the name “Willis” to drop later as one of the 
griffins who lives in the stables.
When players investigate Riley’s journal, describe it as having 
every page ripped out except for one near the back of the 
book, then give them your written page to read. Between 
every session (or so), prepare a new journal page for them to 
discover. Throughout the entries, include clues, red herrings, 
relationship drama, Riley’s thoughts about the PCs, and 
anything else that gives further insight into Riley and the 
mystery that led to their disappearance.
123Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 125 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
Mounts
Access to flying mounts is considered a special privilege 
or mark of achievement, and dedicated students might be 
granted access to the castle stables for difficult assignments. 
Castle High’s stables commonly include griffins, juvenile 
flickerflies, strixwolves, rocs, and even an old wyvern.
Beastbound rangers with a companion that can fly are 
exceptions to this rule. Because the companion is a part of 
their curriculum, they’re allowed to bring it through the halls 
and into class—as long as it’s well-behaved.
Roosts
These winged huts have been used by hermits and witches 
for ages, and they’ve become more popular as people have 
learned new ways to craft them with skyshards. Roosts are 
small buildings that are often, but not always, a single room 
with wings and legs. They (probably) come if called and like 
to receive scritches behind their chimneys. They’re favored by 
sky explorers in recent years due to their hardiness, versatility, 
and ability to anchor to nearly any structure with their talons.
At Castle High, roosts are primarily used to shuttle cargo 
and passengers safely and can be perched by windows 
and balconies to provide extra square footage to faculty. 
Additionally, older students have been known to fly them 
off campus for parties and other extracurricular adventures. 
Just as cars provide privacy and prestige to students on the 
surface, roosts provide them at Castle High.
Skylands
The sky islands that drift above the lithosphere are as varied 
as the formations of the earth below. Each landmass is 
different—some are massive, and some can barely hold a full-
grown goblin—but they’re all held aloft by the magic of the 
skyshards buried in their soil. No one knows what caused the 
skyshards to activate all those centuries ago. Archaeologists, 
historians, and explorers can still be found among the ruins 
searching for an answer! But ever since the skylands rose, 
people have navigated the clouds like they sail through the 
seas, exploring distant islands and designing unique vessels to 
get them there.
Many choose to explore the abandoned structures of ancient 
ruins or the as-yet-uncharted fantastical landscapes of wild 
skylands. The less explored an island is, the more dangerous 
it tends to be. An overwhelming variety of plants, animals, 
monsters, and spirits shelter in these wilds, and only a fraction 
have been documented.
Others choose to study the way skylands move, how they can 
be used for new development, and how to attract those on the 
surface up to this world in the skies. Intrepid entrepreneurs 
have attempted to harness or build upon the ruins, creating 
skyways between islands or offering tourist destinations such 
as theme parks or historical centers.
Skylands drift with weather fronts, travel predictably along 
tradewinds, or rise from the clouds below on updrafts. 
Collisions aren’t unheard of, creating floating debris fields, 
cracking open fortresses, knocking buildings into jarring 
rotation, and nudging entire cities into new trajectories. Older 
ruins can even crumble apart and drop out of the skies entirely 
when their skyshards shatter.
For the students of Castle High, the skylands are both 
playground and mystery, often miles away from (or above) 
adult supervision. They venture out on brooms and mounts 
to relax, explore, or complete assignments that send them 
soaring through the clouds and researching specific lands. And 
with so many students missing, they might also go searching 
for who or what is responsible, hoping to bring their friends 
home safely.
EXAMPLE SKYLANDS
Akroepis: T oppled columns and cracked pillars. Broken 
aqueducts and neglected basilicas. Peacocks roost in cypress 
trees, waterfalls cascade endlessly, and moon ivy grows 
into the heavens. Mysterious sounds emanate from three-
dimensional labyrinths. Built during the time of the Forgotten 
Gods, these ruins are in impeccable condition despite the 
years. Archaeologists frequent this skyland, though it’s so 
dangerous that many never leave.
Cloud Nine Diner: Silver mirror walls covered in vintage 
posters. A jukebox that plays classics and reads people’s 
futures. The best pancakes in the sky served by a no-nonsense 
waitress. Sky shippers at the countertop with stories to 
tell. Open 24/7, this diner feels like it exists outside space 
and time. Alma (she/her), the head waitress, is said to be a 
powerful warlock.
The Corner Store: Narrow aisles brimming with snacks and 
the smell of too many slushie flavors mixed together. Potions 
alongside sodas in standing coolers. Weapons and magical 
items stocked behind the counter. Run by a very, very small 
ribbet named Dabble (she/her), this minuscule skyland is just 
big enough to hold the shop. She always says, “If you need it, I 
got it. And if I don’t got it, you don’t need it that bad.”
Happyland Coaster Park: Rusting rollercoasters and carnival 
games. Slides that lead off the island’s edge. A Ferris wheel 
that stares with an unblinking eye. The scents of cotton candy 
and funnel cake with no apparent source. Randall “Happy” 
Callahan (he/him) tried to create a theme park in the sky 
decades ago, but the operation was shady and the location 
was soon left abandoned. Kids love to come here for secret 
meetings, and everyone says it’s haunted.
Krawl: Moss unfurls over derelict keeps. Wrens scatter and 
ruins invert with the sporadic ringing of a leaning bell tower. 
Dungeons roamed by the undead host untold secrets. Once 
the home of a violent king, the stone seems to know when 
people come within its grasp, and the walls shift to trap 
explorers alongside the remains of fallen armies.
Landren: Crumbling brick and rusted iron. Ceaseless coal fires 
and decrepit smokestacks. Owls pluck rats from alleys, and 
corroded machines carry out their inscrutable programming 
endlessly. Students say whoever runs this skyland is 
manufacturing something dangerous. The island only appears 
at night.
124 Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 126 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
Saelor’s Stadium: Rings of seats filled with thundering 
crowds. Hot dogs and popcorn stuffed into mouths or thrown 
into the air. Bands in bold uniforms playing loud music. The 
stadium is used for a variety of sports, duels, and other 
competitions.
Tirnish: Overgrown skyscrapers and tarnished chrome. Empty 
offices and rusted monorails. Pigeons nest in satellite dishes, 
buckwheat grows from the asphalt, and a dozen drive-ins’ 
worth of pastel automobiles sit in the roads, empty. No one 
knows the official story about what happened here, but the 
radioactive elementals and smiling zombies might provide 
some clues.
OTHER POINTS OF INTEREST
There are books, lessons, and maps filled with seemingly 
infinite lands to explore among the skies. Older students 
describe strange places only they have seen. Radio broadcasts 
describe floating palaces, jungles, and snow-covered peaks 
separated from their mountains. Passed notes illustrate 
cursed cities of unblinking people that appear only during 
specific weather patterns.
If you want to create your own skylands, think about what 
makes each place fantastical and worth exploring. Use the 
following questions as a jumping-off point as needed:
• What’s unique about this skyland? What does it look 
like?
• How big is it? Where is the skyshard that keeps it aloft?
• Who or what lives here, if anyone?
• What can PCs discover on or around this skyland?
• What, if anything, makes this skyland dangerous or 
challenging to navigate?
• How does this skyland relate to the campaign, 
characters, or story beats you’re exploring?
The Skyshards
Skyshards were once believed to be completely mundane, 
useful for currency, jewelry, or decoration. But centuries 
ago, all the skyshards activated at once, causing the people, 
objects, and lands bearing them to lift into the sky and 
drift forever among the clouds, skybound. Many speculate 
that these crystals were once portions of shooting stars, 
magical slag created from the essence of the gods, or even 
the very soul of the Mortal Realm itself. While no one knows 
for sure, in the time since “the Rise,” skyshards have led to 
advancements in architecture and travel the likes of which the 
realm has never seen.
These crystals are now highly sought-after for their ability 
to provide the power of flight. They can be mined from 
skylands or fished from the air, where they drift indefinitely. 
By incorporating them into specially crafted objects designed 
to enhance and focus their potential, these crystals become 
skyshard artifacts, which have unlocked the skies like never 
before. Large chunks can lift entire vehicles, buildings, or land 
masses, while small shards allow users to take to the air on 
their own.
SKYSHARD ARTIFACTS
Once placed into an object and given time to attune, a 
skyshard grants that object controllable flight. An entire 
industry has been created to make artifacts that rise at the 
user’s command and allow anyone to pilot them. From mages 
famous for their custom skyshard cloaks to companies that 
make winged sneakers to unique harnesses built for mounts 
and familiars, the origin, form, and function of each artifact 
vary greatly.
Though skyshards are magical, and only experts can craft an 
artifact that responds to the user, people don’t need magical 
ability to use a skyshard artifact.
Each student at Castle High has been given a skyshard 
artifact. Use the following list as inspiration to help each 
player create their character’s skyshard artifact:
• A broom (the most common option)
• A vacuum, carpet, or other mundane household item
• A medallion, necklace, small trinket, or other keepsake
• A cane, leg braces, wheelchair, or other mobility aid
• A fine cloak, winged sneakers, team jersey, or other 
apparel
• A breastplate, chainmail, shield, or other means of 
protection
• A sword, mace, axe, or other physical weapon
• A wand, staff, scepter, or other magical weapon
You can also ask the following questions as needed to help a 
player create their artifact’s lore:
• How did you acquire your artifact? Was it inherited, 
discovered, crafted, grown, or something else?
• Describe your artifact’s appearance. Is it ancient, 
covered in stickers, always humming, or something 
else?
FLYING WITH STYLE
While brooms are certainly the most common means of 
flying around Castle High, not everyone uses them the same 
way—especially since some skyshard artifacts come with 
personality. Some brooms are stubborn and unruly and, while 
fast, require additional negotiation. But speed and stunts are 
everything at Castle High. Choice broom mods or new racing 
records are common topics in the hallways. The first kid to 
ride their staff switch-footed through the entire atrium without 
getting caught instantly gained indisputable legend status.
There are less common, yet equally fun ways students choose 
to fly. For example, a stylish flying technique known as blade 
riding has emerged in recent years: Students speed by atop 
enchanted swords, axes, and other bladed weapons, often 
borrowing techniques developed generations ago by skaters 
and surfers. Though it has parents and teachers in a panic, 
blade riding is reluctantly allowed.
For more information, see the “Flight” section on page 127.
125Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 127 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
THE INCITING INCIDENT
You can use the prompt below to start your campaign, or create your own.
It’s the last night of orientation week. It’s been intense. 
Everyone has blisters on their sword hands, splinters from 
their broomsticks, and scents of spells clinging to their hair. 
Students are buzzing with conversation out in the courtyard, 
sharing stories from their first week at Castle High. Amid the 
excitement, a shape appears in the clouds, coming closer and 
closer until…
A boy wearing a Castle High sweater and clinging to his 
broomstick crash-lands in the courtyard, unconscious and very 
hurt. As Mrs. Wickett (she/her), the headmaster’s assistant, 
runs to him and calls for everyone to back away, the student’s 
eyelids flit open for just a moment. He locks eyes with the 
nearest student, a tall infernis cheerleader, and whispers, 
“They’re going to get—!” But the boy’s words catch in his 
throat, and he loses consciousness again before he can speak 
the rest.
As everyone is cleared out of the courtyard and shuffled to 
their respective towers, one of the older students says the 
boy’s name is Oliver Fleming (he/him)—one of the eight kids 
who went missing last school year while out exploring the 
ruins. After months with no word, Oliver is back.
A few bold students ask Mrs. Wickett what the school is going 
to do, and if the teachers will talk to Oliver and send people 
out to whichever skyland he came from. “What if he knows 
what happened to the other kids?” But Mrs. Wickett tells 
everyone to hush and head to their dormitories, refusing to 
say anything more about the disappearances until “after Mr. 
Fleming has recovered.”
Will the group sneak out of their tower to find Oliver to ask 
him questions? Will they gather stories about Oliver from 
fellow students to piece together where he’s been? Does Mrs. 
Wickett know more than she’s letting on?
126 Chapter 4: Castle High


===== PAGE 128 =====
CAsT l E  Hig H
CAMPAIGN MECHANICS
The following mechanics are unique to this campaign.
Flight
All attendees of Castle High are required to master flying, 
both for their safety and to explore the skylands. Each 
semester, the latest tricked-out brooms, stylized flight spells, 
and weapon-surfing techniques are showcased by a new 
generation of fliers seeking to define their experience in the 
skies.
During character creation, each player will create a skyshard 
artifact they’ll use to fly. While the PCs are flying, use the 
normal movement rules. They can fly within Close range as 
part of an action roll, but they must roll an appropriate trait to 
move beyond Close range or as their primary action. See the 
upcoming “Using Traits for Flight” section for more details. 
Let the players know that some threats—such as becoming 
Restrained or Vulnerable, losing their artifact, or taking Severe 
damage—may temporarily interrupt their ability to fly (at the 
GM’s discretion).
USING TRAITS FOR FLIGHT
While flying, PCs can take cues from extreme sports, mimic 
birds and other flying animals, or invent their own style. The 
following are some examples of how each trait can be used:
Agility: Unrivaled technique and an uncurbed appetite for 
momentum can make a PC a swift and acrobatic flier. Agility 
might be used to speed-boost off an adversary’s wake, 
maintain an aerodynamic posture, or angle past a winged 
threat.
Finesse: Sometimes focus and a subtle adjustment at the 
exact right moment are all it takes to thread the needle. 
Finesse helps with precision flying techniques, operating finely 
tuned controls, or deftly weaving between obstacles.
Strength: Never skip leg day, because no one’s sure what the 
skies might throw at them next. Fliers with great strength can 
blast through floating debris, hold fast in shifting weather, or 
slam into a racing rival.
Instinct: Some learn to fly by sheer intuition, navigating the 
untamable nature of the winds and sky. Instinct can be used 
to keenly spot a shortcut, follow a migratory current, or sense 
hidden danger in the clouds.
Presence: In the air, some fliers possess natural grace 
and effortless style that make flying look easy. Students 
with presence know how to command attention, cause a 
distraction, or create a spectacle when they take flight.
Knowledge: Some students study everything there is to know 
about magical flight—so they’ve totally got this… in theory. It 
takes knowledge of the skies to chart efficient flight plans, plot 
rotational momentum, or deduce the exact timing of a foe’s 
wingbeats.
A Less Lethal Campaign
For a more lighthearted campaign like Castle High, you might 
decide to narrate most or all damage as nonlethal. This allows 
students to duel each other, unleash magical hijinks on the 
school, or subdue a rival without resorting to true violence. 
Alternatively, you might decide that certain moments are 
dramatic enough to warrant escalation, giving the PCs the 
ability to control whether their damage is lethal or not.
If you’d like to keep death off the table for your campaign, any 
death moves that would normally lead to a character’s demise 
can instead lead to a character ending up in the infirmary for a 
few weeks or being sent home for the semester, making them 
unplayable for some time but not dead.
SESSION ZERO QUESTIONS
Ask your players any of these questions, or make your own.
• Why did you enroll at Castle High?
• Who is one friend you made during orientation? What 
have they told you about their connection to the missing 
students?
• Who is one teacher you made a positive impression on, 
and how did you do it? Who is one teacher you made a 
negative impression on, and what caused it?
• You’ve already begun some of the core classes in your 
track. Pick one feature from your subclass or domains. 
How did you learn it?
• What surprised you when you first entered your tower? 
Were you assigned to the tower you wanted? What aspect 
of your new home most excites you?
• A group in another tower has declared themselves your 
rivals. What tower are they in? What caused this rivalry to 
start? What have they said they’re going to do about it?
• How did Riley’s disappearance affect the last two days of 
your orientation week? What did the adults do about it? 
How has it changed the other students’ behavior?

REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
REign OF THE WEREdRAg O n
In a land ruled by a violent weredragon tyrant, heroes ordained by the stars to defeat her must 
rise to the challenge and defend their people.
Designed by Jeremy Crawford, Rowan Hall, Christopher Perkins, and Spenser Starke
Complexity Rating: ••
THE PITCH
Read this section to your players to introduce them to the 
campaign.
Once upon a time, in the peaceful land of Whelvia, a powerful 
sorceress named Forlorne Lykona deceived the country’s great 
dragon warden to absorb his power and rule the kingdom 
herself. Transformed into a weredragon by the powerful ritual 
that fused the warden’s power with her cold heart, Forlorne 
now takes to the skies, feasting on all who challenge her and 
cursing innocent villagers to become ravenous weredrakes. 
The people called on the brave defenders of the realm, the 
Knights of Whelvia, to protect them—but the knights instead 
bent the knee to the draconic tyrant, allowing her to command 
their forces and leaving the land without protectors.
In a Reign of the Weredragon campaign, you play destined 
heroes of Whelvia, rising up against Forlorne Lykona and her 
knights to save this once-idyllic land from the jaws of tyranny.
TONE & FEEL
Epic, Fairy T ale-Like, Grim, Hopeful
THEMES
Astrology, Fate Written in the Stars, Swords & Sorcery, 
Tyranny’s Rise and Fall
TOUCHSTONES
The Black Cauldron, Conan the Barbarian, For the Queen, 
The Little Mermaid, Sleeping Beauty, The Sword and the 
Sorcerer, The Wolfman
128 Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 130 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
OVERVIEW
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players the following information before character creation.
In the land of Whelvia, some people still tell stories of the 
constellations that illuminate the sky. Among them, dragons 
made of stars appear each night to guide travelers, deliver 
counsel, and perhaps answer the prayers of those who call 
their names in times of need. That is, after all, how the first 
dragon constellation Whelvarax came to be.
Long ago, in the time of Whelvarax (she/her), from whom the 
country of Whelvia would one day derive its name, the land 
was in ruins from decades of invasions. Borders were drawn 
and redrawn as foreign armies came to conquer the verdant 
forests, azure coasts, and high mountain peaks. At the time, 
Whelvarax ignored the people’s plight. The great dragon was 
annoyed by the ruckus and destruction but would long outlive 
the silly conflicts of smaller creatures, so she had no desire to 
wade in.
But the Elder Druids were desperate to stop the fighting. With 
great love for the land and reverence for the dragon, this order 
made an appeal: “Great Whelvarax, we beg your aid in driving 
the invaders from your beautiful home. We know a way to 
raise mortals to godhood through worship. If you help us, the 
people will serve you, and we can found a nation that protects 
your land and exemplifies your wisdom. We will make you a 
god among the stars—an Ascendant.”
Whelvarax was intrigued by this offer and did as the druids 
asked. Using her powerful, fiery breath, she drove out the 
armies and assembled the people to rebuild the territory. She 
taught the Elder Druids and their people how to grow more 
fertile farms, create more just systems of government, and 
defend themselves with more fortitude. Over the decades, the 
land became known for its dragon defender, and people began 
to call themselves Whelvians.
After over two hundred years of prosperity, Whelvarax 
reached the end of her life, and the druids made good on 
their offer. Because her people were so loyal and she was 
so beloved, when the old dragon came to pass through the 
veil of death, the druids were able to perform a ritual that 
called on the gods in the Hallows Above, asking them to raise 
the beloved leader to divinity. Through the druids’ ritual, 
Whelvarax ascended into the sky to become a constellation. In 
subsequent years, people began to look up at night, searching 
for guidance and trusting that their former leader would light 
their way.
Seven dragon wardens followed. Each ruled the land of 
Whelvia with unique powers, personality, and wisdom. Some 
were benevolent, others ruled with fear, but only the most 
powerful ascended to the stars to become Faint Divinities.
During these long years, some dragons sought to expand 
Whelvia’s borders, others to grow the country’s wealth, and 
still others to protect its natural resources. The Elder Druids 
who once saved the land died off, fading into the mists of 
history. In their stead, the Knights of Whelvia rose as sworn 
defenders of the realm. Valorous and true, this band protected 
Whelvians and embodied the hope for a prosperous and just 
nation. Like the druids before them, they were bound to the 
dragon warden through their common goal: the safety and 
prosperity of the people of Whelvia.
129Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 131 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
For the last hundred years, the warden of Whelvia has been a 
purple-scaled dragon named Sazanthe (he/him), who made 
his home in Vexstone Abbey overlooking the sea. He was 
known for his vast knowledge of magic, and visitors came 
from faraway places to confer with him. Sazanthe hoped to 
fortify his people with knowledge and curiosity, ushering in 
an era of peace and prosperity where everyone, even those 
who came from beyond Whelvia, could enjoy the beautiful and 
abundant land they shared.
Like many wardens before him, Sazanthe took a non-dragon 
companion. Warden companions were meant to support 
Whelvians, aid their dragon, and live out the harmonious 
ideals of the druids who once allied with the great dragon 
Whelvarax.
Forlorne Lykona (she/her) did not embody these 
characteristics. An orphaned faun sorceress and cult leader, 
she was more likely to inspire Sazanthe’s suspicion than his 
intellect. But she could read the stars, perhaps better than 
anyone else in Whelvia. In them, she saw Sazanthe’s future: 
that he would become the next Ascendant. Desiring this 
power for herself, she came to Sazanthe promising to share 
an abundance of magic with Whelvia should Sazanthe partner 
with her.
Sazanthe, for all his wisdom, was taken in by the sorceress. He 
was compelled by her incredible talent, and she capitalized on 
this. When the pair undertook the ritual to bond, she instead 
twisted the magic and absorbed the great dragon into herself. 
As Forlorne and Sazanthe became one, the dragon’s shock and 
horror triggered a release of magical energy that tore apart the 
abbey and killed most of the spectators, transforming them 
into ghosts who are trapped in the ruins.
In the months since that night, Forlorne has ruled Whelvia 
as a tyrannical weredragon queen, able to transform at will 
between two forms: her own faun body and Sazanthe’s stolen 
dragon body. Through her absorption of Sazanthe, she intends 
to use the worship of the people of Whelvia to become an 
Ascendant and achieve the power of divinity as so many of 
the dragon wardens before her did. Where Sazanthe spent 
his time as warden bettering the lives of his people, Forlorne 
intends to subjugate Whelvians and use their fear to enhance 
her power.
She withdrew to Nwarlyth, her fortress of stone on the 
region’s tallest mountain, where she uses the Knights of 
Whelvia to carry out her tyranny. Under her orders, they’ve 
turned their forces from protecting against external threats to 
controlling the Whelvian people. Those valorous few who once 
swore to protect the realm now bend the knee to Forlorne, 
claiming that following her ways will “keep the peace.”
Meanwhile, Forlorne sends soldiers to imprison those who 
disagree with her rulings, feeds upon anyone she feels 
threatened by, and uses her dragon’s breath to transform 
dissenters into weredrakes—vicious, bestial predators. 
Sazanthe refused to use this unique power against all but his 
gravest of foes, but Forlorne has embraced it to create a loyal 
army.
Forlorne is desperate to ensure she does all that is necessary 
to ascend to the stars and become a god upon her death, but 
ever since she absorbed Sazanthe and seized his power, the 
constellations she used to look to for guidance—especially the 
stars of the great dragon Whelvarax—have given her nothing 
but silence.
Now, she sends riders in all directions to collect sky seers, 
those who can read the constellations, to provide her counsel 
in place of her own absent ability. But the tyrannical queen 
has a tendency to kill seers who give her answers she doesn’t 
like, leading those with true talent to disappear into hiding and 
false prophets to flock to her fortress. There is a rumor among 
sky seers, whispered in only the most secret places, that 
unexpected and powerful heroes have arisen in Whelvia, and 
they will defeat the weredragon queen.
This land needs protectors—but it won’t be the Elder Druids, 
a dragon, or the Knights of Whelvia riding to the rescue. 
Instead, it will be a band of adventurers destined by the stars 
to play a part in the fall of Forlorne Lykona. You are those 
adventurers.
130 Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 132 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
COMMUNITIES
All communities are available, but some have unique aspects in a Reign of the Weredragon campaign. As needed, provide the 
following information to your players and choose one or more of the questions to ask them during your session zero.
Highborne
Prior to Whelvia’s descent into tyranny, highborne Whelvians 
coexisted relatively peacefully with the peasantry while 
enjoying the prestige and privilege afforded by their status and 
wealth. Now, many highborne households cater to Forlorne’s 
will and whims to gain power. For most, this isn’t motivated by 
a belief that the queen’s decisions are just, or even beneficial 
to Whelvia, but rather the desire to use her reign to grow their 
coffers and improve their station.
• What ties do you have to the Knights of Whelvia, and how 
did their betrayal of the people affect you?
• You are the rightful owner of a valuable parcel of land. 
How has Forlorne’s tyranny placed your holdings in peril?
• You are closely allied with another noble house. Which 
family is it, and how has this alliance been affected by 
recent political turmoil?
Loreborne
Although Whelvia has no large academic institutions, the 
presence of dragons and other knowledgeable individuals has 
fostered smaller enclaves of sages, scholars, sky seers, and 
other truth-seekers. During Sazanthe’s rule, there was a great 
push to cultivate and maintain these collectives, fostering many 
loreborne communities to grow in numbers and influence. But 
ever since Forlorne claimed the throne of Whelvia, loreborne 
communities have become targets. The queen sends her 
knights and their militias to collect or destroy grimoires, capture 
sky seers, and imprison prominent scholars who then face 
corrupt trials that result in them being turned into weredrakes.
• You were punished for warning others of the danger 
Forelorne poses to Whelvia. What was done to you, and 
how did you escape?
• What do you know about Whelvia that no one else knows?
• You protect a small piece of knowledge. What is it, and 
how do you believe the queen and her militia would react 
to learning it?
Orderborne
New orderborne communities are forming, both in support 
and in defiance of Forlorne’s rule. These groups range from 
sects of a larger whole—such as political supporters or 
opponents—to entire settlements.
As Forlorne has gained power, her forces have swelled. The 
Knights of Whelvia control militias that carry out the queen’s 
orders. These groups were once trained to fight foreign 
enemies and keep the peace, but now they’re ordered to hunt 
and harm their fellow citizens for simply disagreeing with the 
beliefs of the queen.
• You were once given an order that you defied. What was 
the order, and what were the consequences?
• When Forlorne’s forces came, your community submitted 
to her rule. Why did they do this, and how did you react?
• You protect someone in your community. Who is it, and 
why are you concerned for their safety?
Reborne
As the queen has gained more power and forced more of 
her subjects to fear for their lives, increasing numbers of 
communities are yielding to her rule. Those who refuse to 
bow alongside their community might leave it instead, either 
to preserve their ideals or save their own lives. People may 
find that their community disavows them if their political 
and moral beliefs don’t align with Forlorne’s proclamations. 
Reborne may come from families, villages, or even entire 
regions of Whelvia that claim to support the queen’s regime—
either as true believers or people too afraid to oppose the 
weredragon.
• Someone in your family sold out a neighbor to Forlorne’s 
forces. What was the neighbor’s crime, and what 
consequences did they face?
• The last words you heard from your family when you left 
will haunt you forever. What did they say?
• Someone you love is a dedicated follower of Forlorne. Who 
are they, and what did they do that let you know it was 
time to leave them behind?
131Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 133 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
Slyborne
The Knights of Whelvia have always worked to stop ne’er-do-
wells across the country. But now, with the knights turning 
their backs on the people in favor of Forlorne’s rule, some 
career criminals are reorienting themselves as well. Once-
apolitical slyborne communities have become champions of 
the downtrodden, vigilantes with axes to grind against the 
weredragon, or even rebel organizations making moves to 
unseat the queen.
• What did you witness that prompted you to start using 
your criminal skills to go against the queen’s regime?
• You have a contact inside Forlorne’s court. Who is it, and 
why have they told you they want to fight Forlorne?
• Since the rise of the queen, your community has begun 
smuggling something through parts of Whelvia. What is it, 
and why must this effort remain secretive?
Some slyborne could be current or former members of 
Forlorne’s network of spies. The queen commonly sends 
individuals to infiltrate groups she believes oppose her. These 
spies might sabotage freedom fighters, tip off the knights, or 
even turn in rebels to face Forlorne’s corrupt court system.
• You’ve spent most of your life indoctrinated by Forlorne’s 
lies. What broke you out, and who are you now running 
from because of it?
• You infiltrated a rebel group and learned critical 
information about their plans. What did you do with this 
knowledge?
• You agreed to become a spy for the queen because her 
forces are holding someone you love captive. Who is it, 
and what deal did they make with you?
Underborne
Beneath the verdant forests, rolling hills, and rugged 
peaks of Whelvia lie rich subterranean dens and enclaves, 
many unknown to the queen and her spies. Enemies of the 
weredragon can find sanctuary here and avail themselves of 
tunnel networks that are often safer to travel than the roads 
above.
The magic portals that permit access to these underground 
sanctuaries are invisible to outsiders and require special keys 
to step through. Members of underborne communities can 
always sense the nature of a portal, which tells them what key 
is needed to pass through it. Portal keys can take many forms, 
such as seeds, berries, leaves from a particular kind of tree, 
passwords, musical notes, or objects of a certain scent or hue.
• Your people have contemplated opening the caverns to 
surface dwellers to help protect them from the queen. 
What stance have you taken and why?
• One of the Knights of Whelvia and their militia have taken 
control of the underground warren you call home. What 
information does your community keep secret from them, 
and what consequences could there be?
• What is the nature of one of the portals that leads to your 
community? What do you keep with you that allows you to 
enter?
Warborne
Since Forlorne’s absorption of Sazanthe and her takeover of 
the country months ago, many peaceful, prosperous parts of 
Whelvia have fallen into destruction and tragedy. Warborne 
individuals are often from small villages that reside on lands 
regularly fought over by the Knights of Whelvia and rebel 
forces. Others come from the warfront itself, their lives 
transformed by the fight for the soul of Whelvia, no matter 
which side of the conflict they’ve fallen on.
• What role have you played in the current conflict so far? 
What circumstances led to your involvement?
• Someone you fought alongside lost their life. Who were 
they, and what did they say to you before they died?
• You once protected someone on the other side of the 
conflict. Who were they, and why did you save their life?
Wildborne
The woodlands of Whelvia are resource rich, which has made 
them highly sought-after by both Forlorne’s forces and local 
rebels, and they’ve become dangerous territory as a result. 
Forests held by the Knights of Whelvia are filled with militia 
encampments that decimate the land through hunting, 
foraging, and deforestation. In response, many wildborne 
are joining rebel groups in an effort to drive out Forlorne’s 
soldiers, using their knowledge of the landscape to build traps 
and orchestrate attacks from the canopies above and the 
underbrush below.
• What sacred place was destroyed by the conflict moving 
into your forest? How has your community reacted?
• In your forest there lives a plant or creature that is 
particularly dangerous to those unfamiliar with it. What is 
it, and what makes it deadly?
• Your home was taken over by one of the queen’s militias. 
What have you often overheard troops saying to one 
another?
ANCESTRIES
All ancestries are available, but one in particular has unique 
aspects in a Reign of the Weredragon campaign. As needed, 
provide the following information to your players.
Drakona
Among the stars in the night sky are dragon-shaped 
constellations, each representing a dragon warden who 
ascended. In Whelvia, some drakona can exhale celestial 
energy akin to starlight when they use their “Elemental 
Breath” ancestry feature. The feature’s effect is otherwise 
unchanged.
132 Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 134 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
TRANSFORMATIONS
All transformations are available, but some have unique 
aspects in a Reign of the Weredragon campaign. As needed, 
provide the following information to your players.
Werewolves & Shapeshifters
Because the queen uses her power as a weredragon to control 
the people of Whelvia, even forcibly transforming those that 
oppose her into weredrakes, anyone who can shift their form 
may be treated with suspicion. People might assume those 
who can transform have that power due to association with 
Forlorne and her terrifying magic. Others with loved ones who 
were transformed into weredrakes might turn to people who 
can shift their forms, hoping to learn ways to help family and 
friends who fell victim to Forlorne’s magic.
CLASSES
All classes are available, but some have unique aspects in a 
Reign of the Weredragon campaign. As needed, provide the 
following information to your players.
Druids
While the order of the Elder Druids that founded Whelvia no 
longer exists, many in this land still treat druids with reverence. 
In particular, villages and small communities that still believe 
in the Ascendants are more likely to treat druids like minor 
celebrities, with locals asking to see displays of magic or receive 
healing. This has more to do with their title as “druids” than 
any specific powers, and other classes that utilize the Sage and 
Arcana domains are less likely to receive similar attentions.
The ancient druids left behind large stones carved with runes 
that defy attempts to decipher them. Druids in modern 
Whelvia might be interested in finding these monoliths, 
studying them, and harnessing their power. There’s hope 
among those who study the ancients and believe in their ways 
that the stones can be utilized to conjure the ancient druids’ 
protective wards, which could be especially potent in the fight 
against Forlorne and her weredrakes.
Like werewolves and shapeshifters, druids might be treated 
with suspicion if they shift into their Beastform around 
Whelvians who fear Forlorne, her weredragon form, and the 
weredrakes she creates. Those who still hold reverence for 
druids might instead seek out these practitioners for this 
exact reason: hoping they might have the power to transform 
their weredrake loved ones back into their original form.
Guardians
The Knights of Whelvia have long been the protectors of the 
realm. Some guardians held childhood dreams of becoming 
knights, going so far as to learn all the proper rituals of 
initiation, only to see their beloved heroes forsake their oaths 
and bow to a tyrant.
A Stalwart guardian might take it upon themself to restore 
the honor of the Knights of Whelvia, perhaps with the help 
of knights who have fallen but aren’t beyond redemption. A 
Vengeance guardian might strive to destroy the fallen Knights of 
Whelvia, judging them as unworthy of the trust placed in them.
Seraphs
While once a seraph who worshipped the Ascendants might 
have been viewed as traditional, or even rustic, this has 
changed since Forlorne’s rise to power. By the weredragon 
queen’s decree, the worship of higher powers other than the 
Ascendants is now illegal in Whelvia. Now a seraph must 
consider the divine being they serve and what that being has 
come to symbolize in this new regime.
133
Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 135 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
PLA YER PRINCIPLES
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players 
the following information before character creation.
Embrace Your Destiny
All the PCs in this campaign have a fate that is intertwined 
with Forlorne Lykona. Consider how your character came 
to know this—perhaps they received some kind of astral 
omen, they were born under strange stars, or they have a 
constellation-shaped birthmark. Who is the sky seer who read 
your character’s fate? What unique detail did the seer tell your 
character about their destiny?
Heal and Unite the Realm
The people of this land remember the idyllic realm Whelvia 
was before Forlorne Lykona became queen. When building 
your character, consider what drives them to hope that the 
realm can be restored and how that affects the way they 
interact with others. Though they might differ in tactics, 
attitude, and levels of dedication, all PCs in this campaign 
should have—or aim to find—a reason to believe in hope for 
Whelvia despite its current state.
Make It Personal
Strive to make the destruction of Whelvia have a personal 
impact on your character’s life. What have Forlorne and her 
subordinates done to set your character on their current path? 
How has her reign of terror changed what is most important 
to them? What specific event or moment made your character 
decide to take the fight to the weredragon tyrant and join 
forces with other like-minded heroes?
Remember Who the Villain Is
Forlorne Lykona is an evil that corrupts all that’s good in 
Whelvia. She turns innocents into weredrakes. She compels 
the Knights of Whelvia to enforce her vile decrees. She 
frightens her subjects into submission while rewarding those 
who spy and snitch on their neighbors. But as your characters 
encounter her minions, be aware that not all of them are 
irredeemably evil or in complete control of their actions. Some 
might even help the PCs under the right circumstances.
The seeds of Forlorne’s destruction are scattered throughout 
Whelvia, waiting to be found. Discover how the tyrant came 
to be. Learn her weaknesses from those who know her best. 
Search for cracks in her armor. Forge alliances. Obtain magic 
that can counter her sorcerous power or break her hold over 
the lesser evils of the realm.
GM PRINCIPLES
Keep the following guidance in mind while you GM this 
campaign.
Make Tyranny Omnipresent
No matter what area of Whelvia the PCs are in, the effects of 
Forlorne’s tyranny should always feel present. Consider the 
ways the culture has changed since Forlorne seized power. 
A former festival of joyous celebration might have been 
transformed into one of regimented reverence—or dangerous 
rebellion. There might be settlements that have fallen 
under complete control of or been leveled by the Knights 
of Whelvia. Travel across the kingdom is regulated, certain 
kinds of speech outlawed, accusations of dissent treated with 
hostility, and certain types of innocent people targeted for 
imprisonment or death simply for existing. Ensure that the 
source of all major conflict in Whelvia directly or indirectly 
traces back to Forlorne.
Showcase Forlorne’s Power
Make it clear to the PCs that Forlorne’s power is extraordinary 
and unmatched. The effects of her mere presence should 
leave NPCs with terrifying stories and horrific nightmares, 
experiences they should share with the PCs. Any direct 
sightings or encounters the PCs have with the weredragon 
should highlight her brutality and unwavering evil—for 
example, Forlorne flying over a town and dropping the head 
of a Whelvian rebel into the center square as a reminder of 
what happens to those who disagree with her, or devouring a 
member of her own forces for failing an order. Above all, she 
should be ruthless, violent, and unyielding.
Before players reach the final battle with 
Forlorne at the end of the campaign, 
any violent confrontations the PCs 
have with her don’t use her stat block. 
Instead, she laughs in the face of 
attacks and disappears from the scene 
at the first opportunity, leaving behind 
utter destruction.
134 Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 136 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
Reveal the Cracks in Her Regime
Though Forlorne herself is very powerful, that doesn’t mean 
everyone in her regime is loyal, competent, or evil. As you’re 
building out your campaign, plant opportunities for the PCs to 
exploit these cracks to get a leg up on their foes. For example, 
the Knights of Whelvia might have a member who’s willing to 
share information for a price or can be persuaded to turn on 
the weredragon under the right circumstances.
For an example NPC who can serve PCs in this way, see 
“Schon” in the “Distinctions” section of this campaign frame.
Build to the Inevitable Confrontation
Though the ultimate confrontation of the campaign should 
be against Forlorne Lykona, it’s vital to have other adversaries 
who stand in the PCs’ way. Consider how you can build to 
the final battle through the prominent adversaries you place 
throughout the campaign.
You might start small, maybe establishing that the town 
the PCs are staying in is under control of the Jagged Knife, 
a criminal gang loyal to the weredragon queen. Their leader 
uses lackeys to enforce the queen’s decrees, spread terror, 
quell uprisings, and eliminate traitors. Later, PCs might 
encounter a Knight of Whelvia who has been assigned by 
Forlorne to command watchtowers, patrol roads, and root out 
troublemakers in an area. Additionally, petty nobles, master 
assassins, pirate captains, royal advisors, and other important 
figures are always vying for a chance to prove their loyalty to 
the queen, and you might use one or more of them to put the 
PCs in a difficult spot during their travels. Think about what 
they each stand to gain from their alliance with her, and use 
that to drive their motivations and tactics in play.
If the PCs are hungry for a chance to meet the villain early in 
the campaign, you might surprise them by giving them what 
they want. It’s fine to have the PCs interact with Forlorne 
before the final showdown. Once her spies realize what the 
characters are up to, she might invite them to her fortress. 
In her faun form, she could use her devilish charm to glean 
their intentions, tempt them with power, and try to bend 
them to her will. If they refuse, she can still let them go 
unharmed—perhaps she expects them to change their minds 
or doesn’t consider them a serious threat, or maybe she plans 
to use them to weed out other disloyal subjects or uncover 
knowledge that has eluded her.
DISTINCTIONS
Use this information to prepare your campaign. You can also 
share it with your players as needed.
The Weredragon Queen
The following sections provide an overview of Forlorne Lykona. 
For her stat blocks, see pages 142 and 143.
THE FIRST ACT OF EVIL
There are many tales of the origin of Forlorne Lykona. Most 
speak of terrible treatment at the hand of her mother, Dolorez 
(she/her). Some include the deep affection she had for her 
twin sister, Harrowyrr (she/her). But all speak of Trigleer’s 
Incantations—a grimoire of dark magic that was used in 
Forlorne’s first act of evil.
What exactly happened in her childhood cottage the night 
of that act is still debated, but the story goes that Forlorne 
used the grimoire to summon a monstrous shadow beast that 
would steal her mother away to the Valley of Death forever. 
Dolorez, a great sorcerer in her own right, set her own house 
ablaze during the attack in an attempt to keep the shadow 
creature at bay with the firelight. Forlorne’s power was greater 
than her mother’s, even at her young age, but while she 
murmured dark spells that ensured Dolorez succumbed to 
the darkness, she did not hear her sister’s cries for help in the 
flame. Harrowyrr was trapped by the fire, burned alive. The 
charred remains of this cottage have sat abandoned since that 
fateful night, a haunted place now known as Witch’s End.
BUILDING FORLORNE COLLABORATIVELY
During your session zero of a Reign of the Weredragon 
campaign, you will build Forlorne Lykona together with your 
players. Print out the Reign of the Weredragon Villain Builder 
sheet (available in the appendix or at www.daggerheart.com/
downloads).
Before character creation, cut out the prompts, fold them up, 
randomly select about fifteen prompts (it’s okay to use more 
or fewer prompts), and place them on the table within reach 
of everyone participating. Then go around the table, starting 
with the player to your left, and have each player grab a 
random prompt, read it aloud, and answer the question. Other 
players may follow up with additional questions based on 
their response, but it’s up to that player if they want to answer 
them. When there are no more follow-up questions, the next 
player reads a prompt and answers it.
If the player chooses a question they would rather not answer, 
or would prefer somebody else answer instead, they can pass 
it to that player or discard it from play. When there are no 
more prompts on the table, continue to character creation.
The questions from the Villain Builder sheet are included on 
the next page for reference and have numbers next to them in 
case your table would rather roll dice than physically cut out 
each option.
Tip: This section draws its primary inspiration from the 
tabletop roleplaying game “For the Queen” by Alex Roberts, 
published by Darrington Press.

===== PAGE 137 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
VILLAIN BUILDER ROLL TABLE
If you want to use the roll table, roll a d4 and a d10. The d4 
should serve as the tens place value, and the d10 should be 
the ones place value. For example, a result of 3 on the d4 
and 7 on the d10 would be the number 37. If a player lands 
on a prompt that’s already been used, they should reroll. 
The process is complete once the players have answered 15 
questions from the table.
Roll Prompt
10
The queen once ordered the creation of something 
beautiful in her honor. What was it, and where 
does it now reside?
11 What is a unique or unexpected part of Forlorne’s 
appearance that affects how people view her?
12
Forlorne and one of the Knights of Whelvia are 
rumored to be having an affair. Who is the knight, 
and what are they known for?
13
Forlorne recently quashed a small rebellion. Who 
led that effort, and what mistake did they make 
that led to their downfall?
14 What symbol do followers of the queen use to 
identify themselves to one another?
15
Forlorne frequently visits a remote Whelvian 
village. Why do the locals believe she keeps coming 
back?
16 Forelorne’s castle contains a secret room that only 
she can access. What does she hide there?
17 What heinous acts has Forlorne imposed upon her 
citizens since becoming queen?
18
What distinct markings, patterns, or features do 
Forlorne’s weredrakes bear once she transforms 
them?
19
What unique magical item is Forlorne rumored to 
seek? What are people afraid she’ll do if she gets 
it?
20
The queen recently lost a close member of her 
royal court. Who were they, and how has she 
responded to their death?
21 What defensive systems are rumored to protect 
the fortress of Nwarlyth where Forlorne resides?
22
Who is the queen’s most recently appointed royal 
advisor, and what aspect of the kingdom are they 
responsible for?
23
The queen has a childhood friend who’s now a 
member of her court. Who are they, and what’s 
strange about them?
24
The queen’s favorite weapon once belonged 
to someone else. What is it, and who owned it 
previously?
25 Which Whelvian hero vocally supports Forlorne? 
How does she reward them?
Roll Prompt
26
What inoffensive item, symbol, or practice did 
Forlorne ban when she came to power? What is 
the punishment for those who are caught violating 
the ban?
27 What is the Whelvians’ nickname for Forlorne, and 
where did it come from?
28 What creature does the queen keep chained in her 
dungeon, and why does she keep it alive?
29
One member of Forlorne’s royal court is treated 
differently from the others. Why is that, and how 
do the other nobles feel about it?
30
Forlorne sometimes disappears from Whelvia for 
short stretches of time. Where do people say she 
goes?
31
Forlorne recently destroyed something vital to 
many Whelvians. What happened, and who has 
been most affected?
32
What act of kindness did Forlorne recently 
commit? What controversy has come about 
because of it?
33
A foreign leader recently asked Forlorne for her 
hand in marriage, hoping to forge a powerful 
alliance. Who were they, and how did Forlorne 
respond?
34
Forlorne holds an annual celebration in her own 
honor. What does she require from citizens during 
the event, and how are they punished if they refuse 
to comply?
35 What symbol do Whelvian rebels use to identify 
themselves to one another?
36
Forlorne is demonizing a specific community, 
blaming them for Whelvia’s problems. Which 
community is it, and what has she tasked her 
knights to do in response?
37
Forlorne elevated someone to power who was an 
unexpected choice. Who are they, and why do 
people believe she chose them?
38
Forlorne recently killed a sky seer. What did this 
person see in the night sky that caused her to kill 
them?
39 What scandalous story is everyone telling from 
Forlorne’s last grand ball?
40 What trait or behavior of others brings out 
Forlorne’s cruelty?
41 Someone saved the queen’s life. Who were they, 
and what happened?
42
What promises has Forlorne made to the people of 
Whelvia, and what, if any, action has she taken to 
fulfill them?
43 Which criminal organization handles clandestine 
tasks for the queen?

===== PAGE 138 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
Roll Prompt
44 What specific claims does the queen’s most recent 
propaganda campaign make to her people?
45
Forlorne meets her sky seers in a very specific 
place. Where do they go, and why does she bring 
them there?
46
The queen’s regime has taken many people from 
their homes. Why were these people chosen, and 
where does Forlorne send them?
47 What new process has Forlorne implemented to 
dispatch those she deems criminal in Whelvia?
48 What did Forlorne say at her last widely attended 
speech? How did the audience react?
49 What is the queen’s rumored vice, and where in 
Whelvia does she go to satisfy it?
The Knights of Whelvia
There are typically around sixteen Knights of Whelvia and, 
under the queen’s rule, each leads a militia that presides 
over a sector of land to enforce Forlorne’s will. In the past 
they protected Whelvia from outside threats, stopped violent 
crime, and even built infrastructure and collaborated with 
citizens to bolster the well-being of all. Turning their swords 
against unarmed Whelvians and peaceful foreigners violates 
the very ideals of the order itself. Now they find themselves 
torn between duty to the realm and duty to their sovereign.
Knights of Whelvia often justify their allegiance to Forlorne by 
making false claims about her intentions and blaming others 
for inciting her acts of terror, but at times they quietly lament 
that the order is a pale shadow of its former self, unequipped 
to deal with a threat as great as the weredragon.
Lady Lavender (she/her), a retired knight, is a noteworthy 
exception who cleaves to her oath even though she’s too old 
to challenge Forlorne openly. Whether the Knights of Whelvia 
as a whole can be redeemed is a question the PCs should ask 
throughout the campaign, with opportunities to form new 
opinions as they meet more members of this troubled order.
Schon
Schon (pronounced “shawn”; they/them) is a firbolg and highly 
skilled sky seer who has served under Forlorne since they were 
both members of the same stargazing cult. Schon was loyal 
to Forlorne and even aided her in her plot to absorb Sazanthe. 
But Schon’s loyalty turned to hatred the moment Forlorne’s 
ritual destroyed the abbey and killed many of the guests. 
Only then did Schon realize the depths of her selfishness and 
cruelty.
Nevertheless, Schon remained in Forlorne’s service as her 
most trusted advisor.
Schon knows Forlorne Lykona better than anyone and is the 
only person she trusts. But she doesn’t know the truth—that 
Schon spends every waking moment plotting her downfall. 
Armed with secrets, Schon can guide the heroes to Heaven’s 
Eye, the hidden observatory where a celestial being called the 
Astromancer awaits them (see “The Astromancer” in the next 
section).
If the PCs are stuck or unsure about how to proceed, have 
Schon relay an anonymous message to them via Astrid, their 
seagull familiar. Schon can suggest a course of action to guide 
the characters and story in a new direction.
The Ascendants
The Ascendants are a collection of Faint Divinities who were 
once mortals but ascended upon their death, becoming 
celestial constellations above Whelvia. The writings of the 
Elder Druids declared that the most powerful, revered, and 
worshipped creatures are worthy of ascension. While mythical 
beings such as dragons, and in particular the dragon wardens, 
often fit these qualifications, anyone deemed sufficiently 
worthy of apotheosis can become an Ascendant.
BELIEF IN THE STARS
While belief in the constellations’ ability to impart guidance 
from the Ascendants has diminished over the many centuries 
since Whelvarax’s reign, citizens across the nation still 
incorporate the night sky into their lives. Most Whelvians 
engage with the stars on a purely cultural level: celebrating 
holidays dedicated to past Ascendants or incorporating 
celestial motifs into their clothing and home, but not 
connecting on a spiritual level. Others follow the various 
religious practices that Whelvians have established around 
Ascendants or visit local sky seers for inspiration or solace 
during important life events. Only the most devout—many of 
them professional or amateur sky seers themselves—believe 
in the stars’ ability to impart destiny or foresee the future.
Work with your players to craft the various rituals and 
customs that Whelvians practice in relation to the Ascendants 
and the stars. Which aspects are only for the devoutly 
religious? Which customs are open to all?
THE ASTROMANCER
There exists a reality in which Forlorne Lykona becomes 
an Ascendant. The Astromancer (she/her) is an immortal, 
celestial creature from that reality—more precisely, the 
Astromancer is Forlorne in an enlightened state of being.
In a possible future—after she accomplishes her goal 
of ascension to become a Faint Divinity—Forlorne’s 
enlightenment opens her eyes to the extent of the horrors she 
committed in the Mortal Realm to gain power. In an effort to 
counteract the tragedies she caused, the Astromancer found a 
way to return to Whelvia in the past and orchestrate her own 
doom. If Forlorne dies, the Astromancer ceases to exist.
Because they are one and the same, Forlorne is unable to 
perceive the Astromancer in any way, shape, or form. 
137Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 139 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
Moreover, the Astromancer is dedicated to remaining 
nonviolent in her immortal state and will not directly harm 
anyone.
The Astromancer appears as a luminous, masked being 
who dwells in a magical observatory called Heaven’s Eye, 
where she waits for the PCs. When the heroes arrive, the 
Astromancer performs a reading of the night sky, drawing 
the party’s attention to prominent constellations in an effort 
to arm them with information they can use to orchestrate 
Forlorne’s downfall (see more about this in the upcoming 
“Campaign Mechanics” section).
The Land of Whelvia
The map of Whelvia (available in the appendix) includes 
marked locations that are briefly described in this section, as 
well as locations without descriptions. You and your players 
should define these undescribed locations, alongside any new 
locations you desire, during your session zero.
As the PCs visit each key location, you can use the information 
below as a starting point, but allow players to describe these 
places as well. When in doubt, you can ask things like:
• Has your character been here before? If so, what’s one 
thing they remember from their last visit? If not, what’s 
one rumor your character has heard about the location?
• Which prominent or familiar figure residing here does your 
character know? What’s noteworthy about this person?
• What unique flora or fauna is this place best known for? 
How does it affect the people who live here?
KEY LOCATIONS
Some of the key locations for a Reign of the Weredragon 
campaign are detailed below.
Froghaven Bog is the swamp that holds what’s left of 
Forlorne’s stargazing cult. It’s rumored that the inside of 
their headquarters, the Dragonmyrth mansion, has become 
its own pocket dimension, a warped place of endless 
hallways, upside-down rooms, and violent cult members. 
What saying or tenet is the cult best known for? Who from 
the cult has spoken out against Forlorne?
Heaven’s Eye is a magically hidden observatory. Inside 
dwells a mysterious celestial being called the Astromancer 
(see “The Astromancer” on page 137). What unique 
architectural feature does Heaven’s Eye have? In what way 
does the Astromancer’s presence affect this place?
Knightstead is the ancestral home of a retired human 
Knight of Whelvia named Lady Lavender, who lives there 
with her two pet hounds. Although she’s too old to swing 
a sword, Lady Lavender aims to redeem the Knights 
of Whelvia by helping all who oppose Forlorne. How is 
the legacy of Lady Lavender memorialized prominently in 
Whelvia? What are the knights who follow Forlorne doing to 
stamp out that legacy?
Nwarlyth is the towering fortress of Forlorne Lykona. 
It houses the magical tapestry that sustains the wards 
protecting Whelvia’s borders, as well as the statues of 
enemies Forlorne has magically turned to stone. What 
prominent figure has recently disappeared while visiting 
Nwarlyth? Who or what stands in defense of the fortress?
Vexstone Abbey is a clifftop ruin haunted by the spirits 
of those killed by the ritual in which Forlorne absorbed 
Sazanthe. These spirits are trapped inside mundane 
objects and can animate them. Rough-hewn tunnels 
connect the abbey’s vaults and cellars to sea caves where 
local pirates conduct business with Forlorne’s underlings. 
How do the ghosts here react to people passing through? 
What wards or charms do the pirates carry to protect them 
from the supernatural forces they often encounter?
Witch’s End is a gloomy grove that contains the charred 
remnants of a cottage that was once Forlorne Lykona’s 
childhood home. Forlorne’s eleven-year-old twin sister, 
Harrowyrr, haunts the grove as an animate skeleton 
wrapped in an ashen shroud. What does Harrowyrr want 
from those who pass through the grove? How has the legacy 
of Forlorne affected the people who still live here?
ADDITIONAL LOCATIONS
The list below contains other locations that can show up 
during the Night Sky Reading (see “Campaign Mechanics”), 
so they’re important to add to the map. As you do, invite your 
players to provide details about each place. If these locations 
play a role in your campaign, build on your players’ choices.
Stillwater Lake, Dryad’s Graveyard, The Whelvewood, 
Elevarne, Starlance Grove, and Nightshade
These are marked with an asterisk on the campaign map’s list 
of locations as a reminder of their importance.
Border Wards
Forlorne Lykona uses magical wards to discourage foreign 
invaders and prevent terrified subjects from fleeing the realm. 
Burning the magical tapestry housed in Forlorne’s fortress, 
Nwarlyth, dispels these wards.
LAND WARD
This ward activates whenever a creature or vehicle tries to 
cross Whelvia’s border by an overland route without Forlorne’s 
permission. When the ward is activated, stony vines erupt 
from the ground, grab the creature or vehicle, drag it down 
into the earth, and entomb it.
OCEAN WARD
Whelvia’s ocean ward destroys any boat that isn’t flying either 
the new Whelvian flag or the flag of the Crimson Trident, a 
consortium that facilitates trade between coastal nations, by 
using watery tentacles to grab the boat and drag it down into 
the depths of the sea.
138 Chapter 4: Reign of the Weredragon


===== PAGE 140 =====
REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
THE INCITING INCIDENT
Use the prompt below to start your campaign, or create your own.
While staying at the Red Leaf Inn, a small establishment 
within the Whelvewood, the party’s breakfast is interrupted 
by an insistent seagull that squawks in their faces before 
disappearing and reappearing in a cloud of glittering blue 
smoke only to squawk again.
The seagull eventually drops a scroll of parchment. Unrolling 
it reveals a map with a route drawn from the inn through 
the Whelvewood to a mysterious location labeled “Heaven’s 
Eye.” Troublingly, the Whelvewood has long been the site of 
skirmishes between Forlorne’s forces and rebels who oppose 
the new queen. Many of the Knights of Whelvia have sent their 
militias to the Whelvewood in an attempt to take the forest 
and establish a large encampment. Meanwhile, rebel forces 
and locals are trying to drive out the tyrant’s soldiers and 
protect the people and creatures living among the trees. Any 
stranger passing through is likely to be targeted by both the 
militias and freedom fighters, as no one knows whom to trust 
and neither side can afford to put faith in the wrong people.
On the backside of the map is the drawing of a rabbit’s 
silhouette and a note that reads:
Find the place where the trees cross
Show this to the Foes of the Fox for safe passage through the 
warren
There will be a price
After delivering the scroll, the seagull steals part of someone’s 
breakfast, squawks loudly, and disappears in its usual smoky 
fashion.
What dangers will the party encounter on the way to Heaven’s 
Eye? Do they trust the seagull and whoever sent the map, or 
are they wary of the mysterious delivery? Will they attempt 
to collaborate with the militias or the rebels to traverse the 
Whelvewood without running afoul of the other group? 
What will they discover in “the warren,” and what price 
are they prepared to pay for safe passage?
FOR THE GM
The seagull was sent by Schon, Forlorne’s right hand 
who’s secretly orchestrating her downfall. Schon, a highly 
competent sky seer, received a message from the stars to 
send the party to Heaven’s Eye. Though they don’t know 
the significance of the location, they do know how to get 
there and that the party will play a prominent role in the fall 
of Forlorne Lykona. Schon’s seagull can reappear to help 
the party if they are lost during their journey.
