REign OF THE  W ERE d RAg O n
CAMPAIGN MECHANICS
The following mechanics are unique to this campaign.
Curses
A creature afflicted with a magical curse gains a special 
condition (see “Conditions” on page 102 of the core rulebook). 
The following two features inflict curses that are common in 
this campaign, but you can also create your own:
• Forlorne’s “Breath Weapon,” which turns people into 
weredrakes, known as the Weredrake Curse.
• Forlorne’s “Stone-Cold Gaze,” which turns creatures and 
their equipment to stone, known as the Petrified Curse.
Any Cursed condition can be cleared only by magic from a 
spell, ritual, magic item, location, supernatural occurrence, 
higher power, or some combination of these options. Two 
examples of curse-ending items—Lady Lavender’s Longsword 
and the Zoetrope of the Bright Bacchanal—are described in 
the upcoming “Keys to Victory” section.
Discovering how to end a curse often requires research, 
which might prompt the PCs to undergo a quest for additional 
information or help from an NPC.
The Astromancer’s Revelations
From the top of the Heaven’s Eye observatory, the 
Astromancer gazes into the night sky. The reading she 
performs for the PCs unveils five revelations. These are 
determined by the players collectively rolling 5d6. Match the 
results on each die to the corresponding d6 row in the Night 
Sky Reading table. Then, assign one die to each revelation 
column. You can have only one die in each column, but if 
the players roll multiples of a number, there might be more 
than one in a row. For example, if the players roll two 3s, you 
might choose to put one in the Key to Victory 2 column and 
one in the Showdown Location column. The result would be 
“Lady Lavender’s Longsword” and “Nwarlyth” respectively. 
Be sure to spread out where the PCs have to go when making 
your selections. For example, avoid having more than one 
revelation occur in Elevarne or Nightshade.
Tip: You can always replace any table entry with different 
information after your session zero, especially if you want to 
use an interesting location, NPC, treasure, or piece of lore 
created by the PCs.
First Revelation (Secret’s Location): Use the Secret’s 
Location column of the Night Sky Reading table to 
determine where the PCs must go to learn a secret that 
could help them defeat Forlorne. Give each player a piece 
of paper, then ask them to concoct one secret Forlorne is 
hiding, either about her history or current rule, and write 
it down without telling the other players what it is. Gather 
up these papers and, between sessions, choose one of the 
ideas from the PCs to inspire the secret they’ll uncover, and 
use that secret to prepare the adventure they’ll go through 
to find it.
Second Revelation (Powerful Ally): Use the Powerful Ally 
column of the Night Sky Reading table to identify one ally 
who can help the PCs defeat Forlorne. This ally has a pool 
of 3 Hope at the start of any scene that includes Forlorne 
as an adversary. A PC within Far range of the ally can spend 
a Hope from this pool as if it were their own. If the ally falls 
unconscious or dies, this feature can’t be used.
Third Revelation (Key to Victory 1): Use the Key to Victory 
1 column of the Night Sky Reading table to identify an item 
that provides a defensive benefit that can help one or more 
PCs in a battle against Forlorne. The item’s location is also 
disclosed. When Forlorne appears, a PC holding at least 
one of these items gains 1d4 Hope. An item marked with 
an asterisk is described in the “Keys to Victory” section.
Fourth Revelation (Key to Victory 2): Use the Key to 
Victory 2 column of the Night Sky Reading table to identify 
a weapon that can cause harm to Forlorne. A PC wielding 
this weapon gains a +1 bonus to their Proficiency for 
attacks against Forlorne. An item marked with an asterisk 
is described in the “Keys to Victory” section.
Fifth Revelation (Showdown Location): Use the Showdown 
Location column of the Night Sky Reading table to identify 
where the PCs are most likely to face Forlorne in their final 
battle. If the PCs arrive at this location with knowledge 
of the secret from the first revelation, the ally from the 
second revelation, and the items from the third and fourth 
revelations, Forlorne appears alone and tries to destroy 
them. Two of Forlorne’s passive features—“Feed on 
Fear” and “T errifying”—aren’t available while she’s in this 
location.

Keys to Victory
Two items in the Night Sky Reading table have the power 
to end magical curses (see earlier “Curses” section) and are 
described in the sections that follow.
LADY LA VENDER’S LONGSWORD
Tier  Physical Weapon (Primary)
This blade’s hilt is shaped like two hounds leaping away from each 
other. The weapon belongs to Lady Lavender, a retired Knight of 
Whelvia who lives in Knightstead.
Trait: Agility | Range: Melee | Damage: d8+10 phy
Burden: Two-Handed
FEATURE
Curse Breaker: This weapon clears all magical curses on a creature it 
touches.
        
Evening the Odds
If the PCs heed the Astromancer’s night sky reading and 
obtain everything they need before reaching Tier 4, allow 
them to advance to at least level 8 before confronting the 
weredragon. You can introduce complications (such as new 
story arcs) that delay the confrontation with Forlorne, allowing 
the PCs to level up at a more natural pace.
Unique Adversaries
This section presents stat blocks for Forlorne Lykona and her 
weredrake creations.
Forlorne Lykona
Forlorne has multiple stat blocks. The first is a framework 
that’s used regardless of her form. She also has one for her 
Dragon Form and her Faun Form. She can shift between these 
using her “Transform” feature. Use the stat block relevant to 
her physical manifestation at any given moment.
FORLORNE L YKONA
Tier  Solo
Statuesque in her faun form, grotesquely beautiful in her purple-
scaled dragon form, the weredragon queen uses magic to defeat all 
who threaten her rule.
Motives & Tactics: Ascend to the stars, learn new magic, rule 
through terror, transform
HP: 11 | Stress: 8
Experience: Arcane Knowledge +5, Death from Above +5
FEATURES
Relentless (4) - Passive: Forlorne can be spotlighted up to four 
times per GM turn. Spend Fear as usual to spotlight her.
Transform - Action: Mark a Stress to have Forlorne shift between 
her Dragon Form and Faun Form. While she embodies a form, 
she uses its statistics and features.
        Feed on Fear - Reaction: When a PC within Close range fails with 
Fear, Forlorne can clear a HP or a Stress.
FAUN FORM
This form reflects Forlorne’s devilish charm, civility, and 
cunning. This is her original body and the shape she is most 
familiar with. She takes this form while overseeing the affairs 
of her realm, and it is the form the PCs are most likely to 
interact with outside combat.
FORLORNE’S FAUN FORM
Diffi   culty: 18 | Thresholds: 35/70
ATK: +6 | Eldritch Incantation: Far | 4d8+14 mag
FEATURES
Battle Teleport - Passive: Before or after making a standard attack, 
you can mark a Stress to have Forlorne teleport to a location 
within Far range.
Grasping Vines - Action: Spend a Fear to target a group within 
Far range. Each target must succeed on an Agility Reaction Roll 
or take 2d8+7 magic damage and become Restrained until they 
break free with a successful Strength Roll. The vines last until 
Forlorne takes Severe damage, uses this feature again, or ends 
the eff  ect.
Stone-Cold Gaze - Reaction: When Forlorne takes damage from 
an attack within Close range, you can spend 2 Fear to force 
the attacker to make an Instinct Reaction Roll. On a failure, 
the target must spend 3 Hope or gain the Petrifi  ed Curse. Until 
this condition is cleared, the target and the equipment they’re 
wearing or carrying turn to stone. While under the Petrifi  ed 
Curse, the target is Restrained and Vulnerable, can’t act, and 
gains a +10 bonus to their damage thresholds.
        
ZOETROPE OF THE 
BRIGHT BACCHANAL
Item
Unless you decide it was taken elsewhere, a magical trinket 
lies buried amid the ashes and timbers of the Lykona cottage 
at Witch’s End, undamaged by fi  re and the passage of time. 
Made of copper, the item is a hollow cylinder with dancing 
fauns cut out of it.
FEATURE
When you place a candle in this zoetrope’s cylinder and turn the 
metal key attached to the base counterclockwise, the cylinder 
rotates, casting a luminous parade of dancing fauns on nearby 
surfaces. While the lit zoetrope turns, it clears all magical curses on 
creatures within Close range.

DRAGON FORM
This form reflects Forlorne’s mercilessness and intolerance 
as she uses the shape of the once-peaceful dragon Sazanthe 
to drive fear into her people. While in dragon form, Forlorne 
is rapacious and terrifying, having cast off the trappings of 
civility and regality to indulge her primal cravings.
FORLORNE’S DRAGON FORM
Diffi   culty: 20 | Thresholds: 40/80
ATK: +8 | Claws and Teeth: Close | 4d10+10 phy
FEATURES
Flying - Passive: While fl  ying, Forlorne gains a +3 bonus to her 
Diffi   culty.
Terrifying - Passive: When Forlorne makes a successful attack, all 
PCs within Far range lose a Hope and you gain a Fear.
Breath Weapon - Action: Spend 2 Fear to have Forlorne exhale 
a cloud of magical energy, fi  lling an area within Close range. 
Each target in that area must make a Presence Reaction Roll. 
On a failure, they must mark 3 Stress or gain the Weredrake 
Curse. While under the Weredrake Curse, the target drops their 
equipment and loot and transforms into a Weredrake adversary 
under the GM’s control. After this condition is cleared, the target 
retains no memory of their time as a weredrake.
Tail Swipe - Reaction: When Forlorne marks 1 or more HP from an 
attack within Very Close range, you can mark a Stress to deal 
2d10+10 physical damage to all creatures within that range.
        
Weredrake
A weredrake is what a PC or an NPC becomes after being 
transformed by Forlorne’s “Breath Weapon.”
A weredrake looks like a wingless, quadrupedal dragon of 
a similar size to the person they once were, but they can’t 
speak and don’t retain that person’s knowledge or memories. 
Elements of the weredrake’s former ancestry might be 
discernible in the creature’s form; for example, a clank 
transformed into a weredrake might retain a mechanical 
appearance, while a faerie weredrake might have a carapace 
instead of scales. These creatures may have other unique 
aspects of their appearance that you can create with your 
players or craft yourself.
A weredrake can assume their original ancestry’s form. When 
they do, they retain the terrifying claws and teeth of their new 
draconic form. They typically use this form as a ruse to lure 
prey close before attacking.
As the players move up in tier, be sure to adjust the 
weredrake’s statistics accordingly. For more guidance, you can 
use the “Improvising Adversaries” section on page 208 of the 
core rulebook.
WEREDRAKE
Tier  Standard
A person transformed into a wingless draconic predator by a 
weredragon’s breath weapon.
Motives & Tactics: Hunger for fl  esh, hunt in small packs
Diffi   culty: 12 | Thresholds: 8/14 | HP: 5 | Stress: 2
ATK: +2 | Claws & Teeth: Melee | 1d8+3 phy
Experience: Pack Hunter +3
FEATURES
Climber - Passive: The Weredrake climbs as easily as it runs.
Pack Tactics - Passive: If the Weredrake makes a successful 
standard attack and another Weredrake is within Melee range of 
the target, deal 1d8+6 physical damage instead of its standard 
damage and gain a Fear.
Innocent Guise - Action: The Weredrake magically assumes the 
form of the person it was before its transformation but retains 
its claws and teeth. This form lasts until it dies or uses this 
feature again to revert to its draconic form.
        
SESSION ZERO QUESTIONS
Ask your players any of these questions, or make your own.
• When was the first time your character came into direct 
contact with Forlorne Lykona? What about that interaction 
will your character never forget?
• A place your character loved has been dramatically 
changed since Forlorne took power. What was it like 
before, and how is it different now?
• Who’s one person your character knows who was 
convicted of crimes against the queen? What was the 
accusation, and where are they now?
• What portion of Whelvia is unaffected by the political 
turmoil plaguing the country? What theories do people 
have about why?
• Your character once met a Knight of Whelvia. What was 
their name, and how did that interaction change the way 
your character treats the knights now?

dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
dAR k HEAR T OF A ndA lUR i A
As the nights grow long in a land of rich wine and ancient superstitions, a band of monster 
hunters battles the creatures that stalk the shadows.
Designed by Carlos Cisco, Rue Dickey, Rowan Hall, and Rob Hebert
Complexity Rating: •••
THE PITCH
Read this section to your players to introduce them to the 
campaign.
Andaluria is a land of sweeping vineyards where beauty and 
dread intertwine. By day, the streets and valleys are alive with 
festivals, music, and the scent of wine—but when the sun 
sets, monsters come out to feast. For generations, the old 
superstitions and folk rites kept the beasts at bay, but of late 
the nights have grown unnaturally long, and the citizens of 
Andaluria live in fear of increasingly terrifying monsters.
In a Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign, you’ll play monster 
hunters who must investigate horrific crimes, navigate 
political strife, and slay supernatural creatures to protect the 
people from eternal darkness.
TONE & FEEL
Gothic, Grim, Heroic, Intriguing, Monstrous, Mysterious, 
Romantic, Spooky, Thrilling
THEMES
Battle Not with Monsters Lest Ye Become One, Dangerous 
Secrets, Driving Back the Darkness, Faith & Tradition, The 
Nature of Evil, Power & Politics
TOUCHSTONES
Bloodborne, Castlevania, Curse of Strahd, Hellboy, John 
Constantine: Hellblazer, Monster of the Week, Night’s Black 
Agents, Slayers, Supernatural, Vaesen, Vampire Hunter D, 
Van Helsing, The Witcher, World of Darkness
144 Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria


===== PAGE 146 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
OVERVIEW
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players the following information before character creation.
A land of sirocco winds, volcanic soil, and simmering intrigue, 
Andaluria is a region forged by rebellion. One hundred 
years ago, its people rose up against the tyrannical Emperor 
Arcedrio (he/him) and cast off the yoke of imperial control. 
T oday, Andaluria appears to thrive due to its lush vineyards, 
its lucrative harbor trade, and the canny administration of 
its Council of Five. Yet beneath the surface, ancient rituals, 
secret bargains, and monstrous hungers still shape the fate of 
its people.
Andaluria’s landscape, like its history, was sculpted by 
shadow and flame. The Serracorte, a jagged chain of dormant 
volcanoes, dominates the north—their obsidian slopes, ideal 
for growing grapes, rise into scrubland and thin pasture 
where hardy shepherds and wild creatures make their homes. 
These mountains shelter Andaluria from the harsher climates 
beyond, giving the region its bright, blazing days and dark, 
sultry nights. The Valmorria River flows from the mountain 
peaks through fertile valleys tangled with grapevines before 
emptying into a deep southern bay where the capital city of 
Jesaña commands the coast, and locals celebrate the long, hot 
summers with music, dancing, and drink.
But the valleys and vineyards of Andaluria also bear more 
sinister fruit: since time immemorial, bloodthirsty beasts—
ghosts, ghouls, and creatures that defy description—have 
emerged from the shadows to feast on the people’s fear. For 
generations, Andalurians defended themselves from these 
creatures with the twin weapons of faith and folklore: the 
sacred rites of the Church of the Golden Eminence and the 
practical magic of the nomadic Caio people. These rituals and 
wards—holy symbols in the shape of the sun, charms of ash 
and bone, and offerings to unseen spirits—were once all the 
protection people needed. Of late, however, these rites and 
relics have lost their bite.
For a century, Andaluria has celebrated its independence with 
festivals of wine and fire, culminating on the summer solstice. 
But this year, the nights have grown mysteriously longer—
and more dangerous—with each sunset. Now, the moon 
lingers overhead unnaturally, monsters prowl the fields where 
revelers once danced, and the wealthy elite hide from the 
horror in masquerades at the hilltop castillo of the mysterious 
Condesa Ysabela Delamoña IV (she/her). The locals whisper 
that death has come to Andaluria, and they fear that, at 
this year’s Midsummer Festival, the streets of Jesaña will 
run red with more than wine. What was once joyous is now 
desperate—celebration turned into apprehension, laughter 
masking dread, and every shadow hiding fangs.
In this climate of fear, the polemical Archbishop Veyra (he/
him) has risen to power by preaching a new church orthodoxy, 
one uncorrupted by “Caio heresy.” As the people’s faith in 
the old ways falters, Veyra’s fiery sermons find purchase and 
threaten to inaugurate a new age of zealotry.
But not everyone has responded to the night’s encroachment 
with trepidation and obedience: many brave Andalurians have 
taken up the mantle of “monster hunter,” defending their 
villages with blade, flame, and forbidden lore when amulets 
and incantations aren’t enough. Across the countryside, 
clandestine cells of the Sombral Spade, a secret order of 
monster slayers led by the enigmatic Julio Cantafierro (he/
him), have arisen to face down the dark. T o the people, these 
hunters are heroes. T o the church, they are heretics. T o those 
in power, they are a dangerous necessity.
You are a band of monster hunters, brought together to battle 
the night. Can you defeat the darkness, or will you become 
what you wish to destroy?
145
Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria

===== PAGE 147 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
COMMUNITIES
All communities are available, but some have unique aspects in a Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign. As needed, provide the 
following information to your players and choose one or more of the questions to ask them during your session zero.
Freeborne
The uprising that deposed the emperor was a formative event 
in the history of Andaluria, and many communities define 
themselves by their contributions to the rebellion. Members of 
these communities strongly distrust anything connected to the 
emperor—and eye most nobles with suspicion.
• Your family lore tells of a relative who acted heroically 
during the rebellion against the emperor. What did they do 
that fills you with pride, and how has it shaped your idea of 
what a “hero” really is?
• You’ve heard rumors that an influential figure has been 
operating behind the scenes to reestablish the empire and 
take the crown for themself. Who are they, and from what 
institution do they draw their power?
• You have a small, private act of rebellion you regularly 
perform to remind you of your freedom. What is it, and 
how long have you been doing it?
Hearthborne
Away from the petty politics of the capital, many Andalurians 
lead steady, wholesome lives laboring in the fields or farming 
the upper reaches of the Serracorte. In light of the recent 
attacks, many hearthborne communities have become more 
wary of outsiders than they once were.
• While most of your community’s important celebrations 
are marked by joy and exuberance, one ritual is distinctly 
somber and reserved. What is it, and why is it so 
meaningful to you?
• Someone in your community disappeared without a trace. 
What do people say happened to them?
• You carry with you something that reminds you of home 
and hearth, bringing comfort and banishing fear in time of 
need. What is it?
Highborne
In Andaluria, social classes are starkly divided. The highest 
social sphere of Jesaña is occupied by the ruling council and 
their families, plus any nobles within their orbit. There are 
also influential socialites and political elites from wealthy 
merchant families who trade an assortment of goods, such as 
the region’s famous wine, to foreign lands for gold, silk, and 
other luxuries. Outside the city, wealth is primarily measured 
in land, with the richest families owning vineyards on the most 
productive tracts in the Serracorte foothills.
• What is your community’s most prized possession, and 
what has been sacrificed to keep it?
• How did your family earn its wealth or reputation? Does it 
fill you with pride or shame?
• You harbor a dark secret that could ruin your family. What 
is it, and what could convince you to reveal it to outsiders?
Loreborne
Andaluria is a land where secrets abound and hidden 
knowledge is a powerful weapon against monsters both 
magical and mundane. A loreborne community can be 
centered around chronicling the region’s dark past, cataloging 
the sins of powerful figures, or wielding esoteric lore against 
the creatures of the night.
• What rumor have you heard about the resurgence of 
monsters in Andaluria, and how do you plan to test it?
• You have been tasked with finding hidden information 
about an important figure. Who is your target, and why 
would you risk everything to learn this knowledge?
• Your community has a specific code they use to test one 
another before sharing information. What is it, and why is 
it used?
Orderborne
Many orderborne characters are adherents—or even clergy 
members—of the Church of the Golden Eminence, the 
most strictly hierarchical institution in Andaluria. The church 
has waned in popularity in recent decades, but the uptick 
in monster activity is calling many lost members back into 
the fold—and Archbishop Veyra’s harsh ideology. This has 
caused a rift between those who value tradition and those who 
champion reform.
• You practice a daily ritual that outsiders view as vapid or 
nonsensical but you believe is essential. What is it?
• Your faith was once shaken when you witnessed another 
member of your community break one of its tenets without 
consequence. Why do you think they got away with it?
• You care very much about someone who doesn’t share 
your beliefs. Who are they, and what punishment are you 
terrified will befall them?
Wanderborne
The Caio are the most prominent wanderborne community 
in Andaluria. Many caravan throughout the region as the 
seasons change and their herds cycle between grazing 
patches. Wanderborne characters can also come from outside 
Andaluria—many are drawn to the area by the promise of 
seasonal work in the fields or at the city docks.
• What one item do you never begin a journey without?
• You came to Andaluria from a far-off place. Why did you 
leave, and what drew you here?
• Which waypoint on your annual circuit do you always wish 
your people could linger at?
146 Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria


===== PAGE 148 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
ANCESTRIES
All ancestries are available, but some have unique aspects in 
a Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign. As needed, provide the 
following information to your players.
Aetheris & Infernis
In Andaluria, aetheris and infernis can be born to any family 
regardless of their parents’ ancestry. Many church adherents 
believe the birth of an aetheris is a divine blessing and often 
recruit or exalt the families into which they are born. On the 
other hand, some church zealots view infernis as ill omens 
or punishments for sin and ostracize their birth families as a 
result.
Clanks
The first clanks in Andaluria were humble creations—wood 
and iron machines built to toil in vineyards, sort grapes, make 
bottles, and craft wine. T oday, the noble houses commission 
exquisite models crafted from porcelain or brass with 
gemstone inlays, flaunting their wealth in mechanical form. 
Whether a vineyard relic or a jeweled servant, a clank in 
Andaluria carries the signs of their maker’s intended purpose.
Elves
Elves of Andaluria embody the land’s flora in their mystic 
form, recalling gnarled grapevines, hardy shrubs, and pale ash 
trees that cling to volcanic soil. In them, the land seems to find 
its voice: resilient, beautiful, and a little uncanny.
CLASSES
All classes are available, but some have unique aspects in a 
Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign. As needed, provide the 
following information to your players.
Bards
Bards are beloved in Andaluria as the keepers of songs, 
tales, and folk wisdom—the knowledge of which can help 
people protect themselves from terrors of the dark. Their 
music fills festivals, their poetry preserves the history of 
rebellion, and their stories remind the people who they are. In 
Jesaña, wealthy patrons shower bards with riches to keep the 
revels—and their reputations—alive. In less-wealthy caravans 
or villages, bards may be offered food, drink, comfortable 
lodging, and other necessities or niceties in exchange for their 
expertise.
• What song, tale, or superstition do you never tire of 
sharing?
• You know one song or story you never share publicly. What 
is it, and why do you keep it secret?
Guardians & Rangers
The first monster hunters in Andaluria were rangers, trained 
to track and kill the unnatural. Many dedicate themselves to 
a single type of quarry—vampires, werewolves, or another 
creature of the night—as one might devote themselves to 
the hunt of elk or boar. Guardians stand beside them as the 
hammer to their spear, wielding specialized arms and brutal 
methods against creatures that don’t die easily. T ogether, they 
form the backbone of Andaluria’s monster-hunting tradition.
• What creature of the night are you sworn to destroy—and 
why?
• Someone’s face, twisted in terror, is seared into your 
memory. Who is it, and why can’t you forget this moment?
Seraphs
Most Andalurian seraphs channel the radiant essence of the 
Golden Eminence. If you decide to play a seraph, think about 
how your character views the institution of the church—or its 
current leadership.
• Your display of power once converted a group of people to 
the Church of the Golden Eminence. What did you do, and 
who did you inspire to believe?
• You harbor a secret doubt about your professed faith. 
What is it, and what do you believe will happen if it’s 
revealed?
Warlocks & Witches
Attitudes toward warlocks and witches vary. According 
to official church canon, pacts and relationships with 
otherworldly entities are sinful by their very nature. But to 
many Andalurians, consorting with such beings is a matter of 
survival. As a result, warlocks and witches who live or work 
in areas dominated by the church often do so in secret or risk 
social persecution, while those who live where folk traditions 
prevail often enjoy increased status and respect.
• Someone you love who believes in the power of the Golden 
Eminence once turned against you. Who was it, and what 
happened?
• You use your magic to perform a service for members of 
your community. What do you do, and who taught you this 
skill?
147Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria


===== PAGE 149 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
PLA YER PRINCIPLES
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players 
the following information before character creation.
Be the Hero
Ultimately, it’s up to you to protect the innocent and defeat 
evil. While there are others who share your mission, they have 
their own mysteries to solve and monsters to defeat. You can’t 
rely on them to do the work, so get your hands dirty.
Confront Darkness in Whatever Form It Takes
Evil often arises in the form of vicious, supernatural creatures 
stalking the shadows, but it also manifests in the hearts 
of people corrupted by hate, greed, or desire. Hunt down 
darkness of every kind, mortal and monster alike.
Embrace the Unknown
Your greatest weapons in this battle will often be ancient 
legends, strange superstitions, dark omens, and esoteric 
rituals. Seek out odd truths and dare to walk into places 
others fear to tread—you never know what you’ll find in the 
dark.
Be Proactive
Once your party picks up a case, it’s up to you to pound the 
pavement, dig up dirt, and run down leads. If you ever get 
stuck, think of all the ways you can find information: searching 
for evidence, interviewing witnesses, gathering rumors, using 
magic rituals, running experiments, or seeking out experts. 
Don’t wait for the action to come to you; chase it down.
Put Your Heads Together
A successful hunt requires your party to acquire scattered 
bits of information and then piece it together to learn what 
you’re up against, where to find it, and how to defeat it. Once 
you’ve gathered several clues, regroup and compare notes. 
Conjecture, guess, and think outside the box. Brainstorm how 
the clues you’ve uncovered might fit together, then figure out 
how to test your theory.
GM PRINCIPLES
Keep the following guidance in mind while you GM this 
campaign.
Portray a Vibrant World Twisted by Shadows
Andaluria should feel rich, decadent, and dangerous. Offer 
players moments of revelry and beauty—festivals, feasts, and 
wine—then twist them into something dark and unsettling. 
Show your players the extravagance of the upper class and the 
heavy toll it takes on those who labor for them.
Feed the Characters’ Fears
Lean into uncertainty. Show the PCs shadows moving just out 
of sight, give weight to every half-heard superstition, endanger 
those they love, and remind them that the night in Andaluria is 
never safe.
Keep Monsters Mysterious
As the PCs uncover their quarry’s hidden patterns and 
weaknesses, don’t let their understanding devolve into 
a mechanistic model for how all supernatural entities 
behave. Keep the creatures the party encounters eerie by 
revealing strange or unexpected powers and vulnerabilities 
in individuals or groups. Challenge the players’ assumptions 
about why certain rites and weapons work while others don’t.
Show and Tell
Give clues freely. If the players get stuck, show them what 
they’ve missed or remind them of what they’ve forgotten. 
Point them toward solid lines of investigation that lead to 
obvious conclusions. Don’t try to trick your players or hide the 
ball.
Let the Players Be Correct
When the players land on a reasonable solution to a mystery 
or a convincing method of dispatching the monster, let their 
solution be the correct one even if it doesn’t match what you 
had planned. It’s not reasonable to expect your players to 
read your mind—remember the core GM principles to “hold 
on gently” and “be fans of the players.” Move things around 
behind the scenes to support the party’s conclusions and 
plans.
Tempt the Players with Dark Bargains
Power, forbidden knowledge, and survival often come at a 
cost. Present alluring “deals with the devil” that force players 
to grapple with the same choices that corrupted Andaluria 
long ago.

 Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria

DISTINCTIONS
Use this information to prepare your campaign. You can also share it with your players as needed.
The Summer of Shadows
Andaluria’s calendar is filled to bursting with feast days and 
festivals. The biggest and most important of these fiestas is 
the Midsummer Festival, which celebrates both the summer 
solstice (the shortest night of the year) and the anniversary 
of the uprising that won Andaluria its freedom. This year, 
however, the nights linger unnaturally, each one stretching 
longer than the last, and people from every echelon of society 
wonder aloud what this mysterious phenomenon heralds.
MONSTERS IN ANDALURIA
Werewolves, demons, and a thousand other creatures of 
the night have always stalked the shadows of Andaluria. In 
the past, sacred rituals and folk magic protected people 
from these mysterious beings, but as the nights have grown 
longer, new and more powerful monsters have appeared, 
undeterred by the practices of the past, and each horror must 
be investigated, understood, and slain through courage and 
ingenuity.
THE SOMBRAL SPADE
In the past, when lord and church refused to defend the 
villages, the people defended themselves. T oday, the Sombral 
Spade, an illustrious order of monster hunters, has reignited 
this tradition. Their leader, Julio Cantafierro, is secretly 
cursed with lycanthropy, an affliction held at bay only by a 
Caio concoction made with special herbs foraged from the 
highest reaches of the Serracorte. Now, the Spade operates 
a clandestine network of cells dedicated to eradicating 
monsters throughout the region—beholden not to the council 
or the church, but the people of Andaluria.
A DEMONIC LEGACY
The recent eruption of monstrous activity within Andaluria 
is no accident. Unbeknownst to most locals, the volcanoes 
of the Serracorte Mountains conceal a portal to the Circles 
Below. A century ago, Emperor Arcedrio used this “hellgate” 
to gain otherworldly power. Though the portal was sealed with 
Arcedrio’s death, it remains to this day, waiting to be located 
and reopened by someone hungry for power and heedless of 
danger. Enter Archbishop Mateo Veyra, a powerful patriarch 
of the Church of the Golden Eminence.
Driven by a desire to restore the waning influence of his 
church and enhance his own status, Veyra consulted the 
apocryphal texts hidden deep within his basilica’s library, 
stole the fallen emperor’s blackened heart from the Condesa’s 
estate, and used it to unleash hell on Andaluria. As Veyra 
draws dark energy from the Circles Below, the night lingers 
unnaturally and the fires beneath the soil of the Serracorte 
flare back to life. Now Veyra preaches penitence and 
obedience to the church, proving the power of his god by 
banishing the very creatures he summons.
The Caio People
Highly influential in political, economic, and social spheres, 
the Caio people have a storied history in Andaluria. Their 
culture is steeped in an ever-evolving oral tradition of folktales 
and fables, passed down through innumerable generations.
Heavily persecuted by the emperor as ready scapegoats in his 
play for power, the Caio were forced into hiding at the height 
of his reign. From these isolated communities rose a group 
of hardened, hopeful adventurers who toppled the tyrant. 
Those heroes established powerful dynasties in Jesaña and 
shepherded in a new age of art, music, and culture.
The Caio people use familial terms loosely and fondly, with 
no special status afforded to blood connection. Many Caio 
were originally wanderers or outcasts who came to Andaluria 
in search of community. Anyone with a willingness to learn 
Caio customs—and share their own—is welcomed into the 
fold, bringing new traditions and ideas while still honoring the 
beliefs and practices of their newly adopted home.
FORTUNES & FORTUNE TELLING
Throughout Andaluria, Caio fortune tellers, known as drabardi, 
are revered for their skill in navigating the threads of fate, 
and fortune-telling is a celebrated profession in many Caio 
communities. Different methods—such as cartomancy, 
tasseography, rune casting, osseomancy, and more—are 
passed down within families, and people from nearly every 
caste or community visit drabardi for guidance and insight. 
Most Andalurians consider ignoring drabardi divinations 
foolhardy and offer little sympathy when misfortune follows a 
warning unheeded.
If a PC wishes to have their fortune told by a drabardi, you 
can use the opportunity to guide their next story arc or 
future character development. Fortunes should provide 
cryptic guidance, not prescriptive instructions. You can use 
the fortune prompts below, or take inspiration from them to 
create your own:
A lark sings from the [direction], heralding the coming [noun]. 
Time is [adjective], the song’s end [promising phrase].
Great potential rests on [possessive pronoun or proper noun] 
shoulders, pressing with the weight of [heavy object]. T o lift 
this weight, one must [piece of advice].
Vice and virtue are two sides of the same coin. Beware an 
excess of [virtue], lest it summon its darker half.
The stars look upon you with [emotion], awaiting your next 
move. A faint light points toward [location], beckoning.

===== PAGE 151 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
Faith & Folklore
There are two predominant religious strains in Andaluria: the 
Caio folk tradition and the Church of the Golden Eminence. 
The Caio people embrace a syncretized collection of fables 
and folk rituals that elevate practicality above orthodoxy. 
These stories, superstitions, and sacred rites—which 
combine protective charms, cryptic folkways, and spirit 
worship—arise out of popular practice and are sustained 
through oral tradition. Members of the church, meanwhile, 
adhere to a set of beliefs ordained by the clergy. Their primary 
article of faith is that all reality is the sacred emanation of 
a single god, unnamed but identified as the Divinarch and 
symbolized by a sun. T oday, cultural drift and shifting political 
realities have eroded the boundaries between these religions, 
and many Andalurians practice an amalgam of the two.
This cultural cross-pollination is not celebrated by everyone, 
however. There are those who still curse the church for its role 
in the emperor’s persecution of the Caio people, and church 
orthodoxy deems Caio rites and rituals blasphemy. As a 
result, its most hardline adherents often ignore, insult, or even 
attack folk practitioners in spite of a growing faction of church 
reformists fighting to purge the institution of its prejudice.
But necessity makes for strange bedfellows, and a smart 
monster hunter will use whatever works—belief be damned. 
In Andaluria, folk rites and church rituals both have the 
power to protect people. As the region’s nights lengthen 
and its monsters grow stronger, many of the usual tools are 
proving insufficient. Everyday Andalurians are beginning to 
look beyond their own beliefs for succor—not in the name of 
spiritual curiosity, but of physical survival.
A Region Forged by Rebellion
Over a century ago, the tyrannical Emperor Arcedrio tightened 
his grip over Andaluria by seducing the leadership of the 
Church of the Golden Eminence into his service with dark 
power from origins unknown. The church branded the Caio 
people heretics and turned much of the commonfolk against 
them. In response to this wave of persecution, five Caio 
families rose up and led the rebellion that shattered Arcedrio’s 
imperial rule, then took power for themselves and founded the 
Council of Five, Andaluria’s most influential political body.
Though many Caio believe they betrayed their people’s ways 
in exchange for luxury and decadence, the Council of Five 
became instrumental in transforming Andaluria from a loose 
affiliation of hamlets and estates into a sprawling economic 
powerhouse fueled by the production of some of the world’s 
most sought-after wine, and their direct descendants still 
wield tremendous political influence from their seats of power 
in Jesaña’s magnificently appointed Plaza del Sol.
A LAND DIVIDED
T oday in Andaluria, nobility and peasantry, councilor and 
commoner, church and folk traditions all clash in a perpetual 
cycle. Andaluria’s economy primarily relies upon its vineyards. 
But while the landowners profit, the laborers see the least 
coin, and the struggle between the rich and the poor shapes 
who lives, who starves, and who is fed to the shadows.
JESAÑA
The crown jewel of Andaluria is Jesaña, a city of twisting 
streets, bright markets, and sprawling docks. The buzz of its 
painted warehouses and dockside taverns only enhances the 
elegance of the terraced plazas where the council holds court, 
while the harbor bustles with trade from foreign lands. The 
city is as much a mask as it is a metropolis: beneath its sunlit 
streets and sparkling fountains lurk hidden crypts, secret 
cabals, and the sinful stain of an ancient curse.
The capital city is ruled by five hereditary offices that wield 
immense power. Each of the current councilmembers is a 
direct descendant or heir of the original five Caio people who 
overthrew Emperor Arcedrio:
• Hellenor Galdez (human, she/her) is the Harbormaster 
and oversees ships and trade.
• Marwin Chopex (emberkin, he/him) is the Quartermaster 
and handles the storehouses, armories, and wartime 
supply.
• Andrea Nocta (firbolg, she/her) is the Captain of the 
Nightwatch and deals with the guards, patrols, and spies.
• Miquela Shaas (faun, they/them) is the Archivist and 
manages records, decrees, and secret histories.
• Exel Legasi (aetheris, he/him) is the Market Keeper and 
governs guilds, fairs, and coinage.
Though united in rule, each office protects its own interests 
first. Their rivalries, compromises, and bargains shape the fate 
of the entire region.
THE CONDESA’S SECRET
In Andaluria, wealth is measured in wine, and there is one 
person whose riches rival the council’s: Condesa Ysabela 
Delamoña. But the most powerful noble in Andaluria is 
no mere mortal. Condesa Delamoña is a vampire who has 
operated for centuries under the guise of an unbroken line of 
successors, her “daughters” merely masks for her immortal 
self. Her wealth flows from her fabled vineyards, but her vines 
drink more than water. Peasants vanish from her lands, taken 
in payment for her eternal harvest. The council looks the other 
way because her wines also fill their coffers—as surely as her 
secrets fill their nightmares.
The Caio uprising might never have succeeded without the 
Condesa’s wealth, which armed the rebels as their resources 
waned. When victory came, Delamoña claimed the emperor’s 
palace stronghold, the hilltop Castillo del Cardenal, which 
overlooks the vineyards below and holds the emperor’s 
remains within its dungeon walls. There, the Condesa throws 
elaborate masquerades and bacchanals for the region’s 
wealthy elite. As the owner of the largest and most productive 
vineyards that supply the city merchants with the rich, 
crimson wine they trade away for foreign gold—and as the 
beloved patron of Andaluria’s popular summer festivals—the 
Condesa wields unmatched influence among the nobles and 
commonfolk alike. Perhaps this is why the whispered rumors 
and secret accusations of strange disappearances and blood-
fed vines that haunt her name are never aired in public.

THE INCITING INCIDENT
You can use the prompt below to start your 
campaign, or create your own.
The wine festival in the vineyard village of 
Santavio has always been a night of drink, music, 
and revelry. This year, however, the joy turned 
to horror: When the first casks of Condesa 
Delamoña’s prized red were tapped, an acrid 
stench rose up, and foul creatures burst from 
the barrels. Twisted things of vine and claw, 
dripping with bloodied pulp, descended upon 
the celebration. Panic surged as the monsters 
dragged villagers screaming into the vineyards.
The Council of Five has dispatched the 
Nightwatch, led by Andrea Nocta, to “restore 
order,” but their mission is clear: protect the 
remaining casks of wine, not the people. Nocta’s 
forces are not equipped with the knowledge 
or experience to hunt the monsters terrorizing 
Santavio, and if these creatures return, they 
could threaten villagers and vintage alike.
As the citizens blockade themselves inside the 
quaint inns and cottages of Santavio, a story 
starts to spread: This is the curse of the Condesa 
filtering down to the people who work her land. 
They say her grapes are secretly fed with the 
blood of the innocent, and their tortured souls 
have transformed the vines into coagulated 
vessels of retribution. Meanwhile, Archbishop 
Mateo Veyra claims that Caio heretics are 
responsible for invoking these evil creatures and 
beckons the terrorized locals into the nearby 
church for protection.
The leader of the Sombral Spade, Julio 
Cantafierro, has requested the party’s aid in 
tracking down the creatures responsible for the 
attacks tonight, recovering anyone they find 
alive, and destroying the source of the vineyard’s 
corruption to prevent another tragedy like this 
from happening again.
What is the party’s relationship to Cantafierro 
and the other members of the Sombral Spade? 
Is it money, honor, or something else entirely 
that compels the party to take up the hunt? 
Will the party prioritize the lives of the villagers 
taken or the people left behind? Based on the 
stories from witnesses to the attack, what about 
the monsters terrifies the party most? What 
evidence did the creatures leave behind, and how 
does it put the party on their quarry’s trail?

CAMPAIGN MECHANICS
The following mechanics are unique to this campaign.
Monster-Hunting Equipment

with such fearsome foes. Encourage players to reflavor any of the standard equipment and loot for the genre. Additionally, they

Transformations
The Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign frame includes special 
mechanics for introducing the Reanimated, Vampire, and 
Werewolf transformations. The following sections detail these 
mechanics, as well as advice for implementing them.
Reanimated
When a PC dies, discuss with their player whether they’re 
interested in their character becoming reanimated. If they are, 
work with them between sessions to discuss what laboratory, 
workshop, or place of magic their character awakens within 
during a future session (for example, a necromancer’s 
laboratory hidden beneath the Plaza del Sol). The character 
gains the Reanimated transformation and their maximum 
loadout is reduced to one. During a rest, they can use a 
downtime move to raise their maximum loadout by one until 
they reach the normal maximum loadout of five.
Vampire
When the PCs are facing a prominent vampire adversary, you 
can add the following feature to their stat block.
Vampire’s Curse - Action: Make an attack roll against a target 
within Melee range. On a success, you can spend a Fear to 
have this adversary sink their teeth into the target’s neck and 
try to turn them into a vampire. Roll a number of d8s equal to 
this adversary’s tier and deal that much physical damage to 
the target. Then roll a d6. If the rolled result matches any of 
the damage dice results, the target marks all their Stress and 
gains the Vampire transformation.
If the player wishes for their character to become a vampire, 
then the attack roll succeeds automatically. The PC takes the 
damage, marks all their Stress, and gains the transformation.
Werewolf
When the PCs are facing a prominent werewolf adversary, you 
can add the following feature to their stat block.
Wolf’s Curse - Action: Make an attack roll against a target 
within Melee range. On a success, you can spend a Fear to 
have this adversary viciously bite the target. Roll a number of 
d20s equal to this adversary’s tier and deal that much physical 
damage to the target. If the target takes Major or greater 
damage from this attack, activate a long-term Lycanthropy 
Countdown (6). It ticks down each time the PC takes a rest 
without using a downtime move to halt their lycanthropy’s 
progression. When it triggers, the PC gains the Werewolf 
transformation.
If the player wishes for their character to become a werewolf, 
the attack roll succeeds automatically. The PC takes the 
damage and activates a long-term Lycanthropy Countdown 
(6) regardless of how many HP they marked as a result of 
the attack. When the countdown triggers, the PC gains the 
transformation.
IMPLEMENTING & REMOVING TRANSFORMATIONS
Before implementing transformation mechanics at your table, 
discuss with your players whether they want to include them. 
If you remove transformations from the campaign, talk about 
why their characters are immune to werewolf and vampire 
bites. Have they acquired rings or tattoos to protect them? Do 
they prepare tinctures or potions during downtime? Are they 
descended from familial lines with inherent resistance?
If you’re using these mechanics, consider how the PCs might 
stop or remove the Vampire or Werewolf transformation, 
should they wish to. There could be a ritual known by a certain 
faction, a consumable available only in a village that’s difficult 
to reach, a person who’s known for aiding those who carry 
such curses, or anything else the party can seek out.
Prioritize player agency and consent over everything else, 
and ensure you’re aiming to foster trust and collaborative 
worldbuilding.

dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
The Hunt
The Hunt is an optional procedure for preparing and running 
monsters during individual sessions or short arcs. In a 
Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign, not every major foe 
needs to be a monster, and this process can be modified 
for any prominent adversary, whether or not they possess 
supernatural abilities.
The basic structure of the Hunt comprises five beats: Arrival, 
Investigation, Escalation, Confrontation, and Epilogue. Not 
every Hunt needs to follow this structure, but it provides a 
solid basis for a monster-hunter adventure loop. As always, 
use what speaks to you and leave behind what doesn’t.
• Arrival: The PCs are hooked into the adventure with a 
strange event that implies monster activity.
• Investigation: The PCs follow leads and gather clues to 
discover the truth of the monster.
• Escalation: The PCs bring together what they’ve 
discovered about the monster and develop a plan of action 
for finding and defeating it.
• Confrontation: The PCs attempt to banish, bind, or 
destroy the monster using what they’ve previously 
learned.
• Epilogue: The PCs wrap up loose ends and move on to 
their next mission.
Making a Monster
Monsters are distinguished from other adversaries in a 
Dark Heart of Andaluria campaign by two things: they’re 
contaminated by the evil energy emanating from the hellgate 
beneath the Serracorte Mountains, and they’re driven to 
destroy, consume, or corrupt the inhabitants of Andaluria. T o 
create a monster, answer four questions about it:
What is it? Typically, the monster is a creature your players 
might easily recognize from legends and folklore, such as a 
vampire, werewolf, ghost, or demon. You can also draw on 
less-common monsters (such as the yeti or chupacabra), 
use an otherwise normal person corrupted by evil (such as a 
serial killer or tyrant), or invent something totally new. Give 
your monster a unique or interesting twist to make it less 
predictable.
What does it want? Decide what the monster wants. 
Common motivations include wrath, lust, power, sorrow, 
revenge, greed, hunger, envy, and fear. Here are some 
examples:
• The ghost of a noble seeks vengeance against the rival 
who arranged their assassination.
• An exile from an otherworldly court wishes to reestablish 
their power in Jesaña’s noble halls.
• A grieving siren lures locals into the bay, hoping their 
presence will banish her sorrow.
Where can you find it? Decide on the monster’s lair or hunting 
grounds. Try to think of places that can provide interesting set 
pieces: haunted houses, ancient cemeteries, village festivals, 
cursed forest clearings, and so forth.
How can you defeat it? All monsters have at least one 
weakness that enables the PCs to defeat them. Weaknesses 
can be material (such as cold iron or wooden stakes), ritual 
(such as chanting a sacred verse or placing a holy symbol over 
a door), circumstantial (such as direct sunlight or the sound of 
church bells), or some combination of these (such as drawing 
a circle of salt while reciting an ancient poem). If a monster is 
killed in a way that doesn’t involve its weakness, it revives or 
escapes, often returning stronger than before. The PCs must 
wield a monster’s weakness against it to slay it permanently.
Optional: What else should you worry about? A well-
designed monster is more than an individual physical threat to 
the party—the monster often commands other adversaries, 
endangers NPCs, and stalks special events or locations. You 
can use the following suggestions to make a monster more 
complex:
• Create the monster’s underlings, such as weaker monsters 
or humanoid NPCs aligned with them.
• Invent important bystanders who could be endangered by 
the monster’s actions, such as innocent villagers, other 
investigators, or city officials.
• Design events or locations that can serve as key set pieces 
highlighting the monster’s strengths, weaknesses, and 
motivations.
Preparing Liminal Clues
Every horror leaves traces. Some are visible. Others wait in the 
threshold between knowing and unknowing.
A Liminal Clue is a piece of evidence or information that exists 
in potential until it’s discovered. When the party searches a 
forsaken chapel, questions a dying witness, or sifts through 
the ashes of a beast’s lair, the clue takes form—not because 
it was hidden there or had that form all along, but because the 
party’s investigation calls it into being.
HOW LIMINAL CLUES WORK
Before a session begins, answer the questions from the 
previous “Making a Monster” section:
• What is it?
• What does it want?
• Where can you find it?
• How can you defeat it?
Then devise 4 to 6 pieces of information or evidence that point 
the players toward answering at least one of the questions 
about the monster. These are Liminal Clues—facts that float 
above the fiction like ghosts, waiting to manifest when called 
into play.
Write the Liminal Clues on notecards and keep them on hand 
during the session. Whenever the PCs delve into danger or 
explore the mystery in a way that could reveal the answer 
to one of the questions, you can refer to your collection of 
Liminal Clues. If a clue fits the tone and place, it manifests 
in that moment. Describe how the clue is revealed. Once all 
clues have emerged, the PCs can confront the horror fully 
armed with knowledge.
154 Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria


===== PAGE 156 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
LET THEM WANDER
Don’t bind clues to any particular place. Instead, let them 
linger over the scenes where it would make sense for them 
to appear. A bloodstained sigil might surface in a chapel or a 
dream, or a survivor’s testimony might emerge from a child’s 
drawing or an overheard conversation. Prepare only what 
information or evidence will manifest, not how or where.
HONOR THE NEW REALITY
Once revealed, a Liminal Clue becomes solid. Record it as 
an undeniable discovery: a fact, a sign, or a symptom of the 
monster’s nature. It’s no longer merely potential—it’s now 
part of the world’s grim reality.
GENERAL OR SPECIFIC?
The Liminal Clues you prepare in advance should be a mix of 
general information (“the victim was torn apart and partially 
devoured”) and more specific pieces of evidence (“the only 
member of the family who wasn’t attacked wears a silver 
necklace”). General information can usually arise in a wider 
variety of locations, but it can be difficult to improvise its form 
in the moment. Specific pieces of evidence, meanwhile, have 
slightly more defined forms but can be more difficult to fit into 
the party’s investigation. Having a mix of both distributes the 
mental load.
GO WITH THE FLOW
Learning to use Liminal Clues is one of the trickiest and most 
rewarding parts of running a Hunt, so give yourself grace as 
you practice, and prioritize providing PCs with clues sooner 
rather than later.
The party might at times need to receive information in 
multiple ways before they’ll act on it. If the players miss or 
ignore a clue you’ve given, use more than one of the ideas 
you planned (or another one you create on the fly) until the 
information has been reinforced enough for them to act on 
it. Where helpful, you can be candid with your players. Use 
phrases like “It’s obvious that…” or “You can tell that…” to 
clearly communicate critical information.
Structure of a Hunt
The following sections provide guidance on running the 
session using the monster you’ve created and Liminal Clues 
you’ve prepared.
ARRIVAL
This is the scene or sequence that first hooks the PCs into 
the adventure by establishing a strange event that implies 
monster activity. There are many ways by which the PCs 
can be drawn into the mission: They might be present 
when a monster attacks, they could be called in by an NPC 
to investigate after the fact, or they might simply stumble 
upon the aftermath by happenstance. During this section of 
gameplay, the party acquires the first layer of information 
about the mystery or mission. The information you deliver to 
the party during this sequence should provide the players with 
multiple solid avenues of investigation.
INVESTIGATION
The PCs follow leads discovered during the Arrival or in this 
sequence. Look for opportunities to introduce Liminal Clues. 
If the party’s investigation stalls, remind them of the following 
possible methods of investigation:
• Searching for and Analyzing Physical Evidence: This can 
include tracks or trails (wet footprints), trace evidence 
(wolf hair), notable items or props (a silver dagger 
inscribed with an insignia), or the results of medical 
examinations (the victims’ lungs are filled with water) and 
scientific experiments (the wolf hair found at the scene 
turns to dust when touched with silver).
• Interviewing Eyewitnesses: An eyewitness might see 
the monster’s activity directly if it occurs in public, or they 
might notice suspicious events or behavior that they can 
relate to the PCs after the fact.
• Seeking Advice from Experts: Archivists, investigators, 
and veteran monster hunters can offer guidance on 
dispatching certain types of monsters or interpreting 
certain clues.
• Collecting Gossip and Rumors: Interviewing NPCs 
wherever they gather, such as at the local market or 
tavern, often yields important information.
• Researching Lore and Local History: Helpful local history 
or monster lore can often be found in written records, 
such as church libraries or public archives.
• Using Magic: PCs with the appropriate spells or abilities 
can perform divination rituals or psychometric readings to 
gain information.
ESCALATION
In this sequence, the PCs bring together what they’ve 
discovered about the monster and develop a plan of action 
for finding and defeating it. Don’t expect the players to 
necessarily reach the exact solution you intended—pivot to 
accommodate anything reasonable that the players come up 
with.
Tip: If the party seems stuck or confused, have the monster 
or its allies threaten or attack them (or an important NPC) 
and convey any undiscovered information in an obvious and 
impossible-to-ignore manner.
CONFRONTATION
In this sequence, the PCs attempt to banish, bind, or destroy 
the monster using what they’ve learned. This usually means 
infiltrating the monster’s lair, setting a trap, or surveilling the 
site of a future attack, and often culminates in a combat—
though sometimes the party will come up with a clever plan to 
avoid violence altogether. In those instances, let the players 
succeed and reward them for thinking outside the box.
EPILOGUE
The party ties up any loose ends. This is a good opportunity to 
push forward any long-term projects, advance character arcs, 
pull on additional story threads, and attend to bookkeeping. 
Optionally, you can set the hook for the next session’s Hunt.

Example of Play
Prepping the Session
Shelley (she/her) is planning to run a monster hunt for 
Quincey (he/him), Mina (she/her), and Lucy (she/her).
MONSTER CONCEPT
Shelley starts by making her monster:
• What is it? Shelley’s been on a classic J-horror kick, so 
she wants to do a ghost story in the vein of Dark Water or 
The Ring. She decides on the ghost of a young woman that 
sneaks into people’s houses at night and sucks the souls 
out of their bodies.
• What does it want? Looking through the list of 
motivations, Shelley feels like wrath and revenge both 
make sense for a ghost. She decides that this is the ghost 
of a young woman named Mary, who went missing one 
year ago and was never found. Mary’s spirit wants revenge 
on the village for not finding her or bringing her kidnapper 
to justice.
• Where can you find it? Shelley thinks the old well at the 
center of town would be a suitably spooky place to be a 
ghost’s lair, so she decides her bones are still there at the 
bottom of the well.
• How can you defeat it? Shelley thinks the ghost can be 
laid to rest by finding its bones and burning them.
• Optional: What else should you worry about? Shelley 
thinks it could be cool if the ghost can summon or 
command the souls that she’s already absorbed, so she 
chooses an appropriate stat block to represent them in 
case she finds a place to confront the PCs with them.
ACT STRUCTURE
Shelley looks at the Hunt structure and comes up with brief 
concepts for what could happen in each act. She isn’t trying 
to plan out a plot; she just wants a general idea of what might 
happen, knowing that she’ll almost certainly have to pivot or 
improvise in response to the players’ decisions.
Arrival: The party will be called into a small village on the 
outskirts of Jesaña to investigate the mysterious death of a 
sleeping teenage boy. Shelley prepares a few questions she 
can ask the players at the top of the session to collaboratively 
build a tighter connection between the party and the mystery:
“One of you knows the mother of the victim. What’s your 
relationship to her? How does she feel about you?”
“You’ve been to this village before. What business did you 
previously have here?”
“What rumors have you heard about this part of Andaluria 
that give you pause?”
Investigation: The party will have free rein to talk with the 
locals and investigate public spaces in the village. There, the 
party will learn about Mary’s disappearance one year ago.
Escalation: Mary will strike again the night of the hunters’ 
arrival. Depending on where the PCs are, they might have the 
chance to intercept the ghost and prevent her from taking 
another victim. If Shelley needs to threaten the PCs directly, 
she can have Mary or her spectral minion (the spirit of her first 
victim) attack the party or a notable NPC.
Confrontation: The party can try to lay Mary’s spirit to 
rest. Shelley expects this to be a combat, so she reskins the 
Minor Chaos Elemental into Mary’s ghost. She expects the 
showdown to happen at the well in the center of town, where 
Mary’s bones are hidden, but it can easily be moved to another 
location if needed.
Epilogue: Shelley expects the PCs will have a roleplay scene 
or two with the locals. Depending on how well the party was 
able to defend the village, they might receive a unique item 
connected to the lore they uncovered or relationships they 
explored before leaving the village.
LIMINAL CLUES
Finally, Shelley needs 4 to 6 Liminal Clues—preferably a 
mix between general information that can be revealed in 
numerous places and specific evidence that gives the players a 
really strong lead (but may have to be fudged during play):
• The victim’s spirit was drained while he slept.
• This week is the anniversary of Mary’s disappearance.
• The village children don’t play near the well anymore 
because they keep hearing mysterious crying echoing from 
there.
• A local returning from the fields late at night saw a young 
woman walking barefoot in the village. She didn’t answer 
when called and disappeared when pursued.
• The ghost avoids bright light and open flame.
• There are wet footprints in the victim’s bedroom.
Playing the Session
As Shelley describes the party arriving at the village, she asks 
her prepared questions to the players. T ogether, they establish 
that Quincey’s character, a slyborne infernis ranger, used 
to work in Jesaña with the victim’s mother, and they had a 
romantic relationship before she got married to someone else 
and moved to the village to raise a family.
ARRIVAL
When the party investigates the victim’s bedroom, Mina’s 
character (a highborne human witch) uses her “Commune” 
feature to contact the boy’s spirit. Shelley decides that 
because the victim’s spirit was absorbed, Mina can’t 
establish a connection to it, but her attempt still yields 
useful information: the room is suffused with the spectral 
echoes of the ghost’s spirit-draining attack. This is close to 
the first Liminal Clue that Shelley prepared, but it’s not an 
exact match; Shelley rewrites the clue as “The victim’s spirit 
was drained by a ghost” and hands the card to Mina. Lucy’s 
character (a ridgeborne faerie sorcerer) looks around the room 
for physical evidence, so Shelley describes wet footprints 
leading from the window to the bed. Importantly, Shelley 
didn’t ask for an action roll from Lucy; just by searching 
an area where the clue could be found, Lucy caused it to 
manifest.
156 Chapter 4: Dark Heart of Andaluria


===== PAGE 158 =====
dARk HEART OF AndA lUR i A
INVESTIGATION
The party splits up to expand their investigation. Quincey and 
Lucy look at the “wet footprints” clue and ask whether the 
village is near a stream or a watermill. Shelley could say, “No, 
but there’s a well,” but instead pivots to make the players 
correct and amends the Liminal Clue about the well to the 
mill. Shelley asks how they would find out, and the players say 
they just want to look around. Shelley says, “Yes, there is a 
dilapidated mill at the edge of the village, which no one’s used 
for years.” Quincey and Lucy decide to investigate the mill.
Mina, meanwhile, decides to pursue the ghost lead: “What 
can I find out about ghosts in this area?” Shelley asks whether 
Mina has specific questions in mind or just wants general 
information. Mina says she wants general information on 
ghosts in Andaluria. Shelley says Mina knows (by reputation) 
of a retired ghost hunter who lives in a cottage in the woods a 
couple hours away. Does she want to take the better part of 
the day to go visit them? Mina says yes, and Shelley cuts back 
to Quincey and Lucy…
As this sequence proceeds, Shelley invents locations and 
NPCs as needed, prompting the players for input as she goes. 
Quincey and Lucy encounter a local group of kids daring one 
another to steal something from “Mary’s mill,” and Shelley 
gives them the Liminal Clue that says, “The village children 
don’t play near the mill anymore because they keep hearing 
mysterious crying echoing from there.” Mina visits with the 
retired ghost hunter and learns that the only sure way to 
defeat a ghost for good is to lay their spirit to rest, either by 
magic ritual or by burning their remains. Shelley makes a note 
for herself that the Liminal Clue “The ghost avoids bright light 
and open flame” has now changed into “T o defeat a ghost, lay 
its remains to rest.”
ESCALATION
Mina rushes back to the village as the sun sets. Quincey and 
Lucy enter the mill but are expelled by Mary and her spectral 
minions because they don’t have a way to defeat her yet. The 
PCs reconvene in the tavern at the center of town and share 
the information they’ve collected. Shelley asks them to spitball 
different ways they might lay the ghost’s remains to rest 
based on the information they have. Quincey recalls that the 
local children called it Mary’s Mill—who’s Mary? The PCs ask 
the other patrons at the tavern and learn about Mary Whitton, 
the woman who went missing one year ago, and that her 
disappearance was never solved. After some discussion (and 
light prompting from Shelley), the players come up with their 
answers to the four key questions:
• What is it? The ghost of Mary Whitton, sucking the spirit 
out of the locals in their sleep.
• What does it want? She’s angry that the village never 
solved her disappearance.
• Where can we find it? In the abandoned mill.
• How do we defeat it? Find her bones inside the mill and 
bury them.
Note that the answer to the final question is not what Shelley 
planned, and it’s based on a little bit of a guess on the players’ 
part. However, it makes sense that Mary is haunting the mill 
because her body was hidden there. The players decide to raid 
the mill and search for Mary’s remains so they can lay her to 
rest.
CONFRONTATION
The party heads to the mill that night. Shelley runs the combat 
mostly as anticipated, but with a complication that Mary 
can’t be defeated until a player finds her bones by searching 
the mill with appropriate action rolls. When the players 
discover Mary’s bones, Shelley asks them to describe the 
ritual they use to lay her to rest—but rules that it will succeed 
automatically because failure would be anticlimactic.
EPILOGUE
Shelley decides to run the Epilogue as a montage in which 
each player gets to describe one way their PC honors Mary’s 
memory:
• Quincey convinces the town to tear down the mill.
• Lucy crafts a simple headstone for Mary and places it in 
the local graveyard.
• Mina returns to the retired ghost hunter and has tea with 
them (she liked this NPC and wants them to become a 
regular fixture throughout the campaign).
Shelley finishes the session with a teaser of the next session’s 
mystery: She describes a local forester running full tilt through 
the dark forest, blood streaming down his face. He breaks 
through the treeline and skids to a stop just before narrowly 
avoiding toppling over a cliff. The full moon glows behind him 
as his eyes dart back and forth, desperate for an escape route. 
Then a bone-chilling howl pierces the night sky.
SESSION ZERO QUESTIONS
Ask your players any of these questions, or make your own.
• You once fought a monster together and one of you 
nearly died. Who saved them, and what scar, physical or 
otherwise, remains from that night?
• Who in Jesaña (a noble, a guild, or even the council) claims 
you owe them a debt?
• What local ward, charm, or ritual do you all believe in, even 
if others dismiss it as peasant nonsense?
• What festival or celebration did you all attend together 
in the past, and what happened there that still lingers 
between you?
• Which of the ruling families has taken an interest in 
your group and why? Do they see you as useful pawns, 
dangerous meddlers, or future allies?
• How does the church regard your party? Are you wayward 
souls to bring back into the fold, heretics to be watched, or 
necessary protectors of the people?
