JOURnEY TO HORiZOn
After a thousand-year cataclysm, a fellowship of heroes embarks from their magically protected 
sanctuary to journey across a broken continent and resurrect the fallen goddess who can heal 
their world.
Designed by James Haeck, Rob Hebert, James Introcaso, and Spenser Starke
Complexity Rating: ••••
THE PITCH
Read this section to your players to introduce them to the 
campaign.
Long ago, the vast continent of Valloren was home to 
wondrous landscapes shaped by the goddess Aurelia, wielder 
of the elemental essence called terrana. But when her 
disciples’ betrayal threatened to extinguish magic forever, 
Aurelia sacrificed her own life to call down the stars and purge 
the land of corruption. T o protect her people from annihilation, 
Aurelia raised magical barriers that would last a thousand 
years.
In a Journey to Horizon campaign, you play heroes chosen 
by fate to leave their recently unsealed sanctuary and travel 
across a turbulent and hostile world on a quest to resurrect 
the goddess.
TONE & FEEL
Adventurous, Epic, Hopeful, Magical, Mysterious, 
Sprawling, Wondrous
THEMES
Faith in Friendship, Growing Shadows, Hope Rekindled, 
New Beginnings, Pathways Long & Winding, Points of Light 
in a Ruined World
TOUCHSTONES
The Dragon Prince, Fallout, Final Fantasy VII, Kipo and the 
Age of the Wonderbeasts, The Legend of Zelda: Breath of the 
Wild, The Lord of the Rings Trilogy, Lunar, Willow
158 Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 160 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
OVERVIEW
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players the following information before character creation.
The land of Valloren is a vast continent first raised out of an 
endless ocean by the goddess Aurelia (she/her). Wielding 
the magical essence terrana, Aurelia created incredible 
magical landscapes: supernaturally lush forests teeming with 
oversized game, crystal mountains floating high above fields 
of singing flowers, and enchanted plains of perpetually falling 
snow—all in impossible proximity to one another.
Amid this tremendous bounty, the people of Valloren 
flourished and multiplied, building shrines across the 
continent to honor their goddess. T o guide and protect her 
creation, Aurelia selected thirteen followers to become her 
magi and granted each of them a fragment of her immortal 
power. Under their watchful eyes and steady hands, Valloren 
enjoyed an Age of Wonder. But it wouldn’t last.
Among Aurelia’s magi were the Lovers, Yrska and Drago. 
Alone, each of them surpassed their fellow magi in skill and 
power—but together, they rivaled Aurelia herself. In pursuit 
of the people’s praise and their own prideful ambitions, the 
Lovers drew upon ever-increasing amounts of terrana to fuel 
the swift expansion of incredible, sprawling settlements.
But while Valloren’s reserves of terrana were endlessly 
renewable, they were not inexhaustible. Whereas Aurelia had 
never drawn more from the land than it could safely replenish, 
the Lovers’ reckless consumption threatened to drain the 
world of magic forever. When Aurelia counseled—then 
commanded—restraint, the Lovers rebelled. In response, 
Aurelia raised an army of devotees and appointed her eleven 
remaining magi its generals. At first, a quick victory seemed 
assured. But one by one, the Lovers lured the other magi into 
their rebellion with promises of unlimited power.
As the war dragged on, the land’s magic was nearly depleted. 
If the last mote of terrana were consumed, magic would 
vanish from Valloren, never to return. Exhausted by decades of 
conflict, Aurelia’s forces were driven back to the eastern edge 
of the continent, where—with no hope of victory—they made 
their last stand. There, in a final desperate gambit, Aurelia 
performed a fatal ritual that raised magical barriers around 
her shrines and pulled the stars from the sky, destroying all 
thirteen traitorous magi and their followers. With her last 
breath, Aurelia promised the faithful that, in time, the barriers 
would fall and she would return.
The Starfall scoured the face of Valloren in a maelstrom of 
cosmic energy, with only a fraction of Valloren’s population 
surviving the cataclysm behind the barriers raised by Aurelia. 
For a thousand years, these survivors persevered within their 
sanctuaries, each enclave completely sealed off from the 
outside world—and one another—as the rest of the continent 
was razed. Vallorens named this period of isolation and 
calamity the Long Sorrow.
In time, the storm relented and the land began to heal. Slowly 
but surely, the quantum of terrana that had been saved by 
Aurelia’s dire sacrifice replenished itself—before surging 
wholly unrestrained. When the sanctuaries finally opened, 
their inhabitants emerged into the wilds of a New Valloren. 
Whole regions had been transformed—first devastated by the 
Starfall, then cleansed in the Long Sorrow, and finally mutated 
by the resurgence of terrana. From the cratered graves of 
fallen stars, a toxic radiance called shadowblight had seeped 
into the environment, turning nearby habitats into malicious 
wastelands stalked by ravening abominations.
Now, the maps and legends of Old Valloren no longer match 
reality—and in the short time since the sanctuaries opened, 
the people of Valloren have set about reforging old alliances 
and cutting new trails. But the outside world is hostile and 
chaotic, with the shadowblight spreading and raw terrana 
amplifying the untamed wilderness. For Valloren to thrive 
once more, Aurelia must return as promised. Only she has 
the power to lift the stars skyward, drive back the smoldering 
creep of shadowblight, and give birth to a second Age of 
Wonder.
But where is she?
At the shrine in the center of the Marrogate sanctuary, at the 
southwestern tip of Valloren, High Priestess Saraya (she/her) 
has made a stunning discovery: a Soul Echo. Through her holy 
order’s sacred rituals, Saraya has learned that, to resurrect 
Aurelia, the lingering fragments of Aurelia’s divine essence 
must be collected from throughout Valloren and transported 
to the site of her death, where body and spirit can be reunited. 
In secret, Saraya crafts a Vessel of Light to protect the Echoes 
and makes preparations for her long journey across Valloren.
Unfortunately, there are those who wish to prevent Aurelia’s 
resurrection. Faith in Aurelia faltered during the Long 
Sorrow’s thousand-year span—and the Cult of the Two-
Headed Coin, a religious faction devoted to the Lovers, grew 
to fill the void. Though most members of Saraya’s Aurelian 
Order remain devoted to the goddess, their ranks have been 
secretly infiltrated by a feared company of cultists known 
as the Fellsworn Templars. Meanwhile, wraithlike Seekers-
in-Shadow—wretched shades that wield terrana and 
shadowblight alike in powerful displays of dark magic—have 
been spotted throughout Valloren.
On the day Saraya plans to embark from Marrogate, the 
Fellsworn T emplars attack. In desperation, she entrusts the 
Vessel to your care and, knowing it means her own death, 
draws the templars’ attention to enable your escape.
Now you must collect the Soul Echoes and resurrect Aurelia 
before darkness and despair overtake New Valloren. Whether 
you’ve prepared for this quest or found yourself entangled 
in it unexpectedly, you know the journey will be long and 
treacherous. The Cult of the Two-Headed Coin is on the rise, 
the Fellsworn are on your trail, and the Seekers-in-Shadow 
stalk the path ahead of you. You’ll find no safety in the 
wilderness and must rely on sanctuaries transformed by one 
thousand years of isolation to rest and recover.
But you must complete your quest, the Vessel must be 
protected, and the goddess must be revived—for only her 
magic can heal this land and save it from destruction.
159Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 161 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
COMMUNITIES
In a Journey to Horizon campaign, all communities aside from wanderborne are available, but some have unique aspects.  
As needed, provide the following information to your players and choose one or more of the questions to ask them during your 
session zero.
All Communities
In this campaign, all PCs are from the sanctuary of Marrogate, 
but that doesn’t mean they all come from the same 
community.
After the players choose communities for their characters, 
discuss as a group how one thousand years of isolation 
inside a sanctuary shaped their characters’ communities and 
establish the necessary truths to support the choices made 
at character creation. For instance, while long sea voyages 
would be impossible during the Long Sorrow, the Marrogate 
sanctuary could border a bay or float atop the ocean waves, 
making a seaborne PC playable.
Bear in mind that no sanctuary is a monoculture. Each 
society still has various social classes, vocations, religious 
enclaves, and other groups and cultures within its walls. Some 
sanctuaries are large enough to encompass more than a 
single biome. If this is the case for your campaign’s version of 
Marrogate, think about how people living in or between each 
of these environments might interact.
Habitat-Based Communities
When a player chooses a habitat-based community for their 
character, such as ridgeborne, duneborne, or seaborne, that 
habitat should inform what kind of sanctuary Marrogate is. 
The magic of terrana allows for biomes to be as realistic or 
fantastical as you want. For example, if different members 
of the party choose all three of the communities previously 
listed, Marrogate could encompass a region with a mountain 
range separating a small sea and a sprawling desert. 
Alternatively, it could be a desert that encircles a large body of 
water with a single mountain jutting from its center.
Loreborne
From the outset of the Long Sorrow, many saw the need 
to preserve not just life but knowledge, and they formed 
communities dedicated to assembling and maintaining 
vast libraries of information from Old Valloren that would 
otherwise be lost.
• What knowledge from Old Valloren did your people think 
most worthy of preservation? How did they keep it safe?
• What do you most hope to learn about New Valloren on 
your journey across the continent?
• What mystery about Old Valloren do you hope to solve by 
exploring New Valloren?
• What was the story or piece of information from the old 
world that you found hidden away in an ancient book—
and that you believe nobody else knows about?
Reborne
As the sanctuaries opened, so did a new chapter for Valloren 
and its inhabitants. Anyone who felt trapped within their 
original community gained the opportunity to leave it behind 
and seek a sense of belonging on the path to parts unknown.
• How or why did you split from your original community?
• How has the opportunity to leave the sanctuary affected 
your perspective on your community? Is this the first time 
you’ve rejected it?
• What person or practice are you sad to have lost when 
you left your community? If you don’t remember your life 
before, what face do you keep seeing as you fall asleep?
Wanderborne
Because of the isolated nature of the sanctuaries, the 
wanderborne community is not available in a Journey to 
Horizon campaign.
Warborne
The isolation and deprivation that characterized life inside 
many sanctuaries sometimes pushed the people there into 
all-out conflict with one another. In some sanctuaries, internal 
clashes lasted generations and eclipsed any hope of peaceful 
coexistence. If a player chooses to play a warborne character, 
it means Marrogate is one such sanctuary. Think about how 
the other PCs, regardless of their community, would have 
been affected by war.
• What political crisis within Marrogate triggered the first 
strike?
• What resources do the people of Marrogate still fight 
over?
• What factions arose against one another within 
Marrogate’s walls?

 Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon

JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
ANCESTRIES
All ancestries are available, but some have unique aspects in a 
Journey to Horizon campaign. As needed, provide the following 
information to your players.
Long- and Short-Lived Ancestries
Many ancestries, such as drakona and dwarf, have natural 
lifespans of 250–350 years, meaning that a full-grown 
adult might be only a handful of generations removed from 
the Starfall. For these people, the Age of Wonder is less a 
legendary lost era and more a near-historical period about 
which they have family lore. On the other hand, some 
ancestries (such as faerie) have a relatively short lifespan. 
Sanctuary life is essentially all they, or any ancestor they feel 
a strong connection to, have ever known. Think about how the 
natural lifespan of your character’s ancestry might shape their 
relationship to Old Valloren, and how they would react to the 
perils and promise of New Valloren.
Clank
Since many raw materials would be scarce in certain 
sanctuaries, a clank’s body composition is often a 
representation of what resources were plentiful where they 
were built. Because clanks often live as long as they can 
source new parts, a clank might have experienced much of the 
Long Sorrow—or even some of the Age of Wonder. Because 
their memories fade over time, however, it’s unlikely that 
many clanks remain who fully remember life outside their 
sanctuary.
Elves
An elf’s mystic form can shift as they travel between regions 
where terrana flows freely. If you decide to play an elf who 
possesses a mystic form, consider how its appearance 
changes as you encounter the strange biomes and natural 
wonders of New Valloren.
Fungril
During the Long Sorrow, the mycelial arrays of fungril 
networks couldn’t penetrate the sanctuary barriers. Now that 
the walls have come down, this network is slowly rebuilding, 
and fungril across the land are connecting with each other for 
the first time. This new connectivity is very exciting to some 
fungril, while others are wary of the risks it might bear. If you 
choose to play a fungril, consider how the slow regrowth of 
this psychic network might affect your journey, and what your 
role might be in its ongoing development.
CLASSES
All classes and subclasses aside from the Syndicate rogue 
are available, but some have unique aspects in a Journey 
to Horizon campaign. As needed, provide the following 
information to your players.
Magic Users in Valloren
In Valloren, all magic relies upon the mystical essence called 
terrana. No matter the source of their power—be it faith, 
education, or talent—a magic user requires terrana to fuel 
their spells. It’s up to your table how terrana manifests: it 
could be a glowing mist that rises out of the land, iridescent 
electricity, or simply an invisible sense of calm that permeates 
the atmosphere.
For the first few centuries of the Long Sorrow, the near-total 
depletion of terrana rendered magic nigh-impossible to cast, 
but the recent exponential resurgence of terrana has given 
birth to a magical renaissance. In New Valloren, terrana is raw 
and wild. Whenever magic is used, the environment responds. 
Plants might bend toward the caster or burst from the soil at 
their feet, visible lines of crackling energy might crawl up from 
the ground and into their body, or rain clouds might churn 
and swirl overhead. The more powerful the spell, the more 
dramatic the effect—and the more potential for surprising or 
chaotic results.
This is especially noticeable for druids, rangers, witches, 
and elemental origin sorcerers, who all have a more intimate 
connection to nature magic through their class features or 
access to the Sage domain.
Druids
A druid’s intimate connection to the natural world can help 
them quickly intuit deeper truths about the strange flora and 
fauna they encounter on their journeys through Valloren, and 
many druids are excited to explore a world made miraculous 
by wild magic. Players who create a druid should think 
about how their abilities might be shaped by the strange and 
chaotic environments they travel through. For instance, their 
Beastform may manifest differently depending on the habitat 
they’re in, or their proximity to the shadowblight might affect 
them in peculiar and dangerous ways.
Guardians & Warriors
Aurelia’s barriers made full-scale war between sanctuaries 
impossible, but many still suffered from prolonged civil 
conflicts. Some sanctuaries kept standing peacekeeping 
forces or militias for the sake of order and stability, with 
variable success. And now that the walls have fallen, many 
sanctuaries have spun up military forces to ensure their 
people’s safety—or the conquest of their neighbors. If a player 
chooses to play a guardian or warrior, it’s likely their character 
has a connection to one of these forces.
161Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 163 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
CLASSES (CONTINUED)
Rangers
Throughout the Long Sorrow, rangers hunted, tracked, and 
patrolled across relatively limited areas. Most have little 
experience beyond one or two biomes, and many creatures 
they encounter beyond the walls of their sanctuary will be 
strange to them. They do, however, have better-than-average 
survival skills honed in their home territories, as well as a 
knack for adapting to whatever they find on their journey. 
Within their sanctuaries, rangers often took on more of a 
conservationist role, rather than a hunter or trailblazer role, 
because land—and the game it could produce—was so 
limited. Once the sanctuaries were unsealed, however, many 
of the first inhabitants to explore the surrounding areas and 
map their new terrain were rangers.
Seraphs
Before the Long Sorrow, nearly all seraphs put their faith in 
Aurelia—but a dead goddess doesn’t answer prayers. If a 
player chooses a seraph at character creation, discuss with 
your table what this could mean: Have they discovered other 
deities? If so, how does that alter their beliefs about Aurelia’s 
place in the pantheon? If they still worship Aurelia, what does 
it mean that their prayers are now being answered? How does 
the knowledge that they are in the presence of a fragment of 
Aurelia’s essence affect their faith?
Syndicate Rogue
Because of the isolated nature of the sanctuaries, the 
Syndicate rogue is not available in a Journey to Horizon 
campaign.
Warlocks
Warlocks wield power bestowed upon them by entities from 
other realms, but in Valloren these magic abilities must still be 
fueled by terrana. Thus, like other spellcasters, warlocks have 
seen their power restored as terrana has returned over the 
last 1,000 years. If you choose to play a warlock, think about 
how your patron came to interact with this realm and what 
their feelings toward Aurelia or terrana would be.
PLA YER PRINCIPLES
If your group decides to play this campaign, give your players 
the following information before character creation.
Explore the Land and Discover Its People
While a trek through the lands of New Valloren is filled with 
danger and hardship, it’s also a wellspring of discovery and 
wonder. No path is properly mapped, and impossible vistas 
lie just beyond the next horizon: crystal ruins untouched for a 
thousand years, a river that glows with a cerulean light, green 
lightning striking a shrine atop a floating mountain. These 
curiosities exist to be investigated and explored.
Characters who take risks and seek to uncover the unknown 
are often rewarded with priceless information or relics from 
an ancient age. Journey smart, but don’t be afraid to go off the 
beaten path when something draws your interest. When your 
character reaches a new sanctuary, learn about its denizens 
and their way of life. When you find a mystery, solve it. If you 
see an injustice, rectify it.
Grow Together
While traveling with your fellowship, ask honest questions and 
give honest answers. Steal moments from the main journey 
for quiet bits of conversation and comfort. Aid your allies and 
help your friends. Long journeys through new places teach us 
about ourselves and one another. Exploring a new land can 
make you more courageous, compassionate, and open-minded 
than before. The experiences your character has and the 
people they meet should change them for the better, one step 
at a time.
Keep Moving Forward
While it’s important to take time away from the main storyline 
for more episodic or emotional beats, don’t forget the ultimate 
goal: to resurrect the goddess Aurelia. The forces of darkness 
are aligned against your party and grow in power with every 
step of the journey. Remember the weight of the mission and 
press on accordingly.
162 Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 164 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
GM PRINCIPLES
Keep the following guidance in mind when you GM this campaign.
Reward Curiosity
Don’t penalize the PCs for exploring the wider world of 
New Valloren. They must face challenges on their journey, 
but not punishment. When a PC delves into a dangerous 
ruin or pursues a tantalizing sidequest, reward them with 
information, treasure, or something else they desire. The PCs 
should never end an adventure empty-handed.
Make the World Wondrous
T errana not only fills Valloren with lush, diverse biomes—it 
also makes those environments fantastical. Your New Valloren 
can include a range of extraordinary landscapes, such as 
clouds you can walk on, a jungle of neon flora, or a lake of 
living glass. This land is also dotted with the remnants of 
the old world, like submersibles of silver now beached on 
the shore and inhabited by giant, human-faced crabs. Create 
environments that the PCs will be eager to explore and let 
your imagination run wild.
Put Other People Out There
New Valloren is vast and unexplored, and the PCs aren’t 
the only people with purpose. Many others are leaving their 
sanctuaries to establish small villages in fantastic places, 
build roadhouses for weary travelers, and go on pilgrimages to 
holy sites. These folks allow you to give the PCs information 
about nearby landmarks and environments, news from the 
sanctuaries, and a way to rest and recuperate away from the 
road. They also allow the PCs to chat with other people aside 
from each other and share stories of their adventures.
Many agents and factions want the Vessel for themselves. 
Hound the party with dark forces that threaten to discover 
their goal. Waylay them with difficult dilemmas. T empt them 
with easy outs, then confront them with the consequences.
Show the Scars of the Long Sorrow
Though terrana has returned to Valloren with a vengeance, 
the continent still carries the trauma of the Starfall and 
its aftermath. The party should come across regions that 
terrana’s resurgence has not reached or that have been 
corrupted by shadowblight. Confront them with places where 
fallen stars exude toxic magic: ruined quarries where time and 
space are warped, sprawling marshes teeming with undead, 
and abandoned fortifications where twisted beasts that know 
only hunger dwell.
Additionally, not everything went according to Aurelia’s plan. 
Some things—somehow—survived the Starfall. There are 
sanctuaries whose walls came down early, some that are still 
trapped within their barrier, and others still that were never 
fully protected to begin with. Some sanctuaries, left to stew in 
their own juices, fell to internal crises or conflicts. And a rare 
few are festering sources of shadowblight, filled with people 
transformed into something unnatural and wrong.
Think in Themes
This campaign features disconnected locations, huge 
swathes of wilderness, a large number of random tables, 
and numerous mechanical abstractions; you can unite these 
disparate elements by linking them to special themes that you 
select while preparing a session, improvise at the table, or 
notice as they naturally recur throughout the campaign. Think 
about how a great episode or arc of a television series often 
juggles multiple storylines that each touch on related ideas or 
questions: “found family,” “loss of innocence,” “what is the 
nature of evil?” If you’re having trouble finding a throughline, 
you can’t go wrong with “the power of love.” Try to weave 
these themes into the people, places, and events you present 
to the party, and the story you tell together will cohere into 
something beautiful and surprising.
163
Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon

===== PAGE 165 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
DISTINCTIONS
Use this information to prepare your campaign. You can also share it with your players as needed.
An Uncharted World of Wonder
There are no accurate maps of New Valloren as it exists today. 
When terrana returned, it didn’t restore the world exactly 
as it was; instead, citrus groves grew where deserts once 
sprawled, icy snowfields covered former grasslands, and 
inland seas became canyon labyrinths.
The map that comes with this campaign frame is of Old 
Valloren. The only data the party can rely on is the locations of 
sanctuaries—though some sanctuaries might have fallen by 
the time the party arrives at them. Outside the sanctuaries, 
New Valloren is wild and dangerous. When the party travels 
from one sanctuary to another, they’ll invariably encounter:
• Overgrown environments bursting with terrana
• Magical wonders that defy explanation
• The ruins of Old Valloren, many of them filled with ancient 
relics or haunted by baleful specters
• Pockets of shadowblight where fallen stars bleed gray
• Lairs and encampments inhabited by shadowblighted 
survivors
• New settlements built by former sanctuary-dwellers—
temporary encampments at first, but eventually 
permanent fortifications and villages as the party’s journey 
wears on
• Travelers, peddlers, and explorers
• Ancient routes and fresh-cut pathways
• Hazards both magical and mundane
• Agents of the enemy (Cultists of the Two-Headed Coin, 
Fellsworn T emplars, and Seekers-in-Shadow)
• Bandits, brigands, and outlaws
IMPOSSIBLE ENVIRONMENTS
The terrana-charged habitats and biomes of Valloren defy 
explanation and border each other in impossible ways: rivers 
flow uphill, tropical jungles give way to frozen tundras, and 
patches of earth only a few leagues wide somehow possess 
their own cycle of day and night. When designing a new area 
for the PCs to explore or traverse, consider the following 
questions:
• What is the natural environment this area is based on, and 
how can I make it magical?
• What unique dangers can the PCs face here?
• What benefits or boons might the party find?
• What unique landmarks or curiosities could the PCs 
encounter?
This campaign frame includes special mechanics for creating, 
exploring, and mapping New Valloren (see the “Journeys in 
Valloren” section on page 169).
164 Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 166 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
Shrines & Sanctuaries
Throughout Valloren are shrines dedicated to the goddess 
Aurelia. In Old Valloren, these were fonts of terrana 
maintained by her priests and devotees. In New Valloren, 
they’re the only points of true safety in a dangerous world. 
Resting and recuperating in the wilderness is difficult, so 
reaching a sanctuary—and therefore a shrine—will usually be 
a moment of relief for the party.
When Aurelia performed her final rite, she caused a ring of 
colossal trees to erupt from the ground surrounding each of 
her shrines. These trees grew until they touched the clouds, 
then twisted and curved in on themselves, forming a tightly 
woven, impenetrable canopy. On the interior face of each 
barrier, the trees bore magical fruit and flower blossoms 
that shone with sunlight and cried tears of rainwater, 
staving off drought and famine throughout the Long Sorrow. 
When creating a sanctuary, think about how it might have 
developed over a millennium of isolation—what changed or 
transformed? What unique customs or cultures emerged? 
What stayed the same or was preserved?
Here are a few sample sanctuaries to inspire your own 
creations:
• Cyntix is a city built atop a seemingly inexhaustible vein 
of iron ore. Its people have become some of the greatest 
blacksmiths and tinkerers of their time. Among other 
incredible steam-powered technologies is a working 
rail system that transports people via mine cart. The 
Machinist Guild operates as the de facto political body, 
though technically there are still noble titles inherited from 
Old Valloren. The current Guildmaster is Esio Bonte (he/
him), a galapa with a level head and an analytical mind.
• Polwood, known as the Sleeping Sanctuary, is named for 
its practice of using a specially brewed tea to place the 
entire population, save a single caretaker, into a trance-
like slumber that could stretch resources for decades. The 
current caretaker, Maia Brody (they/them), is a timid orc 
who has, for some reason, refrained from awakening their 
people even though the sanctuary has been unsealed.
• Harthaven is encased in the colossal ribcage of a long-
dead beast, and its people draw warmth from the life 
force that lingers within the bones. They are healers, 
philosophers, and bonewrights who carve relics of 
protection. The priestly caste that rules over Harthaven 
forbids discussion of the creature’s name or origin. Its 
youngest member, a gnome named Kael Orin (he/him), 
has begun hearing a heartbeat echoing through the 
marrow halls.
• Emberhold is situated inside the caldera of a dormant 
volcano, where its forges never truly cooled. Its people 
channel geothermal heat into radiant energy that 
powers entire neighborhoods. The current Speaker of 
the Cindermarch, a katari named Liora Dane (she/her), 
fears that the resurgence of terrana has reawakened the 
mountain’s lust for fire.
For special mechanics and additional guidance related to 
creating sanctuaries, see the “Dice T ables for Creating 
Sanctuaries” section on page 174.
Not all shrines are identical. Generally, the larger a settlement 
was, the larger their shrine—and the larger the perimeter of 
the sanctuary that rose up around it. A small village might 
have a simple stone shrine about the size of a well, whereas a 
city may be home to a multi-level temple.
The party can take a long rest only at a sanctuary (see 
the upcoming “Campaign Mechanics” section for more 
information). The priests who maintain the shrine within a 
sanctuary are oathbound to care for respectful travelers and 
give them access to facilities for hygiene and laundry, clean 
food and water, and a safe place to recover from wounds or 
illness.
SOUL ECHOES
The goddess is dead, but her spirit reverberates throughout 
Valloren. Now, these vibrations have coalesced into Soul 
Echoes that can be found within ten shrines. T o complete 
their quest, the party must locate all the Soul Echoes, gather 
them into a specially crafted Vessel of Light, and carry them 
to the site of Aurelia’s holy remains. At the start of the Inciting 
Incident, the party will have acquired their first Soul Echo, the 
one placed into the Vessel of Light by High Priestess Saraya. 
T o locate the remaining nine, they must use the Vessel itself: 
the Soul Echoes inside call to their sisters, and the song they 
sing together lights the party’s path.
For more about placing these Echoes on the map, see the 
“Placing Soul Echoes” section on page 169.
THE VESSEL OF LIGHT
The Vessel of Light was created by High Priestess Saraya 
to securely gather and transport Aurelia’s Soul Echoes. The 
nature of the Vessel is intentionally ambiguous so you and 
your table can decide what it is. Below are three options:
• The Vessel is a seemingly mundane object, such as a 
carved urn or a large acorn that must be planted in her 
grave.
• The Vessel is an obviously magical item of power, such as 
a glowing orb that must be hidden under thick velvet to 
avoid suspicion.
• The Vessel is an infant or young child named Echo who 
must be cared for by the party.
Tip: Some players might be uncomfortable with the idea 
of a child being put in danger of any sort but still want the 
Echo to have a personality. If so, you can make Echo a non-
humanoid creature, such as a dragon hatchling or a spirit 
that speaks in the voice of the goddess.
Regardless of the Vessel’s form, the Soul Echoes within it are 
sustained by terrana from the earth and as such must remain 
close to the ground lest they fade and perish. As a result, the 
party can’t significantly shorten their journey by flying across 
Valloren.
165Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 167 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
It’s up to you and your table how much the average person 
knows about Aurelia’s prophecy. Discuss who might have 
heard her last words. They might have been a psychic 
projection heard by all, or only her most faithful followers. 
They might have been written in stone on her shrines, or 
merely an article of faith preached by the hierophants of her 
holy order. Whatever your table decides, think about how the 
form and reach of the prophecy might affect the party’s quest.
CLIFFS OF HORIZON
The site of Aurelia’s final ritual is the northeasternmost tip 
of Valloren, the Cliffs of Horizon. This is the final destination 
of the party’s journey, but they should go there only when 
they’ve collected all ten Soul Echoes to reunite them with the 
goddess’s remains. Doing so will resurrect Aurelia and allow 
her to restore Valloren to its former glory.
As with the Vessel, the nature and form of Aurelia’s remains 
have been left up to your table. She might be a 100-foot-
tall statue, a twisting tree, or a perfectly preserved corpse 
protected by her own sanctuary walls. When the party reaches 
the Cliffs of Horizon with all ten Echoes, use the following 
questions to guide the scene: What’s the current state of 
the site and Aurelia’s remains? What must the party do to 
complete the reunion? In what form is Aurelia resurrected 
from her long death? When the goddess’s soul is reunited with 
her body, how does the world around her change?
The Factions of New Valloren
There are two main religious orders in New Valloren: the 
Aurelian Order and the Cult of the Two-Headed Coin. The 
Aurelian Order is still dedicated to the goddess for whom 
it’s named. In Old Valloren, it was the dominant religion, 
respected and celebrated in virtually every settlement. During 
the Long Sorrow, faith in the goddess—and thus the order’s 
ranks—diminished as prayers to Aurelia went unanswered 
and numerous generations passed without her return. As the 
influence of the Aurelian Order waned, the power of the Cult 
of the Two-Headed Coin rose in equal measure.
Before the Long Sorrow, devotees of the Lovers secretly 
carried coins with Yrska’s head on one side and Drago’s on 
the other. Showing one’s two-headed coin to another devotee 
proved your membership in this secret cabal. During the 
Long Sorrow, some came to believe that the world would 
have been better served by the Lovers’ victory. The ranks of 
the cult swelled until, in many places, there was no longer 
need for secrecy. Many cultists now practice in the open, and 
sanctuaries fly banners bearing the image of the Two-Headed 
Coin. There are even cultists who have infiltrated the Aurelian 
Order: the Fellsworn T emplars. Once the cult’s preeminent 
warriors and spies, many Fellsworn T emplars went into hiding 
during the Long Sorrow. Down the centuries, they secretly 
bequeathed their mantles from mentor to apprentice and 
embedded themselves into the Aurelian Order hierarchy, 
where they could prevent Aurelia’s resurrection—or use the 
vehicle of her rebirth to bring back the Lovers.
166
 Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon

===== PAGE 168 =====
JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
Shadowblight
Shadowblight is the toxic radiance that oozes from the craters 
caused by the Starfall. This destructive energy leaches into 
the earth and drains both life and color from the land. While 
most travelers avoid shadowblighted regions, an adventurer 
might have to cross them to shorten a journey, evade pursuit, 
or locate something important.
At these corrupted sites, acid storms, life-draining fog banks, 
and clouds of impenetrable darkness can manifest at any 
time. Spells cast here can drain the caster’s life force or 
trigger dangerous and unpredictable side effects. The air is 
hot and thick with a malice that makes it difficult to breathe. 
Even just being near a patch of shadowblight is dangerous, 
as twisted beasts and relentless undead often emerge from 
these chaotic wastelands in search of lives to consume.
Areas infected with shadowblight are drained of color and 
eventually dissolve into gray sludge. Any creature, living 
or dead, that stays in close proximity to a shadowblighted 
environment for long enough becomes corrupted in body, 
mind, and soul, turning grayscale and exuding a gloomy 
aura that desaturates their surroundings. Shadowblighted 
creatures are ravenous, undead things that greedily consume 
life and terrana alike.
The Seekers-in-Shadow
For one thousand years, the Seekers stalked the wastelands of 
Valloren. Though the party is unaware, these shades are the 
baleful spirits of the eleven fallen magi, starved of terrana and 
furious at the failure of their rebellion. Now, they finally have 
a chance to bring back their overlords—or take power for 
themselves.
In their natural form, the Seekers are tattered, vaguely 
humanoid shrouds of shadow-stuff with stretched and 
contorted proportions. Much as in life, they can draw terrana 
from the land to power their magic spells. Unlike in life, their 
doing so leaves a wound in the environment that fills with 
shadowblight.
After absorbing terrana, a Seeker’s appearance takes on 
aspects of the environment they devastated. For instance, 
after consuming terrana in a forest, their body might grow 
gray leaves and become covered in bark. A Seeker who 
devastates a snowy field might gain crystalline skin or ice-
white patches of fur. This absorbed terrana doesn’t just 
change their appearance, it also grants them access to new 
abilities that reflect these altered states. When a Seeker is 
defeated, their body regenerates some time later—though a 
party could endeavor to find a special weapon or ritual that 
can destroy the Seeker for good.
Impending Doom
The party’s journey has a ticking clock. The shadowblight 
spreads, the Seekers sniff them out, the Cult of the Two-
Headed Coin expands its reach, the Fellsworn T emplars close 
the distance, and sanctuaries fall to the dangers and discord 
of New Valloren. As the party presses on, the threats they face 
rise to meet them.
This campaign has special mechanics to handle the escalation 
of disasters and cataclysms that befall New Valloren (see the 
“Doom Track” section on page 177).

THE INCITING INCIDENT
You can use the prompt below to start your campaign, or create your own.
Tip: The description of the Soul Echo and the Vessel of Light 
might need to be adjusted based on the table’s description of 
those elements.
Shortly after the sanctuary walls came down, Saraya Rinn 
(she/her) discovered the Soul Echo of the goddess Aurelia 
while praying at the shrine in the center of Marrogate. As 
a high priestess of the Aurelian Order, Saraya immediately 
understood the divine burden fate had placed upon her 
shoulders.
Over the next few weeks, she conducted Aurelian rites to craft 
the Vessel of Light and nurture the Echo inside it, preparing 
to deliver it across Valloren to the Cliffs of Horizon. With the 
help of her apprentice, Tolren (he/him), Saraya gathered 
supplies, planned a route, sent coded messages to her allies, 
and selected a traveling party from among the citizens of 
Marrogate to protect the Vessel on her journey. She prepared 
for every eventuality—except betrayal.
On the day Saraya planned to embark, a band of armed priests 
intercepted her, and T olren revealed his deception: he was a 
Fellsworn T emplar dedicated to the resurrection not of the 
goddess but of the Lovers, Yrska and Drago. No messages 
had been sent on Saraya’s behalf, no path had been cleared 
for her, and no allies awaited her arrival. Her false apprentice 
had merely informed his fellow conspirators of the Soul Echo’s 
discovery and opened the way for their arrival so they could 
take the Vessel by force.
But a high priestess of Aurelia is far from powerless—
especially at the shrine of her goddess in a world newly 
suffused with terrana. At Saraya’s command, a wild 
overgrowth of flora burst through the floor of the shrine, 
entangling her attackers and bringing the temple walls down 
around them. In the chaos, Saraya gave the Vessel of Light to 
her traveling party, secured their vow to deliver it to Horizon, 
and told them to seek out Abbot Kensington (they/them) in 
the nearby sanctuary of Cinteri.
With the Vessel in hand, the Fellsworn T emplars at their back, 
and the uncharted land of New Valloren beyond the barrier, 
the party must flee the city and chart their journey to the east. 
How will they elude the templars ransacking the sanctuary? 
Whom can they trust to help them escape? And what path 
will they choose when they reach the wilderness? Will they 
head straight to Cinteri in search of the abbot or choose a 
less direct—and perhaps more dangerous—route in hopes of 
evading their pursuers?

CAMPAIGN MECHANICS
The following mechanics are unique to this campaign.
The Hex Crawl
For travel across the continent of New Valloren, Journey 
to Horizon uses a map divided into hexagons (commonly 
known as a hex map). Your table can use the map to track 
the party’s movement across the continent, moving one hex 
at a time in a style of travel called a hex crawl. Each hex on 
the map represents roughly 24 miles of land from one side 
to another—just remember that in Daggerheart, narrative 
always supersedes representation with maps and minis, so 
the area represented by some hexes may be a bit larger or 
smaller in your story.
The following sections provide more specific directions on 
how to run this hex crawl, but generally the players will travel 
between sanctuaries one hex at a time, resolving encounters 
along the way. A party can take up to three short rests while 
traveling through the wilderness but is restricted from taking 
long rests outside a sanctuary. Therefore, the PCs must plan 
ahead to ensure they don’t exhaust their resources.
FLOW OF THE NARRATIVE
The journey procedure mechanizes certain aspects of overland 
travel. In some ways, the hex crawl rules are more regimented 
than other aspects of Daggerheart—but they’re designed to 
support the narrative, not abstract it away. We intend this 
system to help your table tell the kind of travel adventure 
stories the fantasy genre excels at, where the heroes cover 
long distances and pass extended periods of time in only a 
handful of scenes, and most mundane or routine events take 
place off-camera.
When using the hex crawl rules, consider the pace and 
flow of this kind of story. Often, there are travel montages 
broken up by dramatic scenes where we zoom in and watch 
the story unfold in detail. As you become comfortable with 
this style of play, you’ll develop your own intuition for how 
much to prepare in advance and how to cooperatively build 
the rest of the travel sequence, empowering your players to 
become fellow authors of the world their characters inhabit. 
For instance, as the PCs plan their route, you can guide the 
conversation by asking one or two of the following questions 
to your players, or create your own:
• What rumors have your characters heard from other 
travelers about the land ahead?
• What sound did your character hear in the night that 
concerns them about the upcoming journey?
• What surprising detail did your character notice that 
makes them excited to venture forth?
• Who does your character know that might be found 
nearby?
• What tales or legends of Old Valloren has your character 
heard about this region?
It’s important that the following procedures feel connected to 
the fiction and driven by character action, so don’t treat them 
like a mere bookkeeping exercise. As with everything else in 
Daggerheart, use them to present difficult dilemmas and fuel 
interesting scenes where the players’ decisions define their 
characters’ internal journeys just as much as the external—
and always amend or ignore what you feel you must in service 
of the story.
JOURNEYS IN VALLOREN
New Valloren is a vast continent distinguished by a patchwork 
of wild environs punctuated by scattered sanctuaries. The 
party’s mission is to locate the nine shrines containing 
Aurelia’s remaining Soul Echoes, transfer each of them to the 
Vessel of Light, and then deliver the Vessel to the Cliffs of 
Horizon where it can be used to resurrect the goddess.
A Journey to Horizon hex crawl uses two copies of the same 
campaign map: a GM’s key map and a player-facing map. The 
GM fills out the key map before the campaign with important 
information about each hex—such as habitat, terrain, and 
potential points of interest or encounters. Meanwhile, the 
player-facing version of the map only indicates the locations of 
sanctuaries and the geography of Valloren as it existed before 
the Long Sorrow. As the party journeys across the continent, 
the players track their movement on the player-facing map, 
and the GM describes the contents of each hex they enter, 
using their key map as a reference. The players should fill out 
the player-facing map as they go until it eventually matches 
the GM’s prepared key map. The campaign map is available 
as a two-page map in the appendix or as a larger map at 
www.daggerheart.com/downloads.
Preparing the GM’s Key Map
There are many ways to fill out the key. You can choose the 
contents of each hex, procedurally generate them, or use a 
combination of both. The following procedure is one method 
of doing so; feel free to use whatever method speaks to you.
Tip: You don’t have to fill out the entire key map before your 
first session—in fact, it’s often better to limit your map 
generation to the hexes the party is likely to reach in the next 
session or two. This has a couple of benefits. First, it greatly 
reduces the amount of time you’ll have to spend prepping 
your first session. Second, you can adjust the contents of the 
hexes or your generation method as the campaign continues 
to better serve the story or dial in the difficulty.
PLACING SOUL ECHOES
Before the first session, choose nine sanctuaries in which 
a Soul Echo can be located. The sanctuaries should be far 
enough from one another that the PCs will have to travel all 
over Valloren to reach each one, but not so far apart that 
their locations become predictable—for example, all along 
the coastlines or in every third sanctuary in a line. You can 
randomly assign them, but if you do, use the randomness as 
inspiration rather than edict. In particular, ensure the Echoes 
are distributed fairly evenly from west to east so that the 
PCs get to collect Echoes early in the campaign but end up 
relatively close to Horizon when they acquire the final one.

===== PAGE 171 =====

FILLING WILDERNESS HEXES
Start in any hex. You fill out the key map one region at a time 
by doing the following.
• Roll a d20 to determine the region’s habitat on the Habitat 
table.
• Roll a d12 to determine the region’s size (how many 
contiguous hexes the region covers). The shape of the 
region can be whatever you want, as long as you don’t 
encircle empty hexes.
• Roll and add the results of a d8 and d6 to determine one 
type of encounter the party might face while traveling 
through the region, as shown on the Encounter table.
• Roll a d4 to determine the terrain of the region on the 
T errain table. This determines how many days it takes the 
party to travel into the hex (see the “Travel Days” section 
on page 176).
• Roll a d100 to determine a rumor on the Rumors table that 
the party might hear about this region. You decide how 
true the rumor is and whether the PCs learn it.
Example Region Generation
GM Peter (he/him) is preparing for the next session and 
needs to generate a new region. He rolls the dice and gets 
the following results:
• d20: 11 (Forest)
• d12: 3 (3 hexes)
• d8+d6: 7+3 = 10 (Cult of the Two-Headed Coin)
• d4: 4 (Extreme)
• d100: 49 (An enormous clockwork machine lies half-
buried.)
Peter outlines 3 contiguous hexes, draws the forest symbol 
in one of the hexes, and adds four dots underneath the 
symbol to indicate the region’s habitat and terrain. He 
decides that, because the region is relatively small and 
difficult to move through, the party might try to go around 
it—and he doesn’t want to make it easy on them. So he 
lays out the region so it blocks the party’s most direct 
path to the nearest sanctuary. As for the rumor about the 
clockwork machine, Peter decides that the machine is a 
relic of the Age of Wonder that the Cult is trying to locate; 
this gives them a reason to be there. Peter plans a combat 
using the Battle Point system from the core rulebook and 
reflavors Jagged Knife adversaries as cultists.

Habitat (d20)
This table describes the dominant habitat of a region, but 
many regions will contain a variety of different environments 
or biomes. Keep in mind that, due to the resurgence of 
terrana in Valloren, nature is wild and chaotic, and two 
radically different biomes can exist adjacent to each other or 
even intermingle in interesting ways. The scale of the hexes 
on Valloren’s map means that you won’t have to track the 
locations of average-sized lakes and rivers. Assume for the 
sake of the narrative that most regions have waterways and 
aquatic sub-areas that one would expect to find there based 
on habitat and terrain.
Roll habitat
1
Shadowblighted: Roll again to determine 
which kind of habitat has been corrupted by 
fallen stars. If you roll another 1, the entire 
region has been completely overtaken and is 
nigh impossible to traverse.
2 Underground: Caves, mines, fungal forests
3–4 Aquatic: Lake, sea, reef, delta
5–6 Wetland: Swamp, marsh, bog, fen
7–8 Grassland: Plains, veldt, savannah
9–10 Tropical: Jungle, rainforest, mangrove
11–12 Forest: Deciduous, evergreen, coniferous
13–14 Drylands: Desert, canyon, prairie, salt flat
15–16 Rolling: Hills, chaparral, moor, heath
17–18 Mountain: Plateau, montane, alpine
19 Frozen: Tundra, taiga, glacier
20 Badlands: Volcano, crystalline, barren
Peter adds three forest hexes to the map.

Encounter (d8 + d6)
These encounters are intended to raise the tension as the 
party journeys through the wilderness and reinforce the notion 
that, outside the sanctuaries, Valloren is a living, breathing, 
dangerous place. Because the following table uses two dice 
added together, the entries closer to the center are more 
likely. As always, use these entries as a jumping-off point for 
encounters and sequences that fit the narrative flow of the 
campaign and the preferences of your players.
Roll Encounter
2 Roll on this table twice and combine the resulting 
entries.
3 The party crosses paths with fellow travelers, 
such as pilgrims, merchants, or explorers.
4
The party suffers a temporary setback, such as a 
dead end, bad omen, spoiled rations, or damaged 
tools. Optional: The GM gains a Fear for each PC.
5
The Seekers-in-Shadow make their presence 
known, and the party must escape their grasp or 
stand and fight.
6
An extreme weather event occurs, such as an 
earthquake, a forest fire, a thunderstorm, or a 
blizzard.
7
The party faces potential adversaries, such as 
bandits, brigands, or mercenaries—or discovers 
warning signs of their recent activity.
8 The heroes find themselves in the lair or hunting 
grounds of a territorial beast or pack of creatures.
9
The heroes must overcome an environmental 
hazard or obstacle, such as a yawning chasm, 
rushing river, or quicksand.
10
The party encounters members of the Cult of the 
Two-Headed Coin, their agents, or signs of their 
recent activity.
11
The party discovers a wondrous or dangerous site 
worth exploring, such as a ruin of Old Valloren or a 
place of magical power.
12
The party gains a bit of good fortune, such as a 
shortcut, good omen, helpful resource, or lucky 
break. Optional: Each PC gains a Hope.
13 The party discovers a settlement or an outpost.
14 The party stumbles across a cache of loot or 
treasure.
Terrain (d4)
A hex’s terrain determines how many days it takes the party to 
enter it. Generally, all hexes within a specific region have the 
same terrain rating, but you can alter this as you wish.
Roll Terrain
1
Optimal: Paved roads, stone highways, or raised 
paths allow swift and reliable travel. These routes 
are more heavily trafficked, increasing the chance 
of meeting merchants, patrols, or other fellow 
travelers. Moving into a hex with optimal terrain 
takes the party about 1 full day of traveling.
2
Fair: Beaten trade roads, caravan paths, or 
wilderness trails provide steady passage. Though 
slower than highways, they’re dependable, with 
only modest risk of delay or misdirection. Moving 
into a hex with fair terrain takes the party about 2 
full days of traveling.
3
Rough: Narrow tracks, game trails, or tangled 
undergrowth make for slow, uncertain travel. 
Travelers risk getting lost, straying into hazards, 
or encountering dangerous creatures. Moving into 
a hex with rough terrain takes the party about 3 
full days of traveling.
4
Extreme: Cliff faces, quagmires, collapsing 
terrain, or trackless wilds push travelers to their 
limits. Progress is grueling, dangerous, and often 
impossible without climbing gear, strong magic, or 
sheer luck. Moving into a hex with extreme terrain 
takes the party about 4 full days of traveling.

This table helps you generate an interesting hook for a region that you can use to flavor encounters or encourage PCs to explore 
New Valloren. Each hook is called a rumor because it could be any mixture of true or false—you decide. You also decide whether 
anyone in the party is actually aware of the rumor. If they are, consider how they might have come across it. They could have 
heard the rumor at the last sanctuary they rested in, bartered for information from a merchant they met on the road, or noticed 
something strange while traveling through the region.

You can also generate sanctuaries by rolling a d20, a d12, two 
d10s, a d8, a d6, and a d4, then comparing the results to the 
tables below. As before, take what’s useful from this process 
and leave the rest.
We recommend that you create each sanctuary prior to the 
session in which you expect the party to reach it—this way, 
you can prepare NPCs, factions, and plot hooks beforehand.
EXAMPLE SANCTUARY CREATION
Roll the following dice:
• d20 (Trade & Exports): 9 (“Crafting specialty food or drink”)
• d12 (Quirk): 2 (“Elaborate murals or public art forms”)
• d10 (Crisis): 1 (“Crime or banditry”)
• d10 (Drive): 1 (“Recognition or redemption”)
• d8 (Political System): 5 (“Merchant guild”)
• d6 (Size): 5 (“Large town”)
• d4 (Population): 1 (“Sparse”)
 
From these results, you describe the sanctuary as a once-
prosperous port city celebrated for its terrana-infused 
liquors and known for the gaudy and artistic murals 
distinguishing the various distributors’ warehouses and 
ships from one another. During the Long Sorrow, however, 
many turned to crime as resources dried up. The merchant 
guild that once controlled the town became a cabal of 
cronies. Once the walls came down, many citizens left. 
The people who remain are trying to rebuild their home’s 
reputation by discovering new distillations that rely on the 
resurgent terrana.

JOURnEY TO  H OR i ZO n
Tracking the Party’s Location
The party tracks their movement with the player-facing map. 
This procedure assumes the PCs are capable wayfinders and 
trailblazers, so they know the contents of the hex they are in 
(their current hex) and any hex adjacent to it. If an adjacent 
hex contains a non-hidden point of interest, such as a tower, 
the PCs are usually aware of it as well. Altogether, this gives 
the players enough information to decide which way to 
proceed. As always, the GM decides how much information 
they convey to the players.
Travel Days
This system assumes that, on a full day traveling, the PCs…
1. Break camp, eat breakfast, and pack up shortly before 
sunrise.
2. Travel overland for about 6–8 hours.
3. T ake a short break at midday to eat and get their 
bearings.
4. Travel overland for another 6–8 hours until about 
sundown.
5. Make camp, prepare and eat dinner, set a watch, and 
bed down for the night.
As needed, various party members scout ahead, forage or 
hunt, refill waterskins, repair gear, and keep watch. If you like, 
you can narrate or even roleplay these activities if your table 
finds it interesting, but you don’t need to spend valuable table 
time describing or tracking these events.
Resting
This campaign frame doesn’t require you to meticulously track 
rations or other supplies. Instead, travel moves at the pace 
of the story, and the party’s resources are tracked via rests. 
Outside a sanctuary, the party can take only short rests, each 
of which represents an entire day spent in one location. Inside 
a sanctuary, the party can take a long rest, which represents 
multiple days recovering from hard travel and rough sleeping.
Keep in mind that the term “rest” has a specific mechanical 
meaning in Daggerheart. Within the fiction, the party still 
rests (in the colloquial sense) most nights they spend in the 
wilderness, but they don’t gain the mechanical benefits of 
a short rest every time they go to sleep. There are very few 
creature comforts in the wilderness, and this means that 
travel wears the PCs down until they take a day off. This 
extended period allows for downtime moves.
The PCs can gain the benefits of a long rest only in a 
sanctuary because that is the only place they can feel safe, 
fully recuperate, and replenish their stock of any resources 
not plentiful in the wilderness. This essentially expands the 
standard Daggerheart adventuring rhythm—where a short 
rest represents an hour or so and a long rest represents a full 
night’s sleep—to this campaign frame’s scale of days, weeks, 
and months.
When the party takes three short rests in a row, their next rest 
must be a long rest (see page 105 of the core rulebook).
Narratively, this limitation on short rests represents the 
party’s inability to fully replenish their resources in the 
wilderness; the heroes can take a day off of traveling to repair 
their gear and rest their weary bodies, but there’s only so 
many times one can stitch armor and field dress wounds 
before they need a few days inside civilization.
SPECIAL DOWNTIME MOVE: LOCATE SOUL ECHOES
In a Journey to Horizon campaign, the players gain access 
to an additional downtime move: Locate Soul Echoes. When 
a PC uses this downtime move, they commune with the 
Vessel and have the Echoes within “call out” to their sisters. 
The PC learns the direction (north, south, east, west, or a 
combination) of the nearest sanctuary with a Soul Echo at 
its shrine. At the GM’s discretion, using this downtime move 
alerts nearby Seekers-in-Shadow of the Vessel’s general 
direction as well.
Encounter Rolls
When the party enters a hex, roll a number of d6s equal to 
the hex’s terrain rating. If any of the dice show a result of 1, 
activate an encounter you designed during region creation. 
Otherwise, you can spend a Fear to activate an encounter.
Tip: You can change the size of the dice you roll to reflect 
more dangerous (d4) or safer (d8) areas.
The party can trigger more than one encounter in a single 
region. If this happens, either roll on the Encounter table 
to generate a new encounter in the moment or shift an 
encounter from a region that the party seems unlikely to pass 
through, then modify or reflavor it to suit the current situation.
Example: The party enters an 8-hex alpine region with 
fair terrain. The GM, Peter, rolls 2d6 and gets a 4 and 
a 1, triggering an encounter. Peter runs the Mountain 
Chasm Traversal environment (9 on the Encounter table) 
he prepared before the session. After the encounter, the 
party moves into another hex with fair terrain, and Peter 
rolls 2d6; he rolls a 3 and a 5, so no encounter is triggered. 
When the party enters the next hex, Peter rolls two 1s, 
triggering an encounter. Peter recognizes that the party 
has already moved past the badlands region with a saloon 
encounter (13 on the Encounter table), so he reflavors the 
saloon to a ski chalet to match it with the alpine habitat of 
the party’s current region.
OPTIONAL RULE: ENDURANCE COUNTDOWNS
You can heighten the tension of traveling through the 
wilderness with this optional rule. At the end of a rest, each 
PC gains an Endurance Countdown (6). When the party enters 
a wilderness hex, each player rolls their Hope Die. If the result 
is equal to or lower than the value of their PC’s Endurance 
Countdown, they mark a Stress. Otherwise, they tick down 
their Endurance Countdown. When a PC’s Endurance 
Countdown triggers, they become Vulnerable until their next 
rest. At the start of a rest, all PCs’ Endurance Countdowns 
end.

WHEN DISASTER LOOMS
It’s possible that a party can be stuck in the wilderness with 
no short rests and all HP and Stress marked. As with a combat 
encounter that results in all PCs being defeated, it’s up to the 
GM to decide how to proceed. Here are a few possibilities:
• The party’s quest ends in failure. Discuss as a table what 
events unfold in the world of Valloren as a result.
• The party is lost forever, but the Vessel finds its way to a 
new band of heroes. The players create new characters 
who continue from where the previous party left off. If you 
decide that a significant amount of time has elapsed since 
the original party fell, discuss as a table what might have 
befallen the continent in the interim.
• The party is rescued by a friendly NPC or taken hostage by 
the enemy, and they most likely no longer have the Vessel. 
Decide what happened to it, then let the PCs figure out 
how to reacquire it.
Traveling over Water
Valloren is crisscrossed by a network of rivers and other 
waterways that can be traveled by boat. It’s also surrounded 
by an ocean that can be traversed by ship. For simplicity’s 
sake, traveling by water uses the same basic mechanics as 
overland travel, with the following changes and clarifications:
• Rivers: The party can travel across overland hexes if 
they acquire passage on a boat and the GM decides that 
there are navigable waterways in the hexes they wish to 
travel through. Moving downstream reduces the terrain 
rating of the hex the party enters by 1—so taking a boat 
downstream into a hex with rough terrain takes only 2 
days instead of 3. Entering a hex by boat upstream takes 
an extra day.
• Ocean: The party can travel through ocean hexes if they 
acquire passage on a ship. When the party enters a 
new ocean hex, roll a d4 to determine the weather they 
encounter:
• On a 1, the party gets a tailwind, and entering the hex 
takes a day.
• On a 2, they enjoy fair weather, and entering the hex 
takes 2 days.
• On a 3, the party encounters rough waters, and 
entering the hex takes 3 days.
• On a 4, the party encounters extreme weather, and 
entering the hex takes 4 days.
Tip: When the party travels by boat or ship, remember to 
describe how working the rigging, fording shallows, and 
performing other necessary jobs exhausts them.
CHARACTER A dV A n CEME n T
The party levels up each time they add a new Soul Echo to 
the Vessel of Light. Because there are nine Soul Echoes (in 
addition to the one they begin the campaign with) for the party 
to collect before delivering the Vessel to Horizon, the party will 
advance from level 1 to 10 over the course of the campaign.
dOOM TRAC k
The Doom Track (available in the appendix or at 
www.daggerheart.com/downloads) represents the growing 
disasters that befall New Valloren if the party doesn’t 
resurrect Aurelia. At the end of each session, mark a box in 
the party’s tier or below. You can’t mark a nested box until you 
mark the box it’s nested under.
When you mark a box on the Doom Track, try to incorporate 
the connected event into the next session, but don’t force 
it if it doesn’t make sense. For instance, if you mark “A new 
settlement is overtaken by surging wilderness,” and the party 
doesn’t leave their current location during the next session, 
it probably makes more sense to wait until they embark on a 
journey into the wilderness. Each event should occur at least 
once after it’s been marked, but that doesn’t preclude it from 
happening multiple times as long as it doesn’t get boring or 
predictable.
Where possible, you should choose an option that makes 
sense—for example, don’t choose “Travelers trade stories of 
nearby Seeker sightings” if the party is deep inside a network 
of abandoned mines and you don’t expect the PCs to interact 
with other travelers for a bit.
As your table’s story develops, feel free to adjust how many 
boxes you mark at the end of a session. If the heroes discover 
a way to drive back the shadowblight or reignite faith in 
Aurelia along their journey, you can refrain from marking a 
box for one session to reflect the extra time the heroes have 
bought themselves. On the other hand, if the heroes travel 
too slowly or let evil expand without resistance (such as by 
allowing a sanctuary to fall to the Cult of the Two-Headed 
Coin), you can mark one or more additional boxes to reflect 
the growing despair taking hold in New Valloren.
Tip: The Doom Track is built for a campaign that lasts 
30–40 sessions. If you want your campaign to be shorter or 
longer, you’ll want to adjust how often it’s marked.

CAMPAign AdVER sAR i Es
This campaign includes two new features, “Shadowblighted” 
and “T erranamancer,” which you can add to any adversary. 
The “Shadowblighted” feature empowers adversaries inside 
shadowblighted regions, while the “T erranamancer” feature 
showcases an adversary’s command of terrana.
Shadowblighted - Passive: This adversary is corrupted by 
shadowblight; describe how this manifests in their form or 
demeanor. When encountered within a shadowblighted region 
or near a powerful source of shadowblight, this adversary 
critically succeeds on attacks on a die roll of 18–20.
Terranamancer - Passive: This adversary can draw on terrana 
and use it to fuel magical effects. Mark a Stress to activate 
an effect based on the habitat of their current location (if an 
effect’s damage roll uses the adversary’s tier, roll the damage 
die a number of times equal to their tier and total the rolls):
• Heat Spell (Desert, Salt Flats, Volcano): The adversary 
exudes oppressive supernatural heat. Each target within 
Very Close range must succeed on an Instinct Reaction 
Roll. T argets who fail must mark a Stress and take d6 
direct magic damage using this adversary’s tier.
• Overgrowth Spell (Forest, Jungle): This adversary 
unleashes a burst of branches, brambles, or vines in all 
directions. Each target within Close range must make an 
Agility Reaction Roll. T argets who fail take d8 physical 
damage using this adversary’s tier and are Restrained until 
they break free with a successful Strength Roll.
• Wind Spell (Cliffs, Plains, Steppe): A tornado surrounds 
this adversary. Each target within Close range must make 
a Strength Reaction Roll. T argets who fail take d8 magic 
damage using this adversary’s tier and are pushed up to 
Far range. Until this adversary takes damage, attack rolls 
against them have disadvantage.
• Ice Spell (Alpine, Glacier, Tundra): This adversary 
unleashes daggers of ice. Make an attack against each 
PC in front of this adversary within Close range. T argets 
they succeed against take d10 magic damage using this 
adversary’s tier and are Frostbitten until they spend a 
Hope to clear the condition. While Frostbitten, a PC gains a 
−1 penalty to their Proficiency.
• Poison Spell (Moor, Swamp, Wetlands): A cloud of toxic 
spores explodes from this adversary. Each PC within Close 
range must succeed on a Strength Reaction Roll or take 
d6 direct magic damage using this adversary’s tier and 
become Nauseated until they clear a HP. While Nauseated, 
a PC can’t gain Hope.
• Stone Spell (Canyon, Mountain, Badlands): This 
adversary gains a bonus to their damage thresholds equal 
to their tier until they take Severe damage or use this 
feature again.
• Water Spell (Riverlands, Sea): This adversary unleashes 
a geyser or tidal wave. Make an attack against all targets 
within Very Close range. T argets they succeed against are 
pushed to Close range of where they were and mark a 
number of Stress equal to this adversary’s tier.
FULL GAMEPLA Y EXAMPLE
Viggo, Mira, and Liv are playing in a campaign with Peter 
as their GM. They’ve just set out from the sanctuary of 
Foothold and intend to travel 10 hexes northeast to the 
sanctuary of Diremark, where they believe they might find a 
Soul Echo. Peter says that the hex directly to the northeast 
is rough plains (grassland with a terrain rating of 3) and 
the hex to the southeast is a sprawling field of gray stone 
pocked with rifts (badlands with a terrain rating of 4).
Tip: The following illustrations represent the party’s current 
hex with a four-pointed star and obscure hexes they wouldn’t 
see with a dark-gray “fog of war.”
The party decides to travel through the plains and spends 
3 days traveling into the first adjacent hex (as the terrain is 
“rough,” it takes 3 days to enter the hex). Peter describes 
the wind-rippled plains the party finds itself in, along with 
the cloud armies waging war in the sky (during prep, Peter 
rolled a 33 on the rumors table for this region). He then 
rolls 3d6 Encounter Dice—but none of them rolls a 1, so no 
encounter is triggered. Peter reveals the hexes adjacent to 
the party’s new current hex.
The hexes to the north and northeast are part of the same 
rough plains region that the party is currently in. The new 
hex to the southeast is part of the same badlands region 
they saw before. The party decides to continue in the same 
direction and spends 3 more days traveling into the next hex.
178 Chapter 4: Journey to Horizon


===== PAGE 180 =====
Journey to Horizon
FULL GAMEPLA Y EXAMPLE (CONTINUED)
Peter rolls 3d6 Encounter Dice again (because the terrain 
rating of the hex the party is entering is also 3), and he 
rolls a 1, 1, and 6. Because at least one die rolled a 1, the 
party triggers an encounter when they enter the hex. Peter 
checks his prep from before the session and sees that the 
region contains a kindly hermit named Cristofero who lives 
in a simple stone hut and weaves special magical talismans 
from the tall, golden grasses that grow here (during prep, 
Peter rolled a 13 on the Encounter table for this region, 
which meant the encounter he created should feature a 
“settlement or outpost”).
Peter tells the players that, as they enter this hex, they 
notice a small plume of blue smoke on the horizon. This 
is the smoke from Cristofero’s chimney, but the players 
wouldn’t know that yet. Liv is playing a Winged Sentinel 
seraph, so she asks Peter if she can fly up and see the 
source of the smoke. Peter says yes and asks Liv to make 
an Instinct Roll, since it seems like Liv’s trying to perceive 
something. Liv rolls a 19 for a success with Fear; Peter 
takes a Fear token and describes what Liv sees: an elderly 
galapa doddering around a stone hut and bundling grasses. 
Because Liv rolled with Fear, however, Peter decides that 
by flying up high, Liv has attracted the attention of a flock 
of black birdlike creatures! This is actually the encounter 
that Peter had generated for the badlands region to the 
east—but it seems clear that the party is avoiding that area 
anyway, so Peter feels comfortable reusing that prep here.
Peter asks the players what they want to do: Stand and 
fight the approaching creatures? Hide from them? Head 
toward the elderly galapa’s hut and risk drawing the birds 
to him?
The party decides to stand and fight, then plays through a 
combat against the bird creatures (Flock of Feather Fiends, 
which Peter previously statted up). The party dispatches the 
adversaries, but Liv marks multiple HP in the process and 
both Viggo and Mira’s PCs mark Stress multiple times. The 
party could take a short rest to clear some of their HP and 
Stress, but they’ve already taken two short rests since their 
most recent long rest.
That means they won’t be able to take any more short 
rests until they take a long rest—and they can do that only 
in a sanctuary. Liv checks the party’s map to see how far 
they are from the nearest sanctuary: 7 hexes. Viggo asks 
Peter to describe the hexes adjacent to the party’s current 
location. Peter tells them that the hex in a direct line toward 
the nearest sanctuary is part of the same grassland region 
they’re currently in—so it has rough terrain (3)—but the 
hex to the north is a forest with optimal terrain (forest with 
a terrain rating 1).
What will the party do? Use their third short rest in a row 
and hope they can hold out until the next sanctuary? Try to 
take the direct route through the rough terrain hex, or move 
into the hex to the north and hope it reveals an easier route? 
Or perhaps investigate the doddering old galapa Liv spied 
before the party was attacked?
SESSION ZERO QUESTIONS
Ask your players any of these questions, or make your own.
• Your characters are all from Marrogate. How was that 
sanctuary shaped by the Long Sorrow? What roles did the 
PCs play there?
• What rumor has your character heard about another 
sanctuary? Who told them this story?
• What remnant of Old Valloren does your character hope 
survived the Long Sorrow? What do they fear they might 
find beyond the walls?
• What advice did Saraya give your party about nurturing 
and protecting the Vessel? What did she tell your 
characters about the ritual they’d have to perform when 
they reach Horizon?
• What does your character carry on their quest to remind 
them of the loved ones they left behind?

APPENDIX

Domain Card reference
DREAD DOMAIN 
BLIGHTING STRIKE
Level 1 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 1
Make a Spellcast Roll against a target 
within Far range. On a success:
• On a roll with Hope, deal d6+1 
magic damage using your 
Proficiency. On a roll with Fear, deal 
d10+1 magic damage using your 
Proficiency.
• The target’s next successful attack 
deals half damage.
On a failure, you must spend a Hope or 
mark a Stress.
UMBRAL VEIL
Level 1 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 1
Once per rest, you can mark a Stress 
to encase yourself in shadowy energy. 
When you do, place a number of tokens 
on this card equal to the number of 
Fear in the GM’s pool. After an attack 
roll is made against you, you can spend 
any number of tokens to give the result 
a −1 penalty per token spent. 
At the end of the scene, clear all 
unspent tokens.
VOICE OF DREAD
Level 1 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 0
You can magically speak to a creature 
you can see, tormenting them with your 
words. Make a Spellcast Roll against 
them. On a success, they must mark 
a Stress and are frozen with terror, 
making them temporarily Restrained.
HIDEOUS RETRIBUTION
Level 2 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 2
When an ally within Close range takes 
damage from a target you can see, you 
can make a reaction roll against the 
target using your Spellcast trait. On 
a success, mark a Stress to deal d6 
magic damage using your Proficiency.
SIPHON ESSENCE
Level 2 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 1
Make a Spellcast Roll against a target 
within Very Close range. Once per 
long rest on a success, the target 
takes d12+4 magic damage using your 
Proficiency. On a success with Fear, 
you gain a +1 bonus to your Proficiency 
for this attack.
You clear a number of Hit Points equal 
to the number of Hit Points the target 
marked from this attack.
SHARED TRAUMA
Level 3 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 1
You can transfer suffering from one 
creature to another. Once per rest, 
mark any number of Hit Points on a 
willing creature within Melee range to 
clear an equal number of Hit Points on 
another willing creature within Melee 
range.
TERRIFY
Level 3 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 1
Make a Spellcast Roll against a target 
within Close range. On a success, 
the target marks 1d4 Stress, and you 
can make the target flee one range 
away from you (such as Very Close to 
Close or Close to Far). On a success 
with Fear, the target also becomes 
temporarily Vulnerable.
CHAINS OF AFFLICTION
Level 4 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 2
Mark 2 Stress to temporarily Chain 
a target within Close range. When a 
Chained creature deals damage, the 
target of their attack marks one fewer 
Hit Point than they normally would. 
You can’t have more than one creature 
Chained at a time.
SUMMON 
HORROR
Level 4 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 2
Once per scene, mark a Stress to 
summon an otherworldly creature 
that deals d8+1 magic damage using 
your Spellcast trait to a target within 
Far range. If the target marks any Hit 
Points from this attack, they must 
succeed on a Reaction Roll (12) to steel 
themselves against the horror or mark 
an equal number of Stress. 
After making the attack, the creature 
dissipates.
DIRE STRIKE
Level 5 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 2
When a target marks any number of Hit 
Points from an attack you make, you 
can spend a Hope to drain power from 
them. The GM loses a Fear.
SPECTRAL MIST
Level 5 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 0
Spend 2 Hope to conjure an eerie 
mist that turns you and allies of 
your choice within Close range 
momentarily incorporeal. While a 
creature is incorporeal, they can move 
through solid objects and are immune 
to physical damage. They become 
corporeal again after they pass through 
a solid object or make an action roll. 
Otherwise, this effect lasts until the 
end of the scene.
DARKFIRE
Level 6 Dread Spell
Recall Cost: 2
Once per scene, spend any number 
of Hope to target an equal number of 
adversaries within Close range. Each 
target makes a Reaction Roll (15). 
T argets who fail take d8+6 magic 
damage using your Spellcast trait as 
they are engulfed in dark fire. T argets 
who succeed take half damage.
